# R-INST-4 — HOSPITALITY, ENTERTAINMENT, POVERTY AND UTILITY INSTITUTIONS (DW program P1b, tranche 4)

# COMPLETE — all thirteen families at depth, assembled 2026-08-23. This dossier was written by THREE lanes in one day: the PARENT lane 05:59–06:25Z (Fable — orientation, the §0 roster, the §1 spine, and a fan-out of six research sub-lanes that the chair killed at ~06:15Z under ODQ §465 when the program's lane cap was cut to two); RESUME 1, 14:06:57–14:46Z (Fable, solo — recovered all six killed sub-lanes from their transcripts and wrote §2–§14, §15 and §Σ, stopped by the chair mid-§Σ); and RESUME 2, from 14:47:33Z (Opus, solo — completed the law map, and wrote the open-questions ledger, the method disclosure and the sub-lane appendix). Model mark: **[OPUS-RUN · FABLE-VALIDATION OWED]** — §0–§14 compiled on Fable (two lanes); §15/§Σ drafted on Fable, completed on Opus; ledger/method/appendix on Opus. **Owner taste-gates this dossier (CT-0 pattern); nothing here is grammar yet.** The status map at §0.3 is the truth of what is at depth, and §L is the numbered ledger of what is not.

**Lane.** TC-R-INST-4, started 2026-08-23T05:59Z (UTC). Read-only on the repo; scratchpad-only deliverable; the receipt `laneTCRINST4-receipt.md` holds the round-by-round log and the resume points. Owner taste-gates this dossier (CT-0 pattern). Charter: `docs/DESIGN_DWELLINGS_PROGRAM.md` §2 (nine laws), §3 (13-system weave), §4 S6 (circulation classes, ODQ §452; storage/service classes, ODQ §453), §5 data contracts, §8 (every entry reports CIRCULATION and STORAGE typology), §15 P1a–P1d; ODQ §435–§438, §452, §453, §456, §462. Sibling dossiers: `draft-R-INST-1-CIVIC-DEFENSE.md` (style template; its §0 is one exclusion cross-check), `draft-R-INST-2-TRADE-CRAFTS.md` (its §0.1b hands eleven entries to this tranche; its §Σ structural findings — `NO_BUILDING`, HOSTED, the culture weight — are TESTED here, not assumed), `draft-DWR1A-CONSTRUCTION-HISTORY.md` (construction substrate, cited by section where an institution leans on it — §7 carries the inn's imperatives). R-INST-3 (faith + learning) runs concurrently; the boundary with it is stated per entry below.

**Labeling discipline.** CONFIRMED = a source fetched or returned in a search digest THIS lane (URL inline; fetch date 2026-08-23 unless stated). CONFIRMED-digest = the claim came back in a search-engine digest quoting the page; the page itself was not opened. CONFIRMED (sub-lane) = fetched by a research sub-agent of this lane on 2026-08-23 and appended verbatim with provenance; the parent re-verified only the figures marked PARENT-VERIFIED. PLAUSIBLE = synthesis or training knowledge — the grammar may NOT consume a PLAUSIBLE figure until P1c verifies it. Contested points carry both sides. Morphology is `EUROPEAN_FANTASY_BASE`: every cited building is a bounded possibility or a counterexample, never a prevalence prior; this dossier mints no probabilities. Conventions-never-expression. Deity doctrine: the hospital's chapel and the almshouse's chapel appear ONLY as liturgical-functional cells (an altar end, a screen, sight-lines from beds), never as theology. Finite semantics: every proposal is a closed enum the clerk composes from. Dates are absolute. The brothel is treated CLINICALLY as a building type (the regulated stew), per the brief.

**Catalog source of record.** `src/data/institutionalCatalog.js` in the clean read-only tree `chair-baseproof-b10ed1a1` (2,526 lines; 311 entries; tier blocks thorp L6–154 · hamlet L155–434 · village L435–890 · town L891–1538 · city L1539–2248 · metropolis L2249–2526). The flat table `RINST2-catalog-flat.tsv` (R-INST-2's `RINST2-flatten.cjs` over the same file) was re-read whole (311 rows) and the candidate rows were read again in the source file at their line numbers. Selection is by SHELF (Entertainment; Infrastructure-water/sanitation; the Religious shelf's healing/charity rows; the Economy shelf's lodging/food-drink rows) + the R-INST-1/R-INST-2 hand-offs + a name/desc keyword sweep over all eleven shelves (inn, tavern, ale, lodging, hostel, bath, brothel, theat, opera, arena, colosseum, gladiat, fight, pit, gambl, gaming, music, bard, perform, festival, hospital, infirm, alms, leper, lazar, orphan, foundling, poor, workhouse, soup, well, spring, cistern, aqueduct, conduit, fountain, sewer, sanitation, oven, laundry, cemetery, grave, lighthouse, bridge, post, relay, stable, livery, hireling, midwife, heal, water). `priorityCategory` is the faction-role axis and is ignored for selection (R-INST-1's rule): "Public bathhouse" whose faction role is `government` is a bathhouse.

**Engine recon (read-only, this lane; CONFIRMED by reading the file).** `src/domain/interior/interiorTemplates.js` (DOOR 3, "THE KEYED SCALE") carries a `vice` template = `common` (front; table/bar/hearth/bench) + `kitchen` (back, tier ≥ 1) + `cellar` (back) + `lodging` (back, tier ≥ 3; bed/table), and a `heals` FUNCTION VARIANT that appends `quarters` (bed/shelf/cauldron) to any kind. `ROOM_KINDS` has no yard, gallery, stable, bath, stage, ward, or court; `FURNISHING_KINDS` has no tub, furnace, stage, tiring-room fixture, bath, or trough. The nature facet is inferred at `src/domain/spatial/cohesionWeave.js` `FACET_INFERENCE` by regex over `name + type + category` — `vice` = `/tavern|brothel|den|gambling|smuggl/i`. A simulation of that table over this tranche's 38 names (executed this lane over NAMES ONLY — the live institution record's `type`/`category` fields were not exercised, so PLAUSIBLE-by-simulation): every INN entry (Wayside inn, Travelers' inn, Inn (multiple) — a town REQ row — Coaching inn) and both alehouses fall to `generic` (the regex has no `\binn\b|ale`), "Gladiatorial school" resolves to `learning` (the `school` token), "Hireling hall" to `civic` (`\bhall\b`), the hospitals/almshouse to `generic`+`heals`, and the bathhouse, theatres, arena, opera house, foundling home, workhouse and every water entry to `generic`. This is flagged in §Σ as an engine-gap (the inference table, not the catalog). R-INST-2's recon of `INDUSTRY_WATER_NEEDS` (six names, none of them a bathhouse or a hospital) stands.

**Search-budget law (inherited from R-INST-2's receipt):** the shared WebSearch quota is finite; each sub-lane is capped at ~12 searches / ~15 fetches, curl for PDFs; the parent spends its own searches on verification of the three grammar-load-bearing figures and on negation searches.

## §1 · THE HOSPITALITY SPINE — the parent lane's own verified findings (round 1, 2026-08-23 06:05–06:20Z; written at the §465 checkpoint)

These are the figures the PARENT fetched itself; they are the tranche's grammar-load-bearing anchors and are PARENT-VERIFIED. The family sections §2–§14 were written afterwards, by RESUME 1, from the six killed sub-lanes' recovered transcripts (§M.2); where a family section supersedes or completes a figure below, it says so in its own provenance line, and §1 is not restated there.

**1.1 The drinking house is a DWELLING, not a building type (family A — the `NO_BUILDING`/HOSTED test passes at the floor).** CONFIRMED: in the medieval period alehouses were "ordinary dwellings where the householder served home-brewed ale and beer"; lodging, where offered, "might be no more than bedding on the floor in the kitchen, or in a barn"; the sign was "a pole above the door, garlanded with foliage"; taverns sold wine, were restricted to towns and "catered to richer patrons"; inns were "generally purpose-built to accommodate travellers" with "more bedrooms than the average house and substantial stabling", some with "galleries around a courtyard" that "provided an arena for plays or cockfights"; pictorial inn signs from the 14th century ([buildinghistory.org: Researching the History of Pubs, Inns and Hotels](https://www.buildinghistory.org/buildings/inns.shtml), fetched 2026-08-23; the page cites Clark 1983 and Pantin 1961 without detail). CONFIRMED-digest (search 2026-08-23): alewives "brewed and sold commercially out of their homes, hanging a bundle of greenery called an ale-stake over the door" (Museum of Oxford page in digest; Wikipedia "Alewife (trade)" in digest). The catalog's hamlet wording ("sold from someone's back room") and village wording ("Home-based. Women brew and sell ale") are therefore historically RIGHT at the floor: the alehouse is a DWELLING with a drinking FUNCTION on its hall/kitchen and a brewing FUNCTION on an outshut — DW law 1 across buildings (R-INST-1's spine finding, reproduced here for hospitality). What a town TAVERN adds (the cellar as primary cell, separate drinking rooms, the sign, the wine licence) is owed by sub-lane AB. Negation result: I searched for the alehouse as a separate building type and found only the dwelling reading; no source this round asserted a purpose-built medieval alehouse (null result reported as evidence).

**1.2 The inn is a COURTYARD WITH A WIDE STREET PASSAGE (families B/C — the parti is sourced; the figures are not yet).** CONFIRMED: in medieval Gloucester the main-street "large plots, most of them occupied by inns, [had] a courtyard and a wide passage from the street"; the New Inn (mid-15th c.) "has a galleried courtyard" whose "jetties continued round two sides and had elaborately carved corner posts"; the early-16th-century Red Lion (no. 84 Northgate St, demolished) had "a cross range parallel to the street and a ground-floor passage leading to a narrow court"; the Ram (early 16th c.) "had an open court approached through a central passage way"; the Fleece had "stone-vaulted cellars … possibly of the 12th century" ([VCH Gloucester vol. 4, Medieval Gloucester: Topography, British History Online](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/vch/glos/vol4/pp63-72), fetched 2026-08-23). CONFIRMED (aggregator, fetched): the New Inn was built 1450 by John Twyning, a monk, as a hostelry for St Peter's Abbey, on the site of an earlier inn; three storeys, oak frame with lath-and-plaster panels; west front of seven bays; "entered via carriage passage from Northgate Street"; "open jettied galleries look over the courtyard" and form the entry to the upper chambers; a proclamation was read from the first-floor gallery in 1553; Historic England list entry 1245714, Grade I ([Wikipedia: The New Inn, Gloucester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Inn,_Gloucester), fetched 2026-08-23). CONFIRMED-digest: the inn "enclos[es] two courtyards with stairs leading to two tiers of galleries" (digest of CAMRA/Tudor Travel Guide pages, 2026-08-23). **NOT FOUND this round:** any stated width or height of the New Inn's carriage passage or of its galleries (three searches; the HE list entry is behind the Cloudflare gate — `RINST4-P-HE-1026704.html` is a 5.8 KB "Just a moment" page, the same gate R-INST-2 hit). The carriage-passage WIDTH BUCKET therefore stays OPEN for sub-lane CD / P1c (ledger item 1).

**1.3 The George, Southwark — the gallery's measured PROGRAM (the figures still owed).** CONFIRMED: the surviving south range is "of three storeys and attic with wood dormers in a tiled roof", "the western half has two ranges of galleries at the first and second floors, the lower one being supported on cantilever beams, and the upper one and roof with weather-boarded parapet, on wooden Doric columns which divide each gallery into six bays"; "both galleries have a wood balustrade of turned balusters with moulded handrail"; the wall behind the galleries "has windows overlooking the galleries, and openings at the eastern end giving access to the staircase", which is "centrally placed within the building"; after the 1676 fire "Weyland rebuilt the inn probably on the old plan" and an 1874 plan "shows the original extent of the inn" ([Survey of London vol. 22, Borough High Street, BHO](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/survey-london/vol22/pp9-30), fetched 2026-08-23 — the volume's own text carries NO measurements). The same page lists the Borough High Street inn strip by name and date (Tabard/Talbot 1306 → rebuilt c. 1629 → demolished 1875; George on the 1542 plan; White Hart 1450 → demolished 1889; King's Head ex Pope's Head; Queen's Head ex Cross Keys; Spur; Nag's Head ex Horse's Head 1542/1634; Christopher) — the city "Inns and taverns (district)" REQ row's measured district is this street, and its PLOT logic (inn yards packed side by side behind narrow frontages, each with a passage) is the district's parti (sub-lane AB owes Carlin/Hare for the counts). CONFIRMED-digest: present building 1677, "the only remaining galleried inn left in London", originally round three sides of the courtyard (National Trust HBSMR record and Wikipedia in digest, 2026-08-23). The gallery WIDTH BUCKET is not yet sourced (ledger item 2).

**1.4 The public playhouse — the Fortune contract of 8 January 1600 (family H — PARENT-VERIFIED; the single best-measured building in this tranche).** CONFIRMED from the transcription at [Shakespearean London Theatres (De Montfort University), Fortune 1600–42, in depth](https://shalt.dmu.ac.uk/locations/fortune-1600-42/indepth.html), fetched 2026-08-23: "the frame of the said house to be set square and to contain 80 feet of lawful assize every way square without and 55 feet of like assize square every way within" (24.4 m outside, 16.8 m inside); foundation "of piles, brick, lime and sand … 1 foot of assize at the least above the ground"; "the first or lower storey to contain 12 feet of lawful assize in height, the second storey 11 feet … and the third or upper storey … 9 feet" (3.66 / 3.35 / 2.74 m); "All which storeys shall contain 12½ feet of lawful assize in breadth throughout, besides a jutty forwards in either of the said two upper storeys of 10 inches" (gallery depth 3.81 m, jetty 0.25 m); "stage shall contain in length 43 feet of lawful assize and in breadth to extend to the middle of the yard" (stage 13.1 m wide × c. 8.4 m deep, since the yard is 55 ft across); "four convenient divisions for gentlemen's rooms and other sufficient and convenient divisions for twopenny rooms"; built "according to the manner and fashion of the said house called the Globe". Cross-checked against [Wikipedia: Fortune Playhouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortune_Playhouse) (fetched 2026-08-23): plot c. 127 × 129 ft; the same 80/55 ft and 12/11/9 ft figures; "each row of galleries was twelve feet deep" (Wikipedia rounds the contract's 12½); stage "forty-three feet across", "covered with tile"; square plan "uniquely among the period's amphitheatres"; lath-and-plaster walls, wood gallery floors; opened 1600, burned 9 December 1621, reopened March 1623 (brick), closed 1642, demolished 1649. INDEPENDENCE NOTE: both pages derive from one primary (Dulwich College, Henslowe-Alleyn Muniment 22); the henslowe-alleyn.org.uk essay returned HTTP 500 this round — so this is ONE primary read through a scholarly transcription, not two sources. GRAMMAR CONSEQUENCE (proposal, PLAUSIBLE): the playhouse parti is `POLYGON_YARD_THREE_GALLERIES` with the gallery as a `GALLERY`-class cell ring of depth bucket 3.5–4.0 m and storey heights 3.7/3.4/2.7 m (descending — law 9's status-graded storeys INVERTED: the cheapest seats highest), the stage as a `dais`-class fixture occupying half the yard, the tiring house as the theatre's `STORE`+`chamber` block, and the "gentlemen's rooms" as `CLOSET`-class cells at the stage ends; the external stair-turret is a `STAIR_HALL` with exterior consequence (a named massing part). HOME: `ROOM_KINDS.dais` exists as a room kind (here it is a fixture); NO TYPED HOME for yard, gallery ring, tiring house.

**1.5 The infirmary hall with the chapel at its end (family J — the parti sourced; the span still owed).** CONFIRMED: St Mary's Hospital, Chichester — site donated by the Greyfriars in the 13th century; building completed 1292; "six massive oak constructed bays" with a kingpost roof "over 40' high"; "converted during the 17th century into eight single bedroom flats" with four chimneys built for the adjoining flats; the two western bays demolished in the late 19th century (the space became the entrance courtyard); flats reduced to five in the mid-20th century ([St Mary's Hospital Almshouses: Buildings](https://www.stmarysalmshouses.org.uk/about/buildings), fetched 2026-08-23 — no sources cited on the page). CONFIRMED-digest (2026-08-23): "on an east to west line, with a large hall used as the infirmary and a chapel at the east end, all under one roof", "a massive flint building like a great Sussex barn" whose tiled roof reaches "almost to the ground", and in the 1950s the inmates' cubicles "about 10' x 10 ft … with dividing partitions but no ceilings" (Sussex Express / Britain Express / Chichester Society pages in digest). DISCREPANCY carried: the digest's "30ft-high" roof vs the site's "over 40'" — unresolved; the Historic England list entry 1026704 that would settle it is Cloudflare-gated (fetch 403; curl returned the challenge page). So the hall was EIGHT bays, now six, and the dated use-change (open aisled hall → 17th-century cells with chimneys → 19th-century truncation → 20th-century re-partition) is a complete DW-law-5 fossil sequence for the hospital type. Hall length × width NOT FOUND this round (ledger item 3; sub-lane JK owes Orme & Webster / Pevsner).

**1.6 Inns were provided in proportion to town size and were rich men's investments (family B — the tier ladder has a documentary basis).** CONFIRMED-digest (abstract of J. Hare, "Inns, innkeepers and the society of later medieval England, 1350–1600", *Journal of Medieval History* 39/4 (2013), via Semantic Scholar / Taylor & Francis digests, 2026-08-23): "there was a regular provision of inns in accordance with the size and importance of the towns"; inns "generated substantial rent and were evidently felt to be worth considerable investment"; innkeepers "were among the rich and influential members of the town"; the documentation is "central southern England". The article body was not opened (paywalled). Whether a VILLAGE off a main road had an inn at all remains the open negation question for sub-lane AB (answered in §3(c) and §M.4: the negation returns a STRUCTURAL answer, not a null, so it carries no ledger number).

**1.7 Engine-recon finding (CONFIRMED by reading the clean tree; simulation PLAUSIBLE).** See the preamble: the interior grammar's `vice` template and `heals` variant exist, but the nature-inference regex leaves every INN row (including the town REQ row "Inn (multiple)") at `generic`, types "Gladiatorial school" as `learning` and "Hireling hall" as `civic`. The catalog is not at fault; the inference table is (engine-gap flag E-1 in §Σ, to be written).

## §2 · FAMILY A — the drinking house: Alehouse (hamlet L375 "Home-brewed ale sold from someone's back room. The hamlet's main gathering place…") · Ale house (village L476 "Home-based. Women brew and sell ale.") · Taverns (5-20) (town L949 REQ "Drinking establishments. Social hubs.")

*Provenance: sub-lane AB (killed 06:14:28Z after 71 tool calls; no report written). Evidence recovered from its transcript: the Brown 2007 Warwick thesis on Southampton's inns, taverns and alehouses (LOCAL, `RINST4-AB-brown-2007-southampton.txt`, 54 MB PDF fetched by curl, 306 pp., pypdf extract read at the sub-lane's `sed` windows and re-grepped by the parent), Pantin 1958 on the Clarendon/Star Inn and King's Head, Oxford (LOCAL, `RINST4-AB-pantin-clarendon-1958.txt`), the Kent Archaeological Society inventories page, Wikipedia "Alewife (trade)" (Bennett 1996 quotations), the mittelalter-lexikon Gasthäuser page, it.wikipedia Osteria, nl.wikipedia Herberg, and six search digests. The parent's §1.1 stands as the floor finding; this section adds the measured room programme and the tavern.*

**(a) Analogue.** The family has THREE buildings, not one, and the catalog's three rows map onto them with one correction. (1) **The alehouse is a dwelling** (parent §1.1, CONFIRMED; SUB-LANE CONFIRMED via Bennett 1996 quoted in [Wikipedia: Alewife (trade)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alewife_(trade)), fetched 2026-08-23 by sub-lane AB: brewing "could be done in the home, supplemented other incomes"; before the mid-14th century ale was "mostly produced in the home, and sold and consumed in either the home or the local alehouse"; the 1552 licensing act put keepers under the justices — CONFIRMED-digest, Wikipedia "Ale Houses Act 1551"). The measured programme comes from Brown's thirty-three Southampton alehouse-keepers' probate inventories, 1550–1700 (LOCAL, Brown 2007 pp. 58–59): an average of **5.8 rooms** per property "including lofts, cellars and any outbuildings, but not counting gardens or backsides"; twenty-one of the thirty-three traded from **four to seven rooms**; one (a French shoemaker) from a **single room**; three late-17th-century keepers from **two rooms**; the top of the sample was a clothier's eleven rooms "(including a brewhouse and a 'cheese house')" and a cooper's **twelve** (d. 1596). Clark's Canterbury figure is five rooms with a nine-room maximum; Southampton's whole-town average was seven (Brown citing Clark, *Alehouse*, pp. 64–5 — LOCAL). The room names ("forechamber", "chamber next the street", "inner chamber", "back chamber") show most alehouses were the **right-angle narrow-deep town house**, "only one room in width, but had rooms extending to the rear and over upper floors", the hall/parlour/kitchen complex on the ground floor and chambers stacked above (LOCAL, Brown p. 59, citing Laithwaite on Totnes) — i.e. the alehouse is Pantin's ordinary shop-house (R-INST-2 §3) with a drinking function on the front rooms. The drinking rooms are the **hall, parlour or fore chamber at the front**, identified in the inventories by seating: one alehouse's parlour + hall + "little hall" held six framed tables with forms, a pair of playing tables, eight stools, three chairs and wainscot benches; another's hall in 1611 held three tables, two pairs of playing tables and "sixty yards of wainscot benches"; a widow could "seat seventeen people in her fore chamber" in 1674 (LOCAL, Brown p. 65). Service sits at the rear of the ground floor: a kitchen and, "in many cases", a **buttery** holding the pots and trenchers (one alehouse buttery: three pint pots, four wine quarts, half- and quarter-pint pots, nine platters, two dozen trenchers) (LOCAL, Brown p. 67). Where there was no cellar "other rooms would be used": a back chamber, "the room where the beer stands", or the keeper's own shop (LOCAL, Brown p. 67) — the beer store is a FUNCTION that lands on whatever cell is free, DW law 1 exactly. Lodging at this grade is "for pedestrians but not travellers on horseback" (LOCAL, Brown p. 23) and beds "migrated into halls and parlours, especially in smaller premises" (p. 67). Live/work: the keeper sleeps in the **fore chamber next the street**, which doubles as the linen store (one keeper's chamber held three coffers of sheets, tablecloths and napkins; a 1583 will leaves the wife "the place where I now lay next the street" — LOCAL, Brown pp. 66–67). Southampton ran **23 licensed alehouses a year 1619–24** (range 21–26) and 16 in 1661–68 for a town of c. 4,200, with a 1603 peak of 69 tipplers licensed and unlicensed = **1 alehouse per 61 inhabitants** (Clark's ratios: Shrewsbury 1:29, Cambridge 1:52, Faversham 1:61) — LOCAL, Brown pp. 23–25. Women were 13.9 % of tipplers on average 1559–1658 (0 % in 1590, 38.6 % in 1628) and 27–48 % of licensees 1619–24, "the vast majority … widowed householders" (LOCAL, Brown p. 87) — the village row's "Women brew and sell ale" is right for brewing before c. 1350 (Bennett) and right for a licensed MINORITY of keepers after 1552; the hamlet row's "back room" is right. The catalog's town row is the one that needs a correction: (2) **the tavern is a different institution**: it sold wine and hot food, "offered no accommodation for travellers", was confined by the 1553 act to "cities, corporate towns, boroughs, ports and market centres" and CAPPED by number — Southampton three, London forty, York eight, Oxford/Salisbury/Westminster three — a cap the town observed in the 16th century (LOCAL, Brown pp. 26–27). Eight Southampton taverners' inventories average **ten rooms** (range four to fourteen; the largest had "a proliferation of chambers as well as a cellar, stable and designated 'larder house'"), mostly the same vertical "column dwelling", but one 1646 taverner's thirteen rooms include chambers "next to and over 'the gatehouse'" and a stable, i.e. a **courtyard** plan (LOCAL, Brown pp. 59–60). The tavern's principal room seats more than the alehouse's: a 1628 tavern fore chamber held three tables and seated twenty-nine "on a combination of joined stools, low stools and joined chairs", and the same room "was overlooked by a gallery draped with say" (LOCAL, Brown pp. 65, 70). So the catalog's "Taverns (5–20)" at town tier is, in the English register, **taverns 2–3 plus alehouses 20–70**; the figure 5–20 is right only if it counts drinking houses of every class (Devon in 1577: 120 inns, 40 taverns, 400 alehouses — LOCAL, Brayshay 1991 Table I citing SP12/96/405). (3) **The cellar tavern**: Tackley's Inn, Oxford — a vaulted five-bay cellar under five shops, "by 1363 the whole cellar was let as a wine tavern"; by the mid-15th century the east half of the tenement (the back block, two shops and solars, and the cellar below) "was a tavern" (LOCAL, Pantin 1962–3 via `RINST2-pantin.txt`, p. 218). Southampton shows the same: "a proliferation of drinking houses operating out of cellars"; the Queen's Arms alehouse (1613–38) stood on two excavated stone cellars; other undercrofts had "vaulted ceilings, fireplaces, windows, mouldings and independent access from the street"; a 1588 licence "to sell beer in his cellar"; 1602 mariners "drinking together in a cellar near unto the custom house"; 1605 jurors complained of "divers tippling houses kept in cellars very unfitting" (LOCAL, Brown pp. 54–55). London legislation (CONFIRMED-digest of Sagui 2025, *Historical Reflections* 51/2 — the article itself returned 403) forbade "a curtain or a bolted door" obstructing the cellar doorway and let customers "accompany their servers into the basement", which Sagui reads as implying drinking was normally on the ground floor with the cellar as the draw-point — consistent with Brown's "stands" (casks drawn in the cellar, the drink carried up; LOCAL, Brown p. 67) and with Pantin's Oxford King's Head, whose two cellars in 1613 "served as a kind of bar or tap-room, being furnished with a table, a form, trenchers and drinking vessels" (LOCAL, Pantin 1958 p. 117). **Signs**: the ale-stake/bush is the medieval marker (parent §1.1); Southampton's records "yield no references to ale-stakes", and the town lacked the "carved wooden 'hands', metal lanterns or painted red lattices" that marked alehouses in Manchester, Coventry and London respectively (LOCAL, Brown p. 46 citing Clark pp. 67–8); a 1574 order that every tippler hang "a sign painted with the town's arms" was cancelled (p. 46); the tavern's bunch of grapes on a bracket is the wine sign (p. 46). **The brewhouse** is the alehouse's one process outshut: Kent inventories (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Kent Archaeology, Arch. Cant. 61](https://www.kentarchaeology.org.uk/journal/61/seventeenth-and-eighteenth-century-inventories), fetched 2026-08-23) — a Faversham brewer 1701 with parlour, little parlour, "fire room", buttery, chambers over parlour/kitchen/maids, garret, and a copper, two tuns, underback, cool-backs, pump; a Yalding yeoman 1706 with kitchen, **brewhouse** (pump, furnace, brewing fat, four tubs, keelers), baking room, milkhouse, hall, "sink", **small-drink buttery** (five barrels) and **strong-drink buttery** (six barrels), parlour, pantry. **Prosperity/wear**: floor = one front room with a bench and a barrel in the back chamber; rung 1 = hall + parlour + kitchen + buttery, chambers over, beer in a back room; rung 2 = a cellar with stands, a brewhouse outshut, playing tables, wainscot benches, painted cloths (one 1596 twelve-room alehouse had wainscot, painted cloths, silk curtains and a looking glass — LOCAL, Brown p. 70); rung 3 (the tavern) = a wine cellar, a gallery over the fore chamber, a "shuffleboard room" (1646 tavern; 1692/1693 inns — p. 66), a tap house. Decline sheds the brewhouse first (buy-in beer), then the cellar (the beer stands in a chamber), then the parlour (one room, a shoemaker selling from his shop). **Era**: dwelling-alehouse from the 12th century; the 1552 licence; beer (hopped, male-brewed, Low Countries) displaces ale from the late 15th century (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, Wikipedia Alewife); the tap house appears in inns by 1593 and in an alehouse by 1649 (LOCAL, Brown p. 66); the coffee house is post-register. **Siting**: the alehouse is anywhere a house is; the cellar alehouses cluster "on or in the vicinity of the two town quays" (LOCAL, Brown p. 67); the tavern is inside the walls near the market.

**(b) Measured.** Black Dog alehouse, French Street, Southampton, 1704 corporation lease: **61 ft deep × 12 ft 6 in wide** (18.6 × 3.8 m), trading since at least 1656 (LOCAL, Brown p. 59). Alehouse room counts 1–12, mean 5.8, modal 4–7 (n = 33); tavern 4–14, mean 10 (n = 8); inn 10–22+, mean 16 (n = 14) (LOCAL, Brown pp. 58–60). Candle stints 1577: inns 5 lb/week average, taverns 3.6, alehouses "just over two", the largest inn 8 lb, one innkeeper 3 lb (LOCAL, Brown p. 57). Seating: 17 (fore chamber), 29 (tavern fore chamber), "sixty yards of wainscot benches" (LOCAL, Brown p. 65). Tackley's Inn shops 8–12 ft wide over a five-bay cellar (LOCAL, Pantin p. 217). The Oxford twelfth-century cellar under the Clarendon site: **25 × 12 ft, 10 ft high at the crown, floor 4–5 ft below medieval street level, entered by a door and steps from the street** — "the usual arrangement" (LOCAL, Pantin 1958 p. 110). King's Head hall c. 16 ft square; Golden Cross hall 17 × 22 ft (LOCAL, Pantin 1958 p. 116). No measured figure was found for a tavern drinking room or a cellar-tavern in Southampton (the thesis says "no ground plans have been located for early modern establishments" — LOCAL, p. 64); Clark's *English Alehouse* (1983) was not opened (Google Books/archive.org listing only — CONFIRMED-digest).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) The negation "the alehouse was not a separate building" is CONFIRMED both ways: it was a dwelling, AND Southampton's twelve-room alehouses out-consumed one inn in candles and "alehouses with a full complement of wine quarts or taverns with guest chambers" blur every line (LOCAL, Brown p. 28) — institutional category is a LICENCE, not a plan. (ii) Peter Clark's "for the poor, by the poor" slogan is called "overly generalising" by Brown (p. 23); keepers included a clothier, a cooper, a cook and a feltmaker — the alehouse is a by-employment of a craft household, which is why its building is the craft's house. (iii) The tavern cap is statutory (1553) and the boroughs observed it, but wine was also retailed "on an unofficial basis by merchants involved in its import" (three merchants paid to discharge fines in 1585 — LOCAL, Brown p. 27): the tavern function leaks into the merchant's house. (iv) The Sagui cellar reading (drinking upstairs, cellar as draw-point) and the Southampton/Oxford evidence (drinking IN the cellar, licensed and unlicensed) are both true: the cellar tavern is a PORT/university-town form where stone undercrofts with street doors existed (R-INST-2 §3 Tackley's; Winchelsea; Southampton), not a general one. (v) Southampton's lack of ale-stakes vs London's red lattice: the sign is regional; the grammar should not mint an ale-stake everywhere.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** Alehouse (hamlet L375, village L476): the dwelling's own — `THROUGH_ROOM` (fore room → hall → back chamber; the narrow-deep plan is "one room in width"), a `STAIR_HALL{WINDER}` or ladder to the chambers; no passage of its own. Where the host is a hall house (village), the `CROSS_PASSAGE{HALL}` is inherited from DWR1A §2-bis.1. Tavern (town L949): the shop-house `THROUGH_ROOM` plus a `STAIR_HALL{DOWN}` to the cellar — the London rule that the cellar door may not be curtained or bolted is a `joint.refused-to-be-severable` attribute (the opposite of the CIRC addendum's `severable` joint: a joint that MUST stay open by ordinance); the cellar-tavern's own street door and steps is the `CELLAR{SEPARATELY_LET}` joint of the CIRC addendum §4.4 with `approach: STREET_STEPS` (Oxford: "entered from the east by a door and steps from the street"); the courtyard tavern (1646) carries the inn's `CROSS_PASSAGE{ENTRY}` and a `GALLERY{INN_GALLERY}` (the 1628 fore chamber "overlooked by a gallery" is an INTERNAL gallery over a drinking room — a `GALLERY_OVER` sub-form at tavern grade, single-sourced). Widths: none measured for this family; the Black Dog's 12 ft 6 in plot width caps every cell at ≤ 3.5 m clear (a derived bound, not a measurement). Stair: ladder (single-room alehouse) → winder (4–7 rooms) → the cellar's street steps.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** `BUTTERY` (pots, trenchers, quarts — a FIXTURE-scale cell "in many cases"; the Kent pair "small drink buttery" / "strong drink buttery" is two cells by strength, each with 5–6 barrels); `CELLAR` with `stands` (the draw-point; inns have TWO by drink — family B); where absent, the beer store is a FUNCTION on a back chamber or the shop (no cell minted); `STILL_ROOM`-class = the **brewhouse** outshut (copper/furnace, tuns, cool-backs, pump) — a process cell with a flue and a water need, adjacency REQUIRED_NEAR(water) and FAR(chambers) PLAUSIBLE; `LARDER` only at tavern grade ("larder house", 16th c.); `PANTRY` only at inn grade; `ATTIC/GARRET_STORE` = the "cheese house"/lofts of the twelve-room alehouse; the publican's fore chamber is the **linen store** (chests — fixtures). Light: the buttery and cellar NONE; the brewhouse ANY with ventilation. Prohibition: none recorded; the London cellar-door rule is the inverse (a door that may not be obstructed).

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: DWELLING_ALEHOUSE` (hamlet/village) = the host dwelling's parti (DWR1A) + function set `{drink_room(front), beer_store(back|cellar), brewhouse?(outshut)}`; `parti: SHOP_HOUSE_TAVERN` (town) = R-INST-2's right-angle shop-house + `{fore_chamber(seats 20–30), wine_cellar(stands), kitchen, larder?, gallery_over?, shuffleboard_room?}`; `parti: CELLAR_TAVERN` (town, port/university register) = `CELLAR{SEPARATELY_LET, STREET_STEPS}` as the PRIMARY cell with hearth and windows; `parti: COURTYARD_TAVERN` (town ceiling) = family B's courtyard at tavern scale (gatehouse chambers + stable). `functions[]`: floor = drink_room + beer_store; rungs = + kitchen → + buttery → + cellar → + brewhouse → + parlour → + playing tables → + tap house; ceiling = the wine tavern with a gallery and a games room. `fixtures[]`: `BENCH_WAINSCOT{yards}`, `TABLE_FRAMED+FORM`, `PLAYING_TABLES`, `STANDS{casks}`, `POTS_SET`, `PAINTED_CLOTHS` (prosperity), `LOOKING_GLASS` (ceiling), `SIGN{ALE_STAKE|BUSH|LATTICE|LANTERN|HAND|GRAPES|BOARD}` (regional; the grapes = wine), `TRUCKLE_BED` (lodging on the floor of a chamber). Structural: `hearth` (every drinking room), `flue` (brewhouse), `waterSupply` (brewhouse). Licensing: alehouse tier ≥ hamlet, any prosperity, era ≥ 12th c., licence-gated after 1552 (a NUMBER cap per town is a law, not a draw); tavern tier ≥ town, statutory cap 2–3 per town (London 40), era ≥ 13th c. (Tackley's 1363), culture: England/Low Countries (the tavern/alehouse split), Germany (Schankstube/Gaststube), Italy (osteria). VERDICT: hamlet Alehouse **HOSTED** (host: the keeper's dwelling; a function set, no building); village Ale house **HOSTED** (same) with `OWN BUILDING` only as the 12-room ceiling; town Taverns **BUILDING** (the shop-house tavern) with `CELLAR_TAVERN` as a register-gated variant, and the REQ row's COUNT corrected to taverns 2–3 + alehouses 20–70 in the English register. HOME: `INTERIOR_KINDS.vice` template (`common` + `kitchen` + `cellar` + `lodging`) fits the tavern; for the alehouse the engine must NOT mint a `vice` building — it must attach `common{bar, bench, table, hearth}` to a dwelling (NO TYPED HOME for "function on a host dwelling", ODQ §462). The FACET_INFERENCE regex maps "Alehouse"/"Ale house" to `generic` (§0 recon) — flagged in §Σ.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) The REQ row "Taverns (5–20)" cannot be satisfied by minting 5–20 buildings of one kind; it needs a COUNT SPLIT by licence class (tavern cap × town size; alehouse ratio 1:30–1:60 of population) — the first catalog REQ whose number is a ratio, not a count. (2) The "function on a host dwelling" outcome (R-INST-2 §Σ gap 5, HOSTED) must allow the HOST to be a DWELLING of the demography layer, not an institution — the alehouse keeper is a clothier, cooper, shoemaker, widow; the engine's one-lot-one-institution model needs `hostedIn: DWELLING(householdId)`. (3) `CELLAR` as the PRIMARY cell with its own street steps (the cellar tavern) is a law-7 joint whose surface building may be someone else's shop — `separatelyLet` (R-INST-2 gap 4) plus `primaryCell: CELLAR`. (4) A door that may NOT be closed (the London cellar door) is the inverse of `severable` — add `joint.mandatoryOpen`. (5) The licence COUNT CAP is a settlement-level law (tavern 2–3 per town) that the parti draw must read before it draws.

**(h) Continental vs English.** North of the Alps the **Gaststube** "was usually the only heated room, where all activities took place — meetings, eating, drinking, and often sleeping" (CONFIRMED-digest, Planet Wissen / mittelalter-lexikon digest), with a **Schankstube** (tap room for casual drinkers) distinct from the Gaststube (dining room where lodgers ate together) in the larger house; the minimum Gasthaus programme was "Küche, Speise- und Schlafraum", beds held two or more and stood several to a Kammer, there was no toilet or washroom "bis zum Ende des MA." (waste went to the stable or dunghill), and the late-medieval house gained Ställe, Lagerschuppen, Wagenremisen, Brau-, Back- und Schlachthaus and gender-separated dormitories (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [mittelalter-lexikon: Gasthäuser](https://www.mittelalter-lexikon.de/wiki/Gasth%C3%A4user), fetched 2026-08-23); **Trinkstuben** and from the 1330s the guilds' **Zunftstuben** are the German drinking rooms that England's tavern and the livery hall split between them (CONFIRMED-digest). The Italian **osteria** is wine-first ("l'elemento immancabile") with food optional, documented at Bologna 1375, Ferrara 1435, Osteria del Sole 1465, often "poor and neglected" buildings whose only architectural note in the fetched page is "a small internal courtyard" with tables (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [it.wikipedia Osteria](https://it.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osteria), fetched 2026-08-23) — the Mediterranean drinking house is a COURT, not a stove-room. The Low Countries **herberg** has a **gelagkamer** (common room), stallen and a **doorrit** (carriage drive-through), and hanging **uithangborden** (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [nl.wikipedia Herberg](https://nl.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herberg), fetched 2026-08-23). The Swiss HLS page (Tavernenrecht/Pintenrecht) returned 403 twice — the legal tiering of the continental inn is ledgered. The grammar consequence: the heated-room register (`Stube` with a Kachelofen, one heated room for everything) versus the hearth register (an open hearth in every drinking room) is a CULTURE gate on the fixture set, not a new parti.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None.

---

## §3 · FAMILY B — the inn: Wayside inn (hamlet L382 "A room above a stable and a common meal for travelers passing through. Exists because the road does.") · Travelers' inn (village L461 "A single inn serving traders, pilgrims, and travelers passing through. Rooms, stabling, and a common meal.") · Inn (multiple) (town L943 REQ "Lodging for traveling merchants.") · Inns and taverns (district) (city L1563 REQ "Multiple inn districts…")

*Provenance: sub-lane AB (Brown 2007 LOCAL; Pantin 1958 LOCAL; Survey of London vol. 22; Wikipedia George Southwark / New Inn / Glastonbury; medieval.eu digest of Hare 2013; knowyourlondon page saved as `RINST4-AB-kyl-borough.html`; the Borough High Street and Everitt/"villages without inns" digests) and sub-lane CD (the Historic England list entries for the New Inn 1245714 and the George 1378357, read through the browser pane and saved as `RINST4-CD-HE-1245714-newinn.txt` — the Cloudflare gate that stopped the parent and AB was passed by the browser; the Survey of London page text `RINST4-CD-sol22-boroughhigh.txt`; the National Trust HBSMR record). Parent §1.2, §1.3 and §1.6 stand and are not restated; this section supplies what they owed.*

**(a) Analogue.** Hare's summary of the late-medieval English inn is the floor programme: "a hall, kitchen, stable and storage plus chambers and the innkeeper's accommodation", with private locked rooms becoming common although "many chambers initially offered only communal sleeping"; inns "became prominent after 1350"; by 1400 "provincial capitals had 10–20 establishments, market towns hosted 2–5, and smaller towns typically had one"; Winchester College spent over £400 on the Angel at Andover 1366–1404 for an expected £10 a year (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [medieval.eu: Medieval Inns](https://www.medieval.eu/medieval-inns/), fetched 2026-08-23, summarising Hare 2013 — the article itself stayed paywalled). The PLAN evidence is now four measured or described buildings across two centuries. (1) **Marshall's Inn, Oxford, 1380 lease** (LOCAL, Pantin 1958 p. 85; the same house in Pantin 1962–3 no. 24): a timber-framed house round a courtyard — two shops flanking an entry, two chambers over them "reached by two stair-turrets with an 'alure' or gallery between them", the hall and "dispence" across the back of the court with a chamber over, two chambers on the south side, a solar over a cellar on the north, and "more chambers and a brewhouse, and presumably the stables" towards the garden; "by 1406 … already being used as an inn", with a painted sign and a lease clause that the lodgers (hospites) "are not to cause destruction" — so the courtyard merchant house BECAME an inn without rebuilding: the parti precedes the institution (law 5). (2) **The New Inn, Gloucester, mid-15th c.** (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Historic England list entry 1245714](https://historicengland.org.uk/listing/the-list/list-entry/1245714), read 2026-08-23 via the browser pane; OGL v3.0): "a large, rectangular building of four ranges enclosing a courtyard entered from Northgate Street through a carriage way in the west range; within the courtyard open galleries providing access to chambers (originally 7.9m x 4.7–5.9m, since subdivided) on each of the upper floors of the four ranges, underbuilt at ground-floor level"; two shops north of the carriage way and the hall/restaurant south of it on the street; "an enclosed yard at rear of the main building is entered from the main courtyard through a carriage way at the south end of the east range" (the stable yard); "Pantin considers that the hall and adjacent kitchen were probably sited in the south range, stabling to rear and parlour in centre of north range"; all four ranges three storeys and attics; the west front "originally of seven framed bays with continuous jetties"; "a continuous gallery at first-floor level on all sides … reached by an external flight of stairs originally in another position and rebuilt in the north-east corner in the C19"; on the east side "above the balustrade is an arcade of six bays" and "the second floor extends over the gallery"; the carriage way is "framed by a pair of massive, moulded timber posts rising to the underside of the second-floor jetty with an arched brace to either side" with "an early infill wall" above the lintel beam; and in the roof over the carriage way "an open truss … indicating the former existence of an upper hall or chamber" — the GATE CHAMBER over the entry. Dendrochronology puts the build at 1432 (CONFIRMED-digest, Historic England blog); "at least 20 bedchambers", c. 200 guests (CONFIRMED-digest); two courtyards and two tiers of galleries (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [gloucestershirepubs.co.uk](https://www.gloucestershirepubs.co.uk/pubs/new-inn-hotel-northgate-street-gloucester/), no sources cited); a real-tennis court was installed in the rear courtyard in 1649 (LOCAL, Edmonds, *The Story of Gloucester Pubs*, `RINST4-CD-gloucester-pubs-edmonds.txt` p. 8 — a local-society walking guide, unreferenced). (3) **The Star Inn, Oxford, c. 1550–60** (LOCAL, Pantin 1958 pp. 94–101): "a block of about 60 feet by 70 feet, built round a small courtyard"; the street range 60 × 22 ft, its ground floor in four bays — a north room, "the entry", and a two-bay south room, all on the site of medieval shops and "probably used as inn parlours"; the first floor had three rooms including a three-bay "Great Chamber" with a stone fireplace; "along the west side of the range was a gallery, giving separate access to the various rooms; this had chamfered posts and originally unglazed openings towards the courtyard, which were afterwards filled with windows" — "a good example of those galleries which were a feature of medieval and later inns, where a desire for privacy and separate access developed comparatively early, whereas in private houses people were content with passage rooms until a much later date"; the stair probably at the gallery's north-west corner; "no trace of any cellars under the east range", but cellars under the north and west ranges in 1380; the south range's ground-floor room 27 × 16 ft was "probably the hall" with a cellar below. (4) **The King's Head, Oxford, 1613 inventory walked through the plan** (LOCAL, Pantin 1958 pp. 115–118): an L-shaped inn — a short three-storey street range of c. 1600 "containing the gateway, parlours and chambers" and a long back range along one side of the yard holding the hall ("about 16 feet square"; "in inns of this period the hall was less important than one might expect, since many meals would be served in private parlours or chambers"), a little parlour, the **spence**, the kitchen (a large stack), the **larder**, the **warehouse**; the "new building" on the street, 26 × 14 ft, with a "portall" (internal porch), wainscot, fixed benches, a wooden bed with hangings and a truckle bed — a bed-sitting room; a "men's chamber" for men-servants beside the gateway; a great parlour with two beds; upstairs the parlour chamber, the great chamber (the two best rooms, two beds each), the **gatehouse chamber** over the gateway ("rather poorly furnished with only one bed"), the green chamber, a row of cocklofts on the street; then "along the gallery of the back wing" the hall chamber (four beds), the chamber over the larder (two beds, a bible, a psalter and a lute — "possibly … the room where the inn-keeper lodged"), two lumber rooms over warehouse and kitchen, a stair "at this point in the gallery" to the cockloft over the kitchen; then "the two cellars, which, as usual, served as a kind of bar or tap-room"; then "the stables, the hayloft, the 'backside' or yard, where evidently the washing was done, and the 'little garden', where there was an arbour". The King's Head "had galleries all along the courtyard side of the back range, and thus had a rather easier system of circulation and access to the first floor rooms" than the Golden Cross; goods £235 against the Golden Cross's £1,087 (1623). This one inventory is the tranche's best ROOM-BY-ROOM inn programme and it confirms the gallery as the inn's circulation spine, the cellar as its tap-room, the gate chamber as its worst bed, and the innkeeper's lodging as an upper chamber in the service range. (5) **The George, Southwark, 1677** (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Historic England list entry 1378357](https://historicengland.org.uk/listing/the-list/list-entry/1378357), read 2026-08-23 via the browser pane; and [National Trust HBSMR MNA147890](https://heritagerecords.nationaltrust.org.uk/HBSMR/MonRecord.aspx?uid=MNA147890), fetched 2026-08-23): "the south range of a coaching inn ranged around a quadrangle"; the galleried west half "would have comprised three bedrooms at each level" reached from the galleries, the ungalleried east half "likely to have served a more communal function … two large rooms forming a kitchen and hall"; a C17 plank door to a cellar "which has reused medieval beams and squared stonework of possible medieval origins"; the present stair is an early-C18 dogleg reset c. 1730. (The parent's §1.3 carries the Survey of London description; the Survey's 1855 report adds that "most of the east end of the yard was occupied by stabling" and the Great Eastern Railway had offices "in rooms on the north side of the inn yard" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Survey of London 22, pp. 9–30](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/survey-london/vol22/pp9-30), fetched 2026-08-23.) DISCREPANCY carried: the HE entry says the north range "was pulled down by the Company in 1874"; the HBSMR says 1889; the Survey of London says Guy's walled off the east yard in 1874 and sold the rest to the railway — the 1874 date is the sale, the demolition date is unresolved. (6) **Southampton's inns in inventories** (LOCAL, Brown 2007 pp. 60–63): fourteen innholders average **sixteen rooms**; the Dolphin and the Crown "at least twenty-two", the smallest ten and eleven; the Dolphin's yard "was originally galleried"; the Bear had "a system of dual entries (onto Above Bar Street and Windmill Lane) characteristic of the type"; inns are known as courtyard or gatehouse type "because of occasional reference in town records to their gates"; the outbuildings — "barns, millhouses, brewhouses, coal houses, bushel houses, wash houses" and two slaughterhouses — and the land: the George's consolidated plot "50 feet wide by 400 feet deep", gardens with an "arbor" of "tables and benches"; a 1478 inn "extended and remodelled" c. 1615 with a "new parlour", "new chamber", "new forechamber"; a 1587 rebuild "from the ground up" that "encroached upon the highway … two foot or more" and paid an annual acknowledgement; service rooms "pantries, larders, cocklofts, haylofts, drying lofts, counting houses, linen rooms" and "the room used to put the tobacco pipes in" (1693); chambers of typically three beds with truckles and locked chests; the **tap house** (George by 1593; Dolphin 1678; the Bear's "drink house" 1647); "shuffleboard" rooms (1692, 1693); the 1686 War Office census: **179 guest beds and stabling for 287 horses** in the town, third in Hampshire; the 1623 corporation claim of "four hundred beds" in inns, alehouses and private houses. Staff sleep in: a "maid's chamber" (1615), an "ostler's chamber with a bedstead and two horse harnesses" (1647), "a bed for the brewers" in the rear stables (1570), three garrets with nine beds for servants (1708), an ostler sharing a bed with a tapster (LOCAL, Brown pp. 96–97). (7) **The gatehouse type**: the George and Pilgrims, Glastonbury, c. 1475 — stone-faced, panelled, three storeys, a "wide flagstone passage" with two rooms left and two narrow rooms right, a stone newel stair at the rear right serving both upper floors, "large cellars beneath the bar", a 1658 horsemill for malt (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: George Hotel and Pilgrims' Inn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Hotel_and_Pilgrims'_Inn,_Glastonbury), fetched 2026-08-23; CONFIRMED-digest, CAMRA). **Siting**: the main street and the gate (Gloucester's Northgate Street; Borough High Street at the bridgehead; Oxford's Cornmarket; Southampton's Above Bar suburb outside the Bargate — LOCAL, Brown p. 62); the inn is the town's **largest private building** ("if alehouses resembled fence-posts, then inns were more akin to fields" — Brown p. 60 quoting Estabrook). **Live/work**: the innkeeper in an upper chamber of the service range (King's Head) or the fore chamber (Southampton); ostlers, tapsters, chamberlains, maids and brewers all on site in named chambers, the stable and the garrets. **Prosperity/wear**: floor (hamlet) = a house with a spare chamber, a stable and a sign; rung 1 = the L-plan (street range with gateway + one back range: King's Head); rung 2 = the full courtyard with a gallery on one side (Star); rung 3 = galleries on all sides with an external stair, two yards, a gate chamber (New Inn); ceiling = the three-tier galleried coaching inn with a tap house, coffee room, assembly room (the Star's 1832 room 63 × 31 × 22 ft — LOCAL, Pantin p. 112). Decline sheds the galleries (glazed in, then walled: the Star's "afterwards filled with windows"), the rear yard (let to hop merchants and a railway: the George 1855), the stabling, and finally the inn keeps only its tap-room ("the innkeepers kept their tap-rooms … open for passing trade" — LOCAL, knowyourlondon page).

**(b) Measured.** New Inn chambers **7.9 × 4.7–5.9 m** (26 × 15–19 ft) originally (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, HE 1245714) — the first measured INN CHAMBER in the corpus; the gallery is an "arcade of six bays" on the east side of a courtyard whose east range fronts the rear carriage way — gallery WIDTH not stated; the carriage way's posts rise to the second-floor jetty (height ≥ two storeys in the frame; lintel height not stated; clear WIDTH not stated — ledger). Star Inn: block c. 60 × 70 ft (18.3 × 21.3 m); street range 60 × 22 ft (18.3 × 6.7 m), four ground-floor bays of which ONE is the entry — so the entry bay ≤ c. 15 ft / 4.6 m structural (a derived bound); south range room 27 × 16 ft (8.2 × 4.9 m) (LOCAL, Pantin 1958 pp. 94–101). King's Head: hall c. 16 ft square (4.9 m); "new building" 26 × 14 ft (7.9 × 4.3 m); the 1849 yard's west end "84–89 feet by 46 feet" (25.6–27.1 × 14 m) (LOCAL, Pantin pp. 92, 116). Golden Cross hall 17 × 22 ft (LOCAL). Clarendon site: frontage 90 ft, depth c. 210 ft, "stabling for 30 horses, coachhouses, lofts" (19th c.) (LOCAL, Pantin p. 121); Oxford's medieval blocks ran back "over two hundred feet" (p. 83). Twelfth-century cellar 25 × 12 ft, 10 ft high (LOCAL, p. 110). Southampton: inn room counts 10–22+, mean 16 (n = 14); the George's plot 50 × 400 ft (15 × 122 m); 1686: 179 beds / 287 horses (LOCAL, Brown). Borough High Street: inns "extended back … by at least 160 feet (50 m)" with "a long, narrow yard via an arched entrance" and shared boundary walls; twelve inn yards on the east side on Rocque 1746 (LOCAL, `RINST4-AB-kyl-borough.txt` — an enthusiast page, unreferenced); "twenty-three coaching inns … at various times" (CONFIRMED-digest); 1381 poll tax "22 innkeepers in the district – with 12 on the high street" (CONFIRMED-digest, a history blog). George 1874 plan "shows the original extent" — not measured in the fetched text. Hare's density figures (10–20 / 2–5 / 1) above. NOT FOUND: any stated gallery width or carriage-way clear width/height for a medieval inn (searched by AB: "medieval inn gateway carriage entrance width height feet archway"; "New Inn Gloucester courtyard feet galleries measured plan"; by the parent: three searches in round 1; by CD: "New Inn … gallery measurements" — all null). The carriage-passage and gallery width BUCKETS are therefore derived in (d) from the coach dimensions (family C) and the bay bound, not measured.

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) The NEGATION "villages had no inn" returns a STRUCTURAL answer rather than a clean null: inns were urban — "a regular provision of inns in accordance with the size and importance of the towns" (Hare, CONFIRMED-digest), Everitt's *English Urban Inn* is the standard starting point, and alehouses "were inadequate for habitual travellers, especially those who were carting goods" (CONFIRMED-digest, Maskell & Gregory / tandfonline digest); the 1577 Privy Council certificate gives Devon 120 innholders, 40 taverns, 400 alehouses and Cornwall NO innholders, 30 taverns, 132 alehouses (LOCAL, Brayshay 1991 Table I, SP12/96/405) — Brayshay reads the Cornish zero as "a misunderstanding regarding definitions". Devon's 120 inns against its parishes (PLAUSIBLE: on the order of 450 parishes) means most villages had no inn; the ones that did sat on "customary stages" of the post roads, Harrison's inns "builded … for receiving of such travellers and strangers as pass to and fro" (LOCAL, Brayshay p. 83). So the catalog's hamlet "Wayside inn" (baseChance 0.2) and village "Travelers' inn" (0.6) are RIGHT as road-gated entries and the village figure is HIGH unless the village sits on a through-road: the licence is `onRoute`, not tier. (ii) "Purpose-built" is only half true: Marshall's Inn was a merchant's courtyard house that became an inn (1380 → 1406); the Star was rebuilt c. 1550 on a medieval tenement; the George was rebuilt "probably on the old plan" after a fire — the inn parti is the courtyard house's, re-used (law 5). (iii) Pantin's courtyard/gatehouse typology is contested by Brown: Southampton's inns "can be confirmed as either of the block or gatehouse variety because of occasional reference … to their gates" — i.e. the distinction is often undecidable from records (LOCAL, Brown p. 61). (iv) The gallery is NOT universal: the Golden Cross had none and used passage rooms; the King's Head had one along one range; the New Inn on all four; Pantin's sentence that inns developed "a desire for privacy and separate access … comparatively early" is the grammar's licence for an early `GALLERY` cell in this kind only. (v) The catalog's hamlet text "A room above a stable" is a PLAUSIBLE floor with no fetched example; the Southampton "bed for the brewers" in the rear stables (1570) is the nearest primary — staff, not guests. (vi) The George's north-range demolition date conflicts (1874/1889) — carried, not resolved.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** Hamlet Wayside inn: the host dwelling's `THROUGH_ROOM` + ladder/winder to "the room above the stable" (`VERTICAL{LADDER}` into a loft — a FIXTURE, CIRC addendum). Village/town inn, rung 1 (L-plan, King's Head): `CROSS_PASSAGE{ENTRY}` through the street range to the yard, with the **gate chamber** over it (`REQUIRED_ABOVE` — the worst-furnished bed); `THROUGH_ROOM` along the back range at ground (hall → little parlour → spence → kitchen → larder → warehouse: Pantin's inventory walk is an enfilade); `GALLERY{INN_GALLERY, OPEN_ONE_SIDE, UPPER}` "all along the courtyard side of the back range" with a stair rising FROM the gallery to the cockloft; `STAIR_HALL{WINDER}` in the street range; `STAIR_HALL{DOWN}` to two cellars that are the tap-room. Rung 2–3 (Star, New Inn): the gallery becomes a RING on one then four sides, "giving separate access to the various rooms", originally UNGLAZED (open) and later glazed (a dated `enclosure` transition OPEN_ONE_SIDE → ENCLOSED — a fossil); the external flight of stairs to the ring (`STAIR_HALL{EXTERNAL}`); Marshall's 1380 form is the proto-type: TWO stair-turrets with an "alure" between them (`STAIR_TURRET ×2` + `GALLERY{TRESAUNCE/ALURE}` — the same 1380 cell the CIRC addendum records for the merchant house); a SECOND carriage way from the main court to the rear stable yard (`CROSS_PASSAGE{ENTRY} ×2` in series: street → guest court → stable court); `EXTERIOR_WALK{YARD ×2}`. The gatehouse type (Glastonbury): a "wide flagstone passage" as `CROSS_PASSAGE{ENTRY}` with rooms either side and a `STAIR_HALL{VICE}` at the rear. Dual street entries (the Bear: two streets) = a `THROUGH_LANE`-like yard with two `ENTRY` joints. Widths — measured: NONE for the passage or gallery (ledger items 1–2); BOUNDED: the Star's entry ≤ 4.6 m structural bay; the medieval route "suitable enough for horses but would not admit vehicles of any large size" until a late-18th-century by-pass entry was cut (LOCAL, Pantin p. 89; the Angel, High Street, did the same) — so the pre-coach inn entry is a HORSE passage. Proposed BUCKETS (derived, vetoable): `ENTRY{HORSE}` 1.8–2.4 m clear, height ≥ 2.4 m (a laden packhorse; PLAUSIBLE), era ≤ c. 1650; `ENTRY{COACH}` ≥ 2.4–3.0 m clear wide × ≥ 2.8–3.2 m high (the Science Museum mail coach 1815–30 is 1.8 m wide × 2.45 m high overall, family C — with outside passengers and luggage more), era ≥ c. 1650 and mandatory at the coaching grade; the New Inn's two-storey-tall carriage-way frame (posts to the second-floor jetty) sits in the COACH bucket by construction even though its roof truss says a chamber once sat over it. Gallery: `GALLERY{INN_GALLERY}` width bucket NARROW–STANDARD 1.2–1.8 m (PLAUSIBLE — one person and a chamber door; the George's six-bay gallery on "wooden Doric columns" and cantilever beams cannot be much deeper than a cantilever allows); length = the range (18–27 m at the Star/New Inn). Stair: winder (rung 1) → external straight flight to the gallery (rung 2–3) → stair-turret pair (the 1380 merchant form) → the vice (Glastonbury) → dogleg (George c. 1730) → the coaching inn's grand stair (post-register).

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** `CELLAR ×2` by drink ("wine cellar" + "street cellar"/"beer cellar" — Southampton; two cellars at the King's Head) with `stands`, doubling as the tap-room — `primaryFunction: DRAW + DRINK`; the street cellar with its own steps (`SEPARATELY_LET` capable — Tackley's); `PANTRY` + `LARDER` + `SPENCE` (the King's Head: spence beside the hall, larder beyond the kitchen — the medieval `ANNEXED_TO_KITCHEN` polarity of the CIRC addendum §4.4); `STORE{WAREHOUSE}` in the back range (the inn as merchants' depot — "centres for marketing of such diverse products as fish, beer, wine and sometimes cloth", Hare via medieval.eu) with a lumber room over; `ATTIC/GARRET_STORE` = cocklofts (guest beds AND lumber), hayloft over the stable, "drying lofts"; `CLOSET{LINEN}` = the "linen room" (inn) or the keeper's fore-chamber chests (alehouse/inn); `CLOSET{PLATE/COUNTING}` = "counting houses" (Southampton inns); the stable's `tack` (harness in the ostler's chamber — a fixture); the yard's `coal house`, `bushel house`, `wash house`, `brewhouse`, `millhouse`, `slaughterhouse`, `barn` as detached `STORE`/process cells; the "room used to put the tobacco pipes in" (1693) is a `CLOSET` of one fixture. Adjacency: cellar under the street range (King's Head c. 1600 — a cellar "found under B", the gateway bay) OR under the back ranges (1380), never both in the fetched cases; the tap-room cellar `REQUIRED_NEAR(hall|entry)`; the hayloft `REQUIRED_ABOVE(stable)`; the brewhouse/wash house `REQUIRED_FAR(chambers)` PLAUSIBLE; the counting house `REQUIRED_NEAR(warehouse)` (R-INST-2 §3). Light: cellars NONE (one "ancient light" into a cellar was blocked by a neighbour in 1601 — LOCAL, Brown p. 63: a cellar light is a litigated fixture); larder NORTH PLAUSIBLE (not stated).

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti` ids: `HOUSE_WITH_SPARE_CHAMBER` (hamlet L382: a dwelling + stable + loft + sign; HOSTED on the keeper's house), `L_PLAN_GATE_AND_BACK_RANGE` (rung 1: King's Head, Golden Cross), `COURTYARD_ONE_GALLERY` (Star), `COURTYARD_GALLERY_RING` (New Inn, Dolphin), `GATEHOUSE_BLOCK` (Glastonbury: a street block with a wide passage, a vice and cellars, the yard behind), `DOUBLE_COURT` (New Inn's guest court + stable court; Southampton's dual-entry Bear), `INN_STRIP` (city L1563: N inns side by side, each a 50-ft frontage × 120–160 m deep yard with one arch — Borough High Street; the district is a STREET parti, as R-INST-2 §15 found for the stable district). `functions[]`: floor = `entry(gate)`, `hall(small)`, `kitchen`, `chambers{≥2, multi-bed}`, `stable`, `cellar(tap)`, `sign`; rungs = + `parlour(s)` → + `gallery` → + `gate_chamber` → + `second_cellar(by drink)` → + `warehouse` + `counting_house` → + `tap_house` (≥ 1593) → + `brewhouse`/`wash_house`/`bakehouse` outbuildings → + `games_room` (shuffleboard ≥ 1646) → + `second_yard` → + `assembly_room` (post-register); ceiling = the galleried coaching inn. `fixtures[]`: `BEDS{3 + truckle}` per chamber, `LOCKED_CHESTS` (guests' goods), `PORTALL` (internal porch in the best parlour), `WAINSCOT+FIXED_BENCH`, `STANDS` (cellar), `SIGN_ON_POST` ("set out the post of his inn" — LOCAL, Brown p. 46), `HORSEMILL` (malt; Glastonbury 1658), `ARBOUR` (garden), `HARNESS_RACK` (ostler's chamber). Structural buckets: `openCourt`, `jettiedRanges`, `galleryRing`, `gatePassage{HORSE|COACH}`, `cellarVault`. Licensing: hamlet/village = `onRoute` (stage/pilgrim road/bridgehead) × tier; town = REQ "Inn (multiple)" count 1 (small town) / 2–5 (market town) / 10–20 (provincial capital) from Hare — a count ladder by town rank; city = the strip district REQ; culture: England/Low Countries (courtyard + doorrit), Germany (Gasthof with Hof), Italy/Spain (the osteria/posada court — untested here), the han (R-INST-2 §16). VERDICT: hamlet Wayside inn **HOSTED** (host: a dwelling on the road; the lodging is a function on its loft/chamber and stable — the catalog text is right); village Travelers' inn **BUILDING** (`L_PLAN` or `COURTYARD_ONE_GALLERY`) gated by `onRoute`; town Inn (multiple) **BUILDING** (courtyard partis, 1–20 by rank); city Inns and taverns (district) **BUILDING ×N as a STREET parti** plus the family A taverns and alehouses mixed among them ("a multitude of taverns and ale-houses with names similar to the inns" on Rocque — LOCAL, kyl page). HOME: the `vice` template's `common + kitchen + cellar + lodging` is the inn's floor and `lodging` at tier ≥ 3 is right; NO TYPED HOME for yard, gallery, stable, gate chamber, tap house, warehouse (the inn's ROOM_KINDS gap is the largest in the tranche: six cells); the FACET_INFERENCE regex leaves every inn at `generic` (§Σ E-1).

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) The inn is the English register's FIRST early-licensed `GALLERY` ring (Pantin's "comparatively early"), an OPEN → ENCLOSED transition (unglazed → glazed → walled) that is a dated fossil — the grammar's `enclosure` attribute needs `mintedBy/killedBy` years. (2) TWO `ENTRY` passages in series (street → court → stable court) and TWO `YARD` cells — the `DOUBLE_COURT` parti needs a yard GRAPH, not one yard. (3) The gate passage has an ERA-GRADED width class: HORSE (medieval) vs COACH (≥ c. 1650) — the same cell re-cut by a technology (the stage-coach), with the by-pass entry as a `mintedBy: COACHING` second joint; DWR1A's mechanisms-travel law applies (the coach travels; the entry re-cuts). (4) The gate CHAMBER is a `REQUIRED_ABOVE(entry)` cell with the LOWEST furnishing grade — law 9's status-graded storeys inverted locally (cf. the playhouse, §1.4). (5) Room COUNT is the inn's prosperity instrument (10 / 16 / 22 rooms; 5.8 / 10 / 16 across the three classes) — P1c should measure room-count distributions per class from inventories (Brown's n = 55 is a ready dataset). (6) The city REQ row is a STRIP of N repeated inn partis on one street — R-INST-2's N-IDENTICAL-CELLS generator at BUILDING scale (gap 3), with frontage 15 m × depth 50–120 m per unit.

**(h) Continental vs English.** The German **Gasthof** is the same courtyard with the Stube register — one heated Gaststube, dormitory Kammern, Stallungen, Wagenremisen (mittelalter-lexikon, family A (h)); the Thurn-und-Taxis **Posthalterei** (family C) is its coaching grade. The Low Countries **herberg** names the parts the English inn has without naming them: gelagkamer (the common drinking/eating room), doorrit (the carriage drive-through), stallen, and the sign (uithangbord); it "also functioned as the community's primary public gathering space for meetings, legal proceedings, and auctions" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, nl.wikipedia Herberg) — the HOSTED functions of R-INST-1 (court) and R-INST-2 (auction) sit in it. The Italian **osteria** is wine-and-court (family A); the Spanish posada/Italian albergo courtyard and the Alpine Hospiz were NOT researched (AB's budget ran out on the English primaries) — ledgered. The Roman **mansio** is the ceiling and the origin: at Letocetum (Wall) a two-storey stone mansio of the early second century "consisted of a group of rooms around a central colonnaded courtyard, among these rooms were a kitchen, well, latrine and sleeping apartments", with a bath house to the south-west (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Historic England scheduling 1006108](https://historicengland.org.uk/listing/the-list/list-entry/1006108), read 2026-08-23 via the browser pane; the first timber mansio's court "approximately 62 feet by 62 feet" — CONFIRMED-digest only). The caravanserai/han (R-INST-2 §16: Sultanhanı 44 × 58 m court; Koza Han 45.9 × 37.5 m, 50 + 54 cells, two-storey gallery) is the same parti — gate, court, cell ring, gallery, stables — at a scale the English inn never reached, and with the guest sleeping OVER his goods rather than in a multi-bed chamber.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None.
