## §4 · FAMILY C — the coaching inn and the post: Coaching inn (town L970 "Purpose-built for road travellers: stabling for relay horses, scheduled coach departures, meals served at fixed hours, and a passenger waiting room. Horses changed here, not just rested.") · Post relay station (town L1029 "Maintains a team of horses for rapid message relay… Requires coaching inn.")

*Provenance: sub-lane CD (killed 06:15:39Z after 61 tool calls incl. 12 browser acts; no report). Evidence: Brayshay 1991 on the West Country post-horse routes (LOCAL, `RINST2-MNO-brayshay-posthorse-1991.txt`, saved by R-INST-2 and re-read by CD), Gutmann on the Thurn-und-Taxis Posthalterei at Stollhofen (LOCAL, `RINST4-CD-stollhofen-posthalterei.txt`, Die Ortenau 81, pp. 297–310), de.wikipedia Kempten Posthalterei, the Science Museum mail coach record, the HE list entries of family B, the Southampton carriers' evidence in Brown 2007 (LOCAL), and the Smithsonian stagecoach page (browser).*

**(a) Analogue.** The coaching inn is NOT a new building type; it is family B's courtyard inn RE-CUT for the coach, and the evidence for the re-cut is direct: at the Star, Oxford, the medieval route to the stables (entry → courtyard → rear passage) "would be suitable enough for horses but would not admit vehicles of any large size. Consequently, in the late eighteenth century, with the development of larger stage-coaches, came the need for a larger, alternative entry, as a kind of by-pass, leading through the King's Head yard to the stables at the back"; "the original archway would then become simply the entrance hall of the inn, and finally the courtyard was roofed over"; "at another Oxford inn, the Angel in High Street, a similar by-pass was created" (LOCAL, Pantin 1958 p. 89). By the 19th century the Star had "stabling for 30 horses, coachhouses, lofts" on a plot 90 ft wide × c. 210 ft deep, and a boundary dispute noted that if the property were split "there would be no stable entrance and one would have to be made at some cost by sacrificing a good room" (LOCAL, Pantin p. 121) — the coach entry is the coaching inn's one NON-NEGOTIABLE cell. The George, Southwark, c. 1830 ran coaches to Maidstone "four times a day", Dover and Canterbury "twice a day", Brighton and Worthing "daily", and was "the depot for a number of goods wagons"; in 1855 "most of the east end of the yard was occupied by stabling" and a railway company had offices on the north side of the yard (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Survey of London 22, pp. 9–30](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/survey-london/vol22/pp9-30), fetched 2026-08-23); the Wikipedia page names the "Parliament Bar" as the former "waiting room for passengers on coaches" and the Middle Bar as the "Coffee Room" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: The George Inn, Southwark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_George_Inn,_Southwark), fetched 2026-08-23) — the catalog's "passenger waiting room" is a ground-floor front room of the street range (the former parlour), and "meals served at fixed hours" is the ordinary of the 17th century, not a room. Borough High Street's inns were "used in the 18th and 19th centuries as depots for carrier wagons and for passenger coaches to and from Kent, Surrey, Sussex and Hampshire" (SoL 22) — the coaching inn is the carriers' inn of R-INST-2 §16 with a timetable. **The post** is the other half of the catalog's pair and it is HOSTED in the inn from its beginning: Brayshay's West Country study (LOCAL, Brayshay 1991, *Trans. Devonshire Assoc.* 123) — post stages "customarily spaced ten or fifteen miles apart and on heavily used routes commercial livery stables were plentiful" (p. 79); the 1577 certificate counts Devon's 120 innholders, and "while it is unrealistic to suppose that all of the 120 inns and 40 taverns in Devon would have offered posting facilities, the majority probably did so … There were also 400 alehouses, but it seems likely that these were mostly rather modest establishments not offering horse-hire services" (p. 79); the private traveller paid 2–3d a mile and "would be obliged to hire two horses, the second one carrying the obligatory guide whose function was to show the way, blow the horn, and to return eventually with both horses to the home stage" (p. 80); royal postmasters "were responsible for keeping 'able and sufficient' post horses ready for travellers to hire — usually a minimum of six, including two for the post-boy's use", supplied the boy's wages, a leather bag "well-lined with cotton or baize", a horn, and "a 'well-bound book of ten or eleven quires of paper'" recording every hire and every letter's arrival time (p. 97); foot-posts took letters "from the 'post-room' at a principal stage to their residences in towns or villages nearby" (p. 81); the royal postmaster at Plymouth 1595–1611, Vincent Scoble, "was the owner of post horses hired by the borough" in the 1580s, his barns were rented as a hospital in 1588, "and it seems likely that he also had a large inn and a livery stable" (p. 96); Exeter, "lacking a royal post room in the borough" in 1585, had the mayor hire sixteen horses for Sir Philip Sidney "from local stables and … from 'strangers'" (p. 86); a postmaster was obliged to deliver letters within twenty miles of the stage town (p. 95). So the Elizabethan post relay is a POST-ROOM (a ledger, a bag, a horn) and SIX HORSES in an innholder's stable — the catalog's "Requires coaching inn" is historically RIGHT, and the stronger statement is that the post relay has NO BUILDING: it is a licence on a stable and a chamber. The continental form makes the same point in a different grammar: at Stollhofen (Baden) the first Thurn-und-Taxis post-station (1698) was lodged in the Gasthaus zum Schwarzen Adler — a former monastic Kellerhof — and the postmaster "was at the same time town and district clerk. So we find the Stadtschreiberei in the same house"; in 1748 postmaster Jacob Kramer received "a building plot at the 'former fortification' free of charge, to build a new post building there", on a NEW straight highway laid past the old lane because of "the goods wagons and country coaches constantly standing there"; the post's income ledger (1757) counts the post 300 Gulden, the Schultheiss office 100, the toll 150; and the administration's aim "to relieve travellers of the worry about food and lodging" produced "around the post stations several inns with large stables, in which up to 50 horses could be accommodated" (LOCAL, Gutmann, Stollhofen, pp. 298–306; quotations translated by the parent). The Kempten Posthalterei (1778) is "a two-storey building" whose "large entrance gateway" was "designed primarily for mail coach traffic" with a scroll gable over it (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [de.wikipedia: Thurn- und Taxis'sche Posthalterei (Kempten)](https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thurn-_und_Taxis%E2%80%99sche_Posthalterei_(Kempten)), fetched 2026-08-23, citing Dehio); Mengen's (1702) "housed a post station, the postmaster's residence, a guest house, and a coach house" (CONFIRMED-digest). The Roman **mansio** (family B (h)) is the ceiling and the origin of the relay: a colonnaded court with kitchen, well, latrine and sleeping apartments, a bath house beside it, at a road junction (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, HE 1006108). **Siting**: the stage towns on an arterial road at 10–15-mile intervals; the inn on the through-street or the bridgehead; the new-road relocation at Stollhofen (the post house moves to the road, and the road moves to the post house). **Live/work**: the postmaster is the innholder (Scoble) or the town clerk (Retzer), lodging in the inn; the post-boy in the stable loft (PLAUSIBLE). **Prosperity/wear**: floor = a post-room (ledger, bag, horn) + six horses in an inn's stable; rung 1 = the coach by-pass entry + coach house + 30 horses; rung 2 = the purpose-built Posthalterei with the coach gateway on the facade and a coach house; ceiling = the mail-coach inn with waiting room, coffee room, booking office and 50-horse stabling. Decline: the railway — the George's yard let to hop merchants and a railway office (1855), the Borough inns "bankrupt … by the 1880s" (LOCAL, kyl page); the stage-coach era in England is c. 1650–1840 and the mail coach 1784–c. 1850 (CONFIRMED-digest, Wikipedia Mail coach).

**(b) Measured.** Royal Mail coach by Waude and Co., London, 1815–1830, Science Museum object 1912-32: overall **2450 × 1800 × 3900 mm (approximate)** — height 2.45 m, width 1.8 m, length 3.9 m (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Science Museum Group: Royal Mail Coach](https://collection.sciencemuseumgroup.org.uk/objects/co25448/royal-mail-coach-mail-coach), fetched 2026-08-23). Stage spacing 10–15 miles; post horses ≥ 6 per royal stage; 2–3d/mile + 4d guide; Exeter 1585 hire of 16 horses (LOCAL, Brayshay). Stollhofen: up to 50 horses per posting inn (LOCAL, Gutmann p. 306); Star Inn 30 horses (19th c., LOCAL Pantin). Stage-coach journey Stollhofen: 3½ days in summer, 4 in winter on its route (LOCAL, Gutmann p. 299). Letocetum first mansio court c. 62 × 62 ft / 18.9 m (CONFIRMED-digest only). NOT FOUND: the clear width or height of any coaching-inn archway (searched: "stagecoach dimensions … inn archway height", "'mail coach' OR 'stage coach' museum collection overall height mm"; the Smithsonian page gives only a model's dimensions — browser-read); the Kempten gateway's dimensions (de.wikipedia: "no measurements"); the Stollhofen plan (the Gutmann article has no plan). The archway bucket is DERIVED from the coach: clear width ≥ 1.8 m + margin, clear height ≥ 2.45 m + the outside passengers/luggage, i.e. **≥ 2.4 m × ≥ 3.0 m** as a floor (PLAUSIBLE-by-derivation; P1c should measure three surviving coaching arches).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) The catalog's "Purpose-built" is the contested word: the English coaching inn is almost always a REBUILT or RE-CUT courtyard inn (Pantin's by-pass; the George's 1677 rebuild "probably on the old plan"); the purpose-built form is the continental Posthalterei (1702, 1748, 1778, 1807) and the English coaching inn of the 1780s–1830s (PLAUSIBLE; no fetched English example was purpose-built). (ii) "Post relay station … Requires coaching inn" at TOWN tier is right for 1580–1700 but the dependency inverts in the Thurn-und-Taxis register, where the post-house OWNS the inn (the Posthalterei with Gasthof and stables under one roof). (iii) "Rapid message relay" — the royal standing posts were laid and DISCHARGED repeatedly (the West Country route funded 1595, discharged 1611, reinstated 1620 — LOCAL, Brayshay pp. 94–95): the post relay is an EVENT-gated institution, not a standing one; when the Treasury withdrew, "provincial post rooms did not close down" but ran commercially (p. 95) — the building never existed, so nothing closed. (iv) The negation "the coaching inn was a new building type" finds its counter in the Star and the Angel; the negation "the post had its own building" finds NO English example before the 18th-century post office and one continental counter (Stollhofen 1748 — and even that was "Posthalterei und Gasthof"). (v) The Smithsonian stagecoach (1818, American) and the mail coach (English, 1815–30) are post-register; the medieval carrier's wagon is the right vehicle for the town tier and no fetched source measured one — the HORSE-passage bucket (family B) stands for every era before c. 1650.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** Coaching inn (town L970): family B's courtyard with `CROSS_PASSAGE{ENTRY, COACH}` — the re-cut or by-pass joint, `mintedBy: STAGE_COACH (≥ c. 1650; 1780s for the mail coach)`, clear bucket ≥ 2.4 m wide × ≥ 3.0 m high (derived), and the OLD entry demoted to `LOBBY{ENTRANCE_HALL}` ("simply the entrance hall of the inn") — a `killedBy` on a passage that becomes a lobby without moving, the same mechanism as DWR1A's stack-into-passage; `EXTERIOR_WALK{YARD}` that may be ROOFED late (the Star's "courtyard was roofed over" — a yard that becomes a hall: `enclosure: OPEN → ROOFED`); `GALLERY{INN_GALLERY}` as family B; a `THROUGH_LANE` where the by-pass runs through a neighbour's yard (the Star's route "through the King's Head yard" — `joint.target: OTHER_BUILDING`, CIRC addendum §4.3 item 1); `STAIR_HALL{DOGLEG}` (George c. 1730). Post relay (town L1029): inherits the host inn's circulation entirely (CIRC addendum §4.3 item 9); its own joints are the stable door and the post-room door off the yard. Posthalterei (continental): the facade gateway as `CROSS_PASSAGE{ENTRY, COACH}` on the STREET elevation (an exterior consequence — the scroll gable over the gate), `THROUGH_ROOM` in the two-storey house, the coach house as an open-fronted cell (`OPEN_AT_ONE_END` — R-INST-2 gap 8). Widths: NONE measured (ledger); buckets above.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** `STORE{COACH_HOUSE}` (Mengen 1702; the Star's "coachhouses" — a vehicle store, wide-doored, at grade, off the yard; sizeBucket LARGE; lightReq ANY); `STORE{HAYLOFT}` `REQUIRED_ABOVE(stable)`; `STORE{HARNESS/TACK}` (fixture in the ostler's chamber); `CLOSET{POST_ROOM}` = the postmaster's ledger room ("a well-bound book of ten or eleven quires", the bag, the horn — fixtures in a small chamber; `GUARDIAN_ADJACENT` to the postmaster's lodging); `CLOSET{BOOKING/COUNTING}` (the coaching office — the George's railway office inherited the position); `STORE{WAREHOUSE}` for the carriers' goods (family B; the George "depot for goods wagons"); the Stollhofen Kellerhof's monastic cellar as the first post-house's `CELLAR`. Prohibition: none recorded; PLAUSIBLE `REQUIRED_FAR(hay, hearth)`.

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: COACHING_INN` = family B `COURTYARD_*` + `{entry: COACH, coach_house, stable{≥ 30 horses}, waiting_room(front), booking_office, tap_house, coffee_room}` — NOT a new parti, a RE-CUT with three added cells; `parti: POSTHALTEREI` (continental, ≥ 1700) = a two-storey block with the coach gateway on the facade + Gasthof + coach house + postmaster's residence under one roof; `parti: NO_BUILDING` for the post relay (a `post_room` closet + `horses{≥ 6}` in the host inn's stable). `functions[]`: coaching inn floor = family B floor + `coach_entry` + `coach_house` + `relay_stable`; rungs = + `waiting_room` → + `booking_office` → + `coffee_room` → + `second_yard` → + `by-pass entry through a neighbour`; ceiling = 50-horse Posthalterei. `fixtures[]`: `LEDGER_BOOK`, `POST_BAG`, `POST_HORN`, `TIMETABLE_BOARD` (the c. 1830 card), `MOUNTING_BLOCK` (PLAUSIBLE), `COACH{1.8 × 2.45 × 3.9 m}` as a yard fixture, `HORSE_TROUGH`. Structural: `gatePassage{COACH}`, `wideDoor` (coach house), `heavyFloor` (hayloft). Licensing: coaching inn tier ≥ town, era ≥ c. 1650 (England) / ≥ c. 1700 (Posthalterei), `onRoute` mandatory, prosperity ≥ middling; post relay tier ≥ town, era 1516 (Tuke) / 1579–1595 (West Country stages) onward, `onRoute` + `stageSpacing 10–15 mi`, and an EVENT gate (royal engagement/discharge). VERDICT: Coaching inn **BUILDING** (a re-cut family-B courtyard; HOSTED on an existing inn is the common English case — `upgradeOf: INN`); Post relay station **NO_BUILDING / HOSTED** (host: the coaching inn or any innholder's stable; the catalog's dependency is right). HOME: `vice` template for the inn; NO TYPED HOME for coach house, waiting room, booking office, post room (four cells).

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) A parti that is an UPGRADE of an existing building (`upgradeOf: INN`, with `mintedBy: STAGE_COACH` on the new entry) — law 5's renovation narrative at institution scale: the coaching inn should appear in a settlement as "the Star, re-cut 1780" rather than as a fresh lot. (2) The passage → lobby demotion and the yard → roofed-hall promotion are two `enclosure`/`class` transitions on existing cells — the CIRC addendum's `mintedBy/killedBy` needs a `reclassedTo`. (3) The post relay is the cleanest NO_BUILDING in the tranche that is nonetheless a LICENCE with fixtures and a headcount (six horses) — the `hostedIn` relation needs a `capacity` field the host's stable must satisfy (the three-clamp ceiling, law 2, applied to an animal count). (4) A through-route across a NEIGHBOUR's yard (`joint.target: OTHER_BUILDING`) at the Star confirms the CIRC addendum's item 1 in the hospitality register. (5) Road-relocation as a siting event (Stollhofen 1748): the post-house moves the road — a map-program seam (the DW grammar owns the gate; the fabric owns the road).

**(h) Continental vs English.** England's post is a ROOM in an inn and a ledger; the Thurn-und-Taxis post (1490s onward; the Stollhofen station from 1698) is a BUILDING TYPE by the 18th century, the Posthalterei, with the coach gateway as its facade — and around it "several inns with large stables" for up to 50 horses (LOCAL, Gutmann). The French relais de poste was NOT researched (CD's searches were German-language and the budget ran out; the Swiss HLS page 403'd) — ledgered. The Roman cursus publicus mansio (Letocetum) is the ceiling: a state hostel with its own bath house. The Ottoman menzilhane and the Persian chapar-khaneh are the setting-agnostic cousins (PLAUSIBLE, not researched); the han of R-INST-2 §16 already carries the caravan grade.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None (a teleport-circle house would be R-INST-5's; the relay as a magical-message station is the same post-room with a different fixture).

## §5 · FAMILY D — performers and hiring (hosted functions): Village musician (village L755 "A resident singer, fiddler, or piper. Plays at festivals and weddings…") · Traveling performers (town L1464 "Bards, jugglers, acrobats visit during fairs.") · Hireling hall (town L1387 "Job board for torchbearers (1 GP/session), porters (5 GP/session).")

*Provenance: sub-lane CD (the SHALT Bull Inn page; Wikipedia Minstrels' gallery; Wikipedia Hiring and mop fairs; de.wikipedia Herbergsmuseum; the Hampshire Archives Trust and Shakespeare-blog REED pages; five search digests on inn-yard playhouses, REED venues, statute sessions, Gesellenherberge; the Cambridge excerpt PDF failed). Brown 2007 (LOCAL) supplies the Southampton galleried-courtyard and civic-venue evidence.*

**(a) Analogue.** All three rows are NO_BUILDING or HOSTED in the English register and the evidence says WHERE they are hosted. **Players**: the London inn-yards — "six London inns were significant sites for drama during the second half of the sixteenth century": the Bull (Bishopsgate, 1575), the Bell (Gracechurch Street, 1576), the Boar's Head (Whitechapel, 1557), the Saracen's Head (Islington, 1557), the Bel Savage (Ludgate Hill, 1579), the Cross Keys (Gracechurch Street, 1579); in 1584 the City licensed four (Bel Savage, Bull, Bell, Cross Keys); "it was the Cross Keys and the Bell that were used as indoor venues, while the Bel Savage and the Bull put on plays outside in their coaching courtyards" (CONFIRMED-digest, 2026-08-23, of the SHALT pages and Wikipedia "Inn-yard theatre"); the Bull had plays "by 1578", its yard is described as a "coaching courtyard", Gosson's spectators "carry their eye through every gallery", and "the Lord Mayor managed to get the Privy Council to ban all use of the city inns for playing in 1594" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [SHALT: Bull Inn 1578–94](https://shalt.dmu.ac.uk/locations/bull-inn-1578-94/indepth.html), fetched 2026-08-23 — no dimensions given). The aggregator claim that inn-yards held "up to 500", with "the balconies … as the upper and lower circles, the rooms of the Inn … private 'boxes' and the yard itself … the 'pit'" is CONFIRMED-digest from an unreferenced teaching site and is carried as PLAUSIBLE. The provincial pattern is the REED finding: "the venue for plays shifted from guildhalls to local inns, and consequently no notice of these performances appeared in the town records"; when town fathers refused the guildhall, a company "set up stage in a spacious second-story room at the White Hart Inn" (CONFIRMED-digest of the Cambridge excerpt, which itself timed out); Stratford's chamberlain paid 19s 4d "for foure companyes of players" in summer 1597 at the Guild Hall (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [The Shakespeare blog](http://theshakespeareblog.com/2012/01/how-chances-it-they-travel-players-in-stratford-upon-avon/), fetched 2026-08-23); Southampton hosted "eight troupes" between October 1593 and September 1594, a 1620 complaint records furniture damage by "Stage players or Interlude players" in "the town hall at Southampton's Bargate", and a Winchester church hosted players on a Sunday in 1617 with arrests (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Hampshire Archives Trust: Shakespeare and Traveling Players](https://hampshirearchivestrust.co.uk/stories/blog/shakespeare-and-traveling-players), fetched 2026-08-23, citing SCA SC6/1/37 f. 16v); Brown's thesis notes Southampton's public houses "enabled players" by providing "outdoor playing spaces (especially in the form of galleried courtyards)" (LOCAL, Brown p. 137); Gloucester's New Inn "was used as a performance venue in the 16th century by traveling theater companies" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, gloucestershirepubs — unreferenced) and Wikipedia's "It is thought that William Shakespeare may have performed at the Inn" is speculation the page itself hedges. So the hosts, in order of record, are: the guildhall/town hall (civic, R-INST-1), the inn's galleried yard (open-air: family B's `COURTYARD_GALLERY_RING` is a playhouse when a scaffold stage is set against one range — the playhouse parti of family H is this yard made permanent), the inn's great chamber (indoor), the church (rare, punished). **Musicians**: the minstrels' gallery "over the screens passage" is the hall's music position (CONFIRMED-digest: "there was usually a minstrels' gallery above the screens passage"; Berkeley Castle's 14th-century hall, Durham Castle, Desmond Hall's restored 15th-century oak gallery — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Minstrels' gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minstrels%27_gallery), fetched 2026-08-23, which flags its own missing citations and offers "no scholarly critique of the concept's accuracy"); the CD negation search for the "minstrels' gallery myth" (Thompson/Woolgar/Vernacular Architecture) returned NOTHING on either side — a NULL: the lane cannot say whether the term is antiquarian. The village musician of the catalog "plays at festivals and weddings" — i.e. in the church house, the churchyard, the hall, the alehouse and the market ground (R-INST-3 holds the church house; the waits' gallery of a town hall is R-INST-1's). **Hiring**: hiring fairs "trace back to Edward III's Statute of Labourers (1351)" and were formalised by "the Statute of Apprentices (1563)" which "designated specific dates when shire constables would announce wage rates and employment terms"; the Statute's final clause (no. 40) set up "Statute Sessions, also known as Petty Sessions, Hiring Sessions and Chief Constable Sessions"; they "took place in market towns" (Beverley, York, Hull, Bridlington, Banbury, Warwick, Tewkesbury); labourers "displayed trade-specific items: Shepherds held a crook or tuft of wool, cowmen brought wisps of straw, dairymaids carried a milking stool or pail and housemaids held brooms or mops"; the hire was sealed with a "fasten-penny" of a shilling and a ribbon (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Hiring and mop fairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiring_and_mop_fairs), fetched 2026-08-23; CONFIRMED-digest, Kussmaul's appendix "Statute Sessions and hiring fairs in England, sixteenth to nineteenth centuries"). The English labour exchange is therefore the MARKET GROUND on a named day (R-INST-2 §2's exterior zoned parti) with the inn as its office — no hall. The continental counter is the **Gesellenherberge**: journeymen's wandering was "already widespread in the late Middle Ages" and "became mandatory in the 16th century" (three years and a day), and the guild hostel where a journeyman lodged and was placed (the Umschau) had "an inner courtyard, stables, laundry room, kitchen, lodging rooms and dining rooms" — the one museum example, Blankenburg (Harz), is a half-timbered house of 1684 used as a hostel only 1884–1916, with Innenhof, Stallung, Waschküche, Küche, Logierzimmer and Restaurationszimmer (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [de.wikipedia: Herbergsmuseum](https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbergsmuseum), fetched 2026-08-23; CONFIRMED-digest, DHM / hwk-bls). The catalog's "Hireling hall" (torchbearers, porters — an adventuring-shelf entry) is therefore a Gesellenherberge in the continental register and a statute-fair-plus-inn in the English; the FACET_INFERENCE regex types it `civic` on the word "hall" (§0 recon) — wrong in both registers.

**(b) Measured.** None for any hosted function (the hosts carry the figures: family B's yards and galleries; R-INST-1's halls). Capacities: "up to 500" (inn-yard; PLAUSIBLE); "eight troupes" per year at Southampton 1593–94 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED); 19s 4d for four companies at Stratford 1597 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). NOT FOUND: any stated stage size in an inn-yard; the Blankenburg bed count; the Bargate hall's size.

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) The inn-yard-as-playhouse is the textbook story and the SHALT page is careful: two of the four licensed inns played INDOORS — the hosted play is a great-chamber function as often as a yard function. (ii) The 1594 ban shows the hosted function can be KILLED by ordinance while the host survives — a `killedBy` on a hosted function, not on a cell. (iii) The "minstrels' gallery" is unverified as a medieval term; the negation search was null; the grammar should call the cell `GALLERY_OVER{SCREENS}` and let the music be a fixture-use, never a room name. (iv) The catalog's "Hireling hall" presumes a building that the English register never built; the Gesellenherberge is an INN with a placement function (HOSTED on family B), and Blankenburg's 1684 house was a hostel only from 1884 — a 17th-century HOUSE re-used, not a purpose-built hall. (v) Negation result for "no village musician": not searched (the row is NO_BUILDING by its own text; nothing to refute).

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** All inherited from the host (CIRC addendum §4.3 item 9). Players in an inn yard: `EXTERIOR_WALK{YARD}` as the pit, `GALLERY{INN_GALLERY}` as the circles, chambers as boxes, the `ENTRY` as the single controlled door (the gatherer's position — family H); players indoors: the hall/great chamber's `THROUGH_ROOM` with its screens. Musician: `GALLERY{GALLERY_OVER}` above the screens in a hall (where the hall grade licenses it), else the floor. Hiring: `EXTERIOR_WALK{GROUND}` (the market place on statute day) + the inn's common room; Gesellenherberge: family B's court + `THROUGH_ROOM` lodging rooms + a `Restaurationszimmer` (the Gaststube).

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** None owned; the players' `STORE{PROPERTY}` travels in a wagon and is a fixture on the host yard; the Gesellenherberge's `Waschküche` (laundry/scullery class) and `Stallung` are the host inn's.

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: NO_BUILDING` for all three rows, with `hostedIn` targets: Village musician → `{CHURCH_HOUSE (R-INST-3) | HALL{GALLERY_OVER} | ALEHOUSE (A) | MARKET_GROUND}` and a `seasonal` flag (festivals, weddings); Traveling performers → `{GUILDHALL (R-INST-1) | INN{YARD | GREAT_CHAMBER} (B) | CHURCH (rare)}` with `seasonal: FAIR` and a `killedBy: ORDINANCE` (1594); Hireling hall → English `{MARKET_GROUND on STATUTE_DAY + INN common room}`; continental `GESELLENHERBERGE = INN(B) + placement function` (a FIXTURE set: the Umschau board/book, the Herbergsvater's lodging). `fixtures[]`: `SCAFFOLD_STAGE` (set against a range), `GATHERER_BOX` (at the entry), `PROPERTY_WAGON`, `MUSIC_STAND/BENCH` in the gallery over, `HIRING_TOKENS` (crook, straw, pail, mop), `FASTEN_PENNY`, `WAGE_PROCLAMATION` (a document fixture on the market cross), `UMSCHAU_BOOK`. Licensing: tier ≥ village (musician), ≥ town (performers, hiring); era: players 1557–1642 at inns (1594 ban in London), hiring 1351/1563 onward, Gesellenherberge ≥ 16th c. continental; culture: the hiring fair is English/Irish, the Herberge German. VERDICT: all three **NO_BUILDING / HOSTED** at every tier (host named above); the catalog's "Hireling hall" text should lose the word "hall". HOME: NO TYPED HOME (ODQ §462); the FACET_INFERENCE `civic` for "Hireling hall" is an error flagged in §Σ.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) A hosted function needs a `seasonal`/`calendar` attribute (fair days, statute day, festival) — the host cell is the same; the fixture set appears on a date (R-INST-2 gap 11's `seasonal` extended to HOSTED functions). (2) A hosted function can be KILLED by ordinance independently of its host (1594) — `killedBy` must attach to the `hostedIn` relation, not only to cells. (3) The inn yard is the playhouse's ancestor: family H's parti must be derivable from family B's `COURTYARD_GALLERY_RING` + a fixed stage — the grammar should express the playhouse as a SPECIALISATION of the galleried court, which is also what the Fortune contract's "galleries" say (§1.4). (4) The catalog row "Hireling hall" should be re-typed (owner data note) or its `civic` inference corrected.

**(h) Continental vs English.** England hires on the market ground by statute and plays in inn yards and guildhalls; the German-speaking lands lodge the wandering journeyman in a guild Herberge that places him (a hosted office in an inn), and the Italian commedia troupe plays in the palazzo courtyard and the piazza (PLAUSIBLE, not fetched). The Spanish corral de comedias (a tenement courtyard made a permanent playhouse, 1570s) is the exact continental twin of the inn-yard playhouse and belongs to family H's ledger (not fetched this lane).

**(i) Fantasy note.** None ("Hireling hall" is an adventuring-shelf fiction; its historical floor is the Herberge/statute fair above; the "job board" is a fixture the inn's common room can carry without a building).

## §6 · FAMILY E — the bathhouse: Public bathhouse (town L978 "Communal bathing facility with heated water. In some cultures a social hub; in others suspect as a venue for vice…")

*Provenance: sub-lane EFG (killed 06:12:56Z after 50 tool calls; no report). Evidence: the Society of Antiquaries of Scotland open-access Bearsden excavation chapter (LOCAL, `RINST4-EFG-bearsden-excavations.txt`, Breeze & Banks, 157 KB, fetched by curl from books.socantscot.org — the one measured bath-house in the tranche), the mittelalter-lexikon Badhaus page, altbasel.ch Badstuben dossier, the Fränkisches Freilandmuseum's Wendelstein Badhaus page, the Altstadtfreunde Nürnberg Irrerstraße 1 page, Wikipedia El Bañuelo and Great Hammam of Pristina, the Chesters bath page, and seven search digests; the Heidelberg Arnold paper and the itv-grabungen article were gated (Anubis challenge / 404).*

**(a) Analogue.** The catalog's one row straddles THREE building types that share a programme (undress → warm → hot/sweat → wash → rest) and differ in the heat engine. (1) **The medieval Badstube** (German/Swiss/Central European; the English "stew"): "Vorraum (Diele), Heiz- und Auskleideraum und Badstube parterre, die Wohnung des Badstübners im Obergeschoss" — an entrance hall, a combined stoking-and-undressing room and the bath chamber on the ground floor, the bath-keeper's dwelling above; "in der Nähe von Fließgewässern oder ergiebiger Schöpf- oder Laufbrunnen angelegt" (sited by running water or a productive well/conduit); wood consumption "einige Hundert Festmeter p.a."; both steam bathing and wooden tubs (badevaz), the water dosed with salt, resin, herbs or sulphur; the Bader performed "Rasieren, Kopfwäsche, Massieren, Frottieren, Aderlassen, Schröpfen, Klistieren und kleine chirurgische Eingriffe"; the earliest Central-European documentary bathhouse is Fulda c. 1150; by the late 15th century Ulm had 168 bathhouses (as printed — see (c)), Vienna 21, Frankfurt 15; the culture "ended in the 16th century" under the English Sweat and syphilis ("1495 ins Teutschland") and rising wood prices (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [mittelalter-lexikon: Badhaus](https://www.mittelalter-lexikon.de/wiki/Badhaus), fetched 2026-08-23). The search digest of the same sources adds the plan type — "rechteckige oder trapezoide Grundriss mit dickem Außenmauerwerk" — and the heat engine: "Der Kessel für das warme Badewasser befindet sich in einem Raum, der vom benachbarten Heizraum unterirdisch befeuert wird. Auf einer ebenfalls mit einem gemauerten Bogen überwölbten Heizkammer liegen größere Kiesel. Sind diese erst heiß genug, werden sie mit Wasser übergossen" — i.e. a vaulted stoking chamber fired from the next room, carrying the copper and a bed of stones for steam; the sweat bath has "hölzerne treppenartige Gestelle zum Sitzen oder Liegen" (stepped wooden benches) (CONFIRMED-digest, 2026-08-23, of itv-grabungen / mittelalter-lexikon / Arnold). The one standing example: the **Badhaus from Wendelstein** (re-erected at the Fränkisches Freilandmuseum), dendro-dated **1450**, replacing a house burnt in the First Margrave War 1449/50, "massivem Erdgeschoss und Fachwerkobergeschoss" with an unusually tall ground floor of **4 m**; the ground floor held "Badstube mit Bade- und Schwitzbereich, die Schürkammer, die Zisterne und die Umkleideräume" (bath room with bathing and sweating zones, the stoking chamber, the CISTERN, the changing rooms) and a "Schwitzofen" of stones; the timber upper storey and part-converted roof held "insgesamt vier Wohnungen" for the barber-surgeon's family and tenants (the "Beständner" of the museum's title); water from the Schwarzach; bathing ceased 1814, the barber-surgeon's right lapsed 1849 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Fränkisches Freilandmuseum: Badhaus mit Beständnerwohnungen aus Wendelstein](https://freilandmuseum.de/besuchen/orientieren-im-museum/baugruppe-mittelalter/badhaus-mit-bestaendnerwohnungen-aus-wendelstein), fetched 2026-08-23). The excavated example: **Irrerstraße 1, Nuremberg** — first recorded 1326 as the "Irrerbad", the standing bathhouse built 1510, rebuilt 1691, bathing ended 1695; excavation found the bath oven's remains on the ground floor, "Umrisse eines Ofens mit Aufgusssteinen, einer heutigen Sauna vergleichbar", and "unter dem Fußboden … Reste einer Heizanlage ähnlich den Bädern im alten Rom" (an underfloor heating system), above an iron-smelting reheating furnace and a fully cellared 12th-century house burnt down (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Altstadtfreunde Nürnberg: Irrerstraße 1](https://www.altstadtfreunde-nuernberg.de/de/projekte/alle-projekte/haeuser/irrerstrasse-1.html), fetched 2026-08-23). Basel's dossier gives the fittings and the run of closures: "Zubern und Becken" (tubs, several persons to a tub), the steam bath's "Ofenbank" where guests reclined while water was poured on hot stones, the "Badequaste oder Badewedel" whisk — a wedel hung outside "signaled the bath was ready"; water from springs round Andreasplatz, an internal spring at one bath with the overflow piped to a public fountain, the Riehenteich canal in Kleinbasel; the Bader's trade: hair-cutting, tooth-drawing, bloodletting, cupping (a "Schröpfstube" recorded 1437), wound care, herbal baths; eleven named Badstuben with dates from 1287, a men's bath 1404–1484, a women's bath converted to a TANNERY by 1674, one bath running 1362–1814 (452 years); 1505 the sick were banned; syphilis from the late 15th century and the Reformation's ban on mixed nude bathing ended "the golden days" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [altbasel.ch: Badstuben im mittelalterlichen Basel](https://altbasel.ch/dossier/badstuben_basel.html), fetched 2026-08-23). So the Badstube is a TWO-STOREY HOUSE with a process ground floor and a dwelling over — live/work is the rule (the Bader's family upstairs; Wendelstein's four flats) — and its process cells are: a water source (cistern/spring/conduit), a stoking chamber with a copper and a stone bed, a tub room, a sweat room with stepped benches, an undressing room, an anteroom. (2) **The English stew**: the Bankside "stews" began "as public bathhouses licensed by the Bishop of Winchester in the twelfth century" and "by the Tudor period they had become houses of ill repute" (CONFIRMED-digest — a Tudor-history site; the etymology from 'estuwes'/stove is the digest's); Henry VIII closed the Southwark stews by proclamation on 13 April 1546 (CONFIRMED-digest; the 1504 closure by Henry VII "for fear of syphilis" is the same digest) — in England the bathhouse and the brothel are ONE building by the 15th century (family F), and the public bath vanished until "the Royal Bagnio" of 1679 off Newgate Street, "built by Turkish merchants in an eastern style with a cupola roof over the main bath hall", with "sweating, rubbing, shaving, cupping and bathing", separate days for women and men, and an associated coffee house, used until demolition in 1876 (CONFIRMED-digest of [exploring-london.com: Lost London – The Royal Bagnio](https://exploring-london.com/2020/03/20/lost-london-the-royal-bagnio/)). The catalog's "In some cultures a social hub; in others suspect as a venue for vice" is exactly this split, and its DATE is the 1490s–1560s: before syphilis the Badstube is the social hub; after it the stew is the vice. (3) **The hammam** (the sequence-of-rooms type the catalog's "heated water" undersells): the Ottoman bath "consist[s] of soğukluk, ılıklık, sıcaklık, and külhan" — the camekan (reception/dressing hall with a central fountain and platforms or cubicles), the soğukluk (a warm transition room), the hararet/sıcaklık (a domed, often octagonal or square hot room "heated by hot, dry air through ducts in walls and floor" with the göbek taşı marble platform at its centre), and behind the hot room's wall the külhan with "a water tank, a furnace, and storage" (CONFIRMED-digest, 2026-08-23); the Great Hammam of Pristina (second half of the 15th century, Mehmed II) is a DOUBLE hammam (men's and women's halves) of "approximately 800 square meters", "an entrance hall, a middle warm area, and a main warm area", hypocausts under marble floors, a hot room of "16 domes, each containing 15 holes" for light and heat retention, stone walls with brick domes and humidity-resistant "horasan" mortar (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Great Hammam of Pristina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Hammam_of_Pristina), fetched 2026-08-23); the Andalusi **El Bañuelo**, Granada (11th or 12th century) runs reception/relaxation → changing → cold room (bayt al-bārid) → warm room (bayt al-wasṭānī, the largest, "a large central vault or dome … surrounded on three sides by smaller barrel vault sections") → hot room (bayt al-sakhūn) → service area with "the furnace and boiler" whose hot air and smoke ran "through pipes and conduits under the floors of the hot and warm rooms"; star- and octagon-shaped skylights light the vaults and vent steam; after 1492 it served the moriscos, then became "a public laundry facility and later as a dwelling" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: El Bañuelo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Ba%C3%B1uelo), fetched 2026-08-23). (4) **The Roman ceiling and origin**: the Bearsden fort bath-house (Antonine Wall, after AD 142) is the tranche's one room-by-room MEASURED bath: "cruciform in plan. The main axis was 28m long externally, and the width varied from 4.5m at the west end to 5m at the east (minus the hot bath). The main spine fell into three roughly equal-sized parts: the changing room, cold room and heated range. The hot dry room and the cold bath were placed approximately in the centre of the building, to each side of the cold room"; the changing room (apodyterium) "was of timber throughout. It measured 9m or 10m by 4m"; the cold room (frigidarium) "7m × 4.5m", timber at the west, stone at the east, flagged; the apsidal cold bath "3m × 3m" with a plug-hole drain; the first warm room (tepidarium) "3.5m × 2.5m" with a semicircular niche; the second warm room "3.5m × 3.1m" with three flues in its north wall and wall-jacketing; the hot room (caldarium) "3.5m × 3.1m", the only room with its hypocaust pillars burnt; the hot bath (alveus) "2.5m by 1.25m" on 750 mm pillars over a floor of flags and opus signinum, its south wall 1.5 m thick and buttressed; the furnace (praefurnium) "a channel, 550mm wide and 4m long, leading towards the centre of the hot room", with a stone-lined furnace chamber outside the east wall and "wide cheeks … to support the boiler heating the water for the hot bath"; the hot dry room (laconicum/sudatorium) "almost square, measuring internally 4.75m × 4.25m", walls 650–680 mm, floor on dwarf walls 580 mm high in two rows; hypocaust pillars 540 mm (warm) / 620–700 mm (hot); "all the floors sloped a little to the east" to shed water; the main entrance from the west by a gravel path 700 mm wide; the latrine "5m by about 4.4m externally" beside it; a coin of Antoninus Pius (154–5) in the hot-bath rubble (LOCAL, Breeze & Banks, *Bearsden: A Roman Fort on the Antonine Wall*, ch. 3.3, pp. 47–60 of the PDF). Chesters' sequence (apodyterium with "seven niches in the end wall" → tepidarium → caldarium/sudatorium → frigidarium; the caldarium "about 40 degrees Celsius"; "two bronze boilers") is the same programme without figures (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [odysseyadventures: Chesters Bath House](https://www.odysseyadventures.ca/articles/hadrian-wall/chesters_baths.htm), fetched 2026-08-23). **Structural imperatives** (all four types): WATER (spring/cistern/conduit/river — the Badstube is sited by it; the hammam has a tank; Bearsden has drains under every floor), FIRE (a furnace chamber OUTSIDE the bathing rooms with a flue under them: praefurnium / Schürkammer / külhan — the fuel store beside it with "no internal door" to the hot room is the PROHIBITION the brief asked for, and the evidence is the Bearsden furnace chamber "butting up to the east side of the cheeks" outside the wall), HEAVY WET FLOORS (flags on pillars; opus signinum), STEAM (vaults and domes with holes — Pristina's 16 domes × 15 holes; the Bañuelo's star skylights — or a tall 4 m ground storey at Wendelstein), SMELL/MORALS (the 1505 sick ban; the 1546 closure). **Siting**: by water, in the town (Basel's eleven are on named streets; Irrerstraße; the Bankside on the river); the hammam beside the mosque in a külliye (digest). **Prosperity/wear**: floor = one heated Stube with a tub and a stove (the bath as a ROOM of an inn or a barber's house — HOSTED; the Badstube's 16th-century afterlife); rung 1 = the two-storey Badstube with Diele, Heiz-/Auskleideraum, Badstube, Schwitzbad, cistern, the Bader above; rung 2 = separate men's and women's baths (Basel 1404; Pristina's double hammam), the Schröpfstube; ceiling = the hammam sequence with domes, or the Roman range with cold bath + hot bath + laconicum + latrine. Decline sheds the sweat bath first (fuel — "rising wood prices"), then the public function (sick banned, mixed bathing banned, syphilis), and the building becomes a tannery (Basel 1674), a laundry then a dwelling (Bañuelo), a brothel (Bankside), or keeps only the barber-surgeon (Wendelstein to 1849).

**(b) Measured.** Bearsden (LOCAL, Breeze & Banks): overall 28 m × 4.5–5 m; apodyterium 9–10 × 4 m; frigidarium 7 × 4.5 m; cold bath 3 × 3 m; tepidarium I 3.5 × 2.5 m; tepidarium II 3.5 × 3.1 m; caldarium 3.5 × 3.1 m; alveus 2.5 × 1.25 m; laconicum 4.75 × 4.25 m; praefurnium channel 0.55 × 4 m (+1.2 m beyond the wall); pillars 0.54–0.75 m; walls 0.55–0.77 m (laconicum 0.65–0.68 m; alveus south wall 1.5 m); dwarf walls 0.58 m; flues 100–110 × 150 mm; path 0.7 m; latrine 5 × 4.4 m ext. Wendelstein 1450: ground storey 4 m tall; four flats above (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). Pristina: c. 800 m², 16 hot-room domes (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). Counts: Ulm 168 (as printed; see (c)), Vienna 21, Frankfurt 15 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, mittelalter-lexikon); Basel eleven named (altbasel). Fuel: "einige Hundert Festmeter" of wood a year (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). NOT FOUND: any measured Badstube plan (the Nuremberg excavation page gives no dimensions; the Heidelberg Arnold PDF is behind an Anubis challenge; the search "Badstube Ausgrabung Grundriss Maße" returned bathroom-renovation adverts — a null); any hammam room dimension (searched Bursa/Skopje/Sarajevo — null); the Bañuelo's room sizes (Wikipedia: "no specific room dimensions"). The medieval bath-ROOM bucket must therefore be DERIVED from Bearsden's 3.5 × 2.5–3.1 m heated cells and the Wendelstein 4 m storey — flagged PLAUSIBLE-by-analogy in (f).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) The negation "the bathhouse was suppressed / closed" is CONFIRMED for England (1546) and for the German towns (Nuremberg's last Frauenhaus 1562; Basel's baths dwindle after 1505; Irrerstraße bathing ended 1695; Wendelstein 1814) — but with two counters: one Basel bath ran 452 years to 1814, and the bath returned to London as the Turkish bagnio in 1679. The right model is a CULTURE × ERA gate with a long tail, not a hard stop. (ii) The mittelalter-lexikon's "Ulm 168 bathhouses" is implausible for a town of Ulm's size (PLAUSIBLE: c. 10–20,000 people) against Vienna's 21 and Frankfurt's 15 — it is probably a misprint or a count of something else (bath-DAYS, or baths in the Ulm territory); the parent declines to relay it as a figure (number audit: a ratio of one bath per ~100 inhabitants is an order of magnitude off Vienna's). (iii) The stew/brothel identity is English; in Nuremberg the Frauenhaus was REQUIRED to give the women a bath weekly "in seinem haus" (family F) — the bath is a cell of the brothel there, not the brothel a use of the bath. (iv) The Roman bath is a GARRISON building at Bearsden (a fort's bath outside the rampart) — a military register, not a civic one; the civic thermae (Bath, Caracalla) were NOT fetched (the Gutenberg Davis text on Bath appeared in a digest only). (v) The Bañuelo's hypocaust "similar to the Roman" is an analogy the page itself makes; the Islamic bath's heat engine is a boiler-and-duct system, not pilae.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** The bath is the tranche's purest **sequenced THROUGH_ROOM enfilade with a thermal gradient**: Badstube — `LOBBY{Diele}` → `Heiz-/Auskleideraum` → `Badstube` (tub) ↔ `Schwitzstube` (a dead-end hot cell); Roman — `apodyterium` → `frigidarium` (the hub: cold bath and laconicum open off it on opposite sides — a CROSS at the centre of a linear spine) → `tepidarium I` → `tepidarium II` → `caldarium` (+ alveus) — each door a threshold with a runnel for surface water (Bearsden's "shallow runnel … carved across it") and a step DOWN of 80–160 mm room to room (a graded floor: the CIRC addendum's `direction` attribute applied to a thermal enfilade); hammam — `camekan` (the only big room; a `LOBBY`-class hall with galleries/cubicles) → `soğukluk` → `hararet` with `halvet` cells off it; the `külhan` is reached ONLY from outside (`joint.refused` between furnace and hot room — the fuel/fire prohibition). The Badstube's upper dwelling is reached by its own `STAIR_HALL{WINDER}` from the Diele (PLAUSIBLE — the Wendelstein page does not describe the stair). Widths: Bearsden's cells 2.5–4.5 m across — a BATH-CELL bucket 2.5–3.5 m (heated) / 4–4.75 m (dry/cold) — and its path 0.7 m; the hammam's camekan is the only room above 7 m (PLAUSIBLE). No corridor anywhere (the bath is the enfilade's strongest case). Gender: TWO parallel enfilades (Pristina's double hammam; Basel's separate men's and women's baths) — a `duplicate(gender)` rule on the whole parti.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** `STORE{FUEL}` = the wood store at the furnace mouth, OUTSIDE the bathing rooms, `FORBIDDEN_DOOR(hot rooms)`, `REQUIRED_NEAR(furnace)`; "several hundred cubic metres a year" makes it the largest store in a Badstube — sizeBucket LARGE, lightReq NONE, and an exterior consequence (the wood yard); `STORE{LINEN}` = towels/sheets (the bather's hire — fixture-scale; the apodyterium's "seven niches" are lockers — fixtures); `CELLAR/UNDERCROFT` = the vaulted stoking chamber and the hypocaust basement (a SUB-FLOOR process cell with no walls above — R-INST-2 gap 9; Wendelstein's 12th-century cellar beneath the Nuremberg bath is a law-7 fossil); `SCULLERY`-class = the boiler/copper room (Schürkammer / külhan: water tank + furnace + storage in one service cell — the brief's "bathhouse fuel/linen store" is one cell at this grade); `CISTERN` (Wendelstein) as a `VERTICAL{SHAFT}` fixture; the Bader's `CLOSET{SURGERY}` (the Schröpfstube of 1437 — a one-function cell); the hammam's `halvet` cubicles are cells, not storage. Prohibition: fuel ↔ hot room (no door); sick ↔ bath (1505 — a person-rule, not a cell-rule).

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: BADSTUBE_HOUSE` (two storeys; process floor of 4 m + dwelling over; enfilade Diele → Heiz/Auskleide → Bad → Schwitz; cistern; furnace outside) · `parti: ROMAN_RANGE` (linear 28 × 5 m spine with a cross of cold bath + laconicum at the frigidarium; praefurnium at the hot end; latrine beside — the ceiling in the military/Roman register, and the fantasy "ancient bath" default) · `parti: HAMMAM_DOMED` (camekan hall → warm → domed hot with halvets; külhan behind; double for gender) · `HOSTED{host: BARBER_HOUSE | INN}` (a single heated Stube with a tub and a copper — the post-1550 English and German floor). `functions[]`: floor = `undress`, `heat_source`, `tub`, `water`; rungs = + `sweat_room` → + `separate furnace cell` → + `cistern/boiler` → + `barber/surgery` → + `gender duplicate` → + `cold plunge` → + `hot plunge` → + `laconicum` → + `latrine`; ceiling = the thermae (not fetched). `fixtures[]`: `TUB{wood, 2–4 persons}`, `STONE_BED{Aufgusssteine}`, `OFENBANK`, `STEPPED_BENCH`, `COPPER/BOILER`, `NICHE_LOCKERS{7}`, `WEDEL_SIGN` (the bath-whisk hung out = OPEN), `GOBEK_TASI`, `FOUNTAIN` (camekan), `DRAIN+RUNNEL` (every threshold), `SKYLIGHT_STARS`. Structural buckets: `furnace` (EXTREME fire grade — R-INST-2 gap 11), `waterSupply` (mandatory), `heavyFloor` (pillared hypocaust / flags), `wetFloor` (sloped, drained), `tallGround` (4 m), `steamShell` (vault/dome with vents), `fuelHunger` (the siting pull R-INST-2 named). Licensing: tier ≥ town (the catalog is right); era: 1150–c. 1560 as a PUBLIC building in the German register, 12th c.–1546 in England (then a brothel; the bagnio from 1679), hammam any era in the Islamic register, Roman ceiling; prosperity ≥ middling (fuel); culture gate on the heat engine (stove+stones / hypocaust / duct-and-boiler); `closedBy: {SYPHILIS 1495+, REFORMATION, FUEL_PRICE, PROCLAMATION 1546}` dated events. VERDICT: town Public bathhouse **BUILDING** (`BADSTUBE_HOUSE`) in the continental register 1150–1560; **HOSTED** (barber's house / inn Stube) after; in the English register **BUILDING** only as the 12th–15th-century stew that then BECOMES family F's brothel (a dated `reclassedTo`), or the 1679+ bagnio. HOME: NO TYPED HOME (ROOM_KINDS has no bath, furnace room, sweat room, dressing room — four cells); `FURNISHING_KINDS` lacks tub, stove, bench-bed; FACET_INFERENCE → `generic` (§Σ).

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) A THERMAL-GRADIENT enfilade: rooms ordered by temperature with a floor that steps DOWN and drains along the order — a `sequence` attribute on THROUGH_ROOM chains that S5 placement must respect (no shortcut door from cold to hot; the cross at the hub is the only branch). (2) The furnace is a cell with a REFUSED joint to the rooms it heats (fire is fed from outside) — the CIRC addendum's `joint.refused` gets its hospitality member; the hypocaust is a sub-floor cell (gap 9) spanning several rooms. (3) `duplicate(gender)`: a whole-parti mirror (double hammam; men's/women's Badstuben) — a new generator rule distinct from N-identical-cells. (4) The bath is the tranche's clearest DATED CLASS TRANSITION: Badstube → brothel (England) / tannery (Basel 1674) / laundry → dwelling (Granada) / barber only (Wendelstein) — `reclassedTo` events with named causes (disease, ordinance, fuel). (5) A FUEL-HUNGER siting pull and a fuel STORE larger than any bathing cell — law 2's economic clamp has a physical term here (wood price closes baths). (6) The catalog's "heated water" should be a heat-engine ENUM {STOVE_STONES, HYPOCAUST, BOILER_DUCT, NONE(cold spring)}.

**(h) Continental vs English.** England is the POOR register for this family: the stew becomes the brothel and the public bath disappears 1546–1679; the German lands have the Badstube in every town until the 1560s (Basel's eleven, Nuremberg's Irrerbad 1326–1695), with the Bader as a licensed surgeon-barber and the building a two-storey house; the Islamic world has the hammam as a permanent civic institution attached to the mosque and the market (Pristina's double hammam of c. 800 m²; the Bañuelo's three-vault sequence); Rome is the origin and ceiling (Bearsden's 28-m range as the smallest complete programme; the urban thermae untouched). The Finnish/Russian smoke sauna (PLAUSIBLE, not researched) is the stove-and-stones engine at its floor — a single cell — and belongs beside the Badstube's Schwitzbad in a setting-agnostic enum.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None (a magically heated bath needs no furnace cell and no fuel store — the one place the fantasy register simplifies the parti; flag for R-INST-5 that removing the furnace removes the refused joint and the fuel-hunger pull).

## §7 · FAMILY F — the regulated stew (the brothel as a building type, treated clinically): Brothel (town L1492 "Tolerated or regulated prostitution.") · Brothel (red light district) (city L2044 "Legal or tolerated prostitution quarter.") · Red light district (city L2076 "Large organized prostitution quarter.")

*Provenance: sub-lane EFG. Evidence: Pullwitt, "Das Nürnberger Frauenhaus" (LOCAL, `RINST4-EFG-pullwitt-frauenhaus.txt`, 30 pp., extempore.org PDF fetched by curl — the Nuremberg Frauenhausordnung quoted from Baader's Polizeiverordnungen and the Staatsarchiv), the Wellcome Collection article on the Bankside ordinances, the Pérouges guild page on the French prostibulum publicum, the pompeiiinpictures Lupanar page, and four search digests (Bankside 1161/1546; Frauenhaus closures; Castelletto/Onestà/Rossiaud; Pompeii Lupanar). The exploringsouthwark.co.uk page had been hijacked by a casino-advert site (the fetch returned gambling copy — reported as a source-integrity finding, not used).*

**(a) Analogue.** The regulated stew is a licensed HOUSE with a keeper, a common room and chambers, and the licence — not the plan — is what the sources record. (1) **The Nuremberg Frauenhaus** (LOCAL, Pullwitt pp. 10–21, quoting the Frauenhausordnung via Baader): the house was town-owned and let to a Frauenwirt; the ordinance fixed the women's payments — "zwen und viertzig pfenning" a week for board, "sieben pfenning wochengelts", a penny per customer and three pence per night-guest — fixed costs of 49 Pf/week against a day-labourer's 8–9 Pf/day and a journeyman's 16–20; a visit cost "three pence, or nine pence for the night", so 2–5 Pf by day and 10–15 Pf overnight all told; the keeper could seize a debtor's "claider und anders, so sie bey im in seinem haws hette" but could not pawn or lend the women (pp. 10–11); the women could board elsewhere, the keeper supplying the crockery (p. 11); the council ordered the keeper "alle wochen zum minnsten ein bade zu machen und zu heben in seinem haus" — a WEEKLY BATH in the house (p. 11: a bath cell inside the brothel); married women and burghers' daughters were barred (p. 12); closing time was **23:00**, "very long" given fire precautions (p. 12); in 1496 the house held **26 women**, in 1562 only **10–12** (p. 21); the council closed it in March 1562 "während eines Pestzuges" under Reformation pressure — "Was die Syphilis nicht vermochte, dass vermochte die Reformation in Nürnberg" (p. 21); the free/secret trade outnumbered the licensed house: Nuremberg's "sant" on the Regnitz bank, the Plärrer and the "Judenpühel vor dem Langertor" were the tolerated open-air districts "am Rand oder vor den Toren der Stadt" (p. 15); a secret brothel ("Hurntaiber") was a HOUSE where "die wirtin hielt eemener in einer stuben und in einer andern stuben jung gesellen tag und naht" — two Stuben, married men in one, young men in the other (p. 14); at Constance during the Council of 1414–18 a quartermaster counted "in einem Hauss etwan 30" women and "bei 700" in all, "ohne die in den Ställen lagen und in den Bdstuben" — women lodged in stables and bathhouses (p. 14). The Herrenberg house bought "a new bed for the Stube" in 1494 (CONFIRMED-digest). The SITING sequence is the grammar's prize: the Nuremberg house stood off a lane ("gesslein"); after the Praisengasse neighbours complained in 1543 the council built "ein thor mit zweien flügeln und ein klein thürlein mit ein anhengen stein, damit jedes mal wider zufallen" across the lane — a two-leaf gate with a self-closing wicket — placed "so that the sight of the house" was cut off from passers-by; in 1546 the women were forbidden dances and the wine market; in 1557 a LOCK was put on the gate, the keeper holding the key, "to lock it at one in the morning … and keep it shut all day, but open it an hour into the night and leave it open till daybreak" (LOCAL, Pullwitt pp. 19–20) — the brothel was first hidden (a screen gate), then made nocturnal (a timed gate), then closed. (2) **The Bankside stews** (Southwark, Liberty of the Bishop of Winchester): the ordinances "Touching the Government of the Stewholders in Southwark under the Direction of the Bishop of Winchester" — 36 regulations with fines (A3: forcibly detaining a woman, 100 s; B11: a woman "cannot be forced to board at a stewhouse but must be allowed to board wherever she choose", 20 s; B4: no married women or nuns, 12 d; B2: no man held "as prisoner for any debt"; B25: no woman with the "burning disease"; B7: no "dragging men in from the street", 20 s); the bishop's text claims 1162 but "the document itself has been dated to the 15th century" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wellcome Collection: The bishop's profitable sex workers](https://wellcomecollection.org/stories/the-bishop-s-profitable-sex-workers), fetched 2026-08-23); the digest adds the 39-rule count (a different transcription), the "single women … ought to have their own going and coming at their own liberty", stewholders "to be married and the premises to be used as brothels only" (no victualling — a use-exclusivity rule), and Stow's description of the houses as "whited and painted, with signes on the front, for a token of the said houses" (CONFIRMED-digest); the stews began as the bishop's 12th-century bathhouses (family E), were closed by Henry VII in 1504 for a year and by Henry VIII's proclamation of 13 April 1546 for good, after which "many observers believed that things had gotten worse" (CONFIRMED-digest). So the English stew is a ROW of whitewashed riverside houses, each with a sign, each a former bath, in a LIBERTY outside the City's jurisdiction — the city row "Red light district" is this: a licensed STREET in a legal enclave. (3) **The Mediterranean municipal brothel**: Rossiaud's four-tier hierarchy puts the "prostibulum publicum — a municipal brothel constructed, maintained and governed by public authorities, princely, municipal or ecclesiastical" at the top; "most cities" had one (CONFIRMED-digest); the French house had "two distinct areas: individual rooms or chambers where prostitutes received clients privately; a common space for socializing" with wine, meals and (forbidden) dice; at Valence in 1444 the women's quarter was "grand comme une petite ville … fermé tout l'entour de murs et de une seule porte" — walled, with ONE door (Lalaing 1500) — and a room's kit was "a bed, linen sheets, a quilt and a cover" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [La Guilde Pérougienne: Être mérétrice au prostibulum publicum](https://www.laguildeperougienne.fr/2021/04/11/etre-meretrice-au-prostibulum-publicum/), fetched 2026-08-23, citing Rossiaud); Venice's Castelletto (1360) at the Rialto was "guarded by six armed keepers", later dispersed to the Carampane; Florence's Onestà (1403) was the magistracy "to establish brothels in the city, licence prostitutes and pimps … regulate their activities" (CONFIRMED-digest). (4) **The Roman ceiling**: the Pompeii Lupanar VII.12.18 — "five small rooms (each approximately 1.4 by 2.7 meters), each furnished with a built-in stone bed" c. 1.8 m long, "arranged on both sides" of a central corridor, a latrine "behind a screen wall", no windows ("dimly lit by oil lamps"), an upper floor of five more rooms "accessible via an independent entrance" for "a more refined clientele"; "the only purpose-built brothel that has been definitively identified" at Pompeii; excavated 1862 (CONFIRMED-digest; SUB-LANE CONFIRMED for the corridor-and-five-rooms layout and the latrine, [pompeiiinpictures VII.12.18](https://pompeiiinpictures.com/pompeiiinpictures/R7/7%2012%2018%20p2.htm), fetched 2026-08-23 — the page gives no dimensions; the 1.4 × 2.7 m figure is digest-only). **Live/work**: the keeper and his wife live in (Bankside: stewholders "to be married"); the women board in or out (both ordinances make boarding OPTIONAL — the chamber is a workplace first); the Nuremberg keeper "zu beherbergen" forbidden classes — the house LODGES. **Prosperity/wear**: floor = a house with a Stube and two or three Kammern (the Hurntaiber's two Stuben); rung 1 = the licensed house: common room with wine and a hearth, chambers, a bath cell, a keeper's lodging, a sign, a lane gate; rung 2 = the walled quarter with one door (Valence; the Castelletto with armed keepers); ceiling = the Lupanar's purpose-built corridor-and-cells block with a separate upper entrance. Decline: the gate (1543) → the lock (1557) → the closure (1562; 1546) — the building survives as a house.

**(b) Measured.** Lupanar cells c. 1.4 × 2.7 m, beds c. 1.8 m, 5 + 5 cells (CONFIRMED-digest only). Nuremberg: 26 women (1496) → 10–12 (1562) — a HEADCOUNT, the only capacity figure for a medieval house; Constance 1414–18: c. 30 in one house, c. 700 in the city (LOCAL, Pullwitt). Tariffs (LOCAL, Pullwitt): 49 Pf/week fixed costs; 3 Pf/act, 9 Pf/night; closing 23:00; the 1557 lock hours. Bankside fines 100 s / 20 s / 12 d (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). NOT FOUND: any plan or room dimension of a medieval Frauenhaus or stew-house (Pullwitt is a legal history; the Bankside houses are known from Stow's words and the 1542 plan, neither fetched with figures); the Valence quarter's size beyond "as large as a small town"; the Castelletto's form. The BROTHEL CHAMBER bucket is therefore DERIVED: a one-bed Kammer of the Stube house (DWR1A's chamber 7–12 m² PLAUSIBLE) down to the Lupanar cell (3.8 m²) as the floor.

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) The brothel is NOT a purpose-built type in the medieval register — it is a HOUSE under a licence (Nuremberg's town-owned house; Herrenberg's Stube with a new bed; the Hurntaiber's two Stuben; the Bankside's former bathhouses): the only purpose-built brothel in the evidence is Roman. The catalog's "Brothel" as an institution with a `vice` interior is right only as a FUNCTION on a dwelling/bath parti. (ii) The licensed house was the MINORITY of the trade: "die Privatprostitution den größten Anteil … gestellt hat" (LOCAL, Pullwitt p. 15) and the tolerated open-air districts were at the edge or outside the gates — the city row "Red light district" is TWO things: a licensed street inside a liberty (Bankside) and an unbuilt edge zone (Nuremberg's sant/Plärrer) — NO_BUILDING for the latter. (iii) The negation "closed for syphilis" is CONTESTED by the sources themselves: "there is no surviving ordinance describing the closure of a Frauenhaus due to syphilis" (CONFIRMED-digest) and Pullwitt's "what syphilis could not, the Reformation could"; the English digests credit syphilis for 1504/1546 — carry both. (iv) Boarding OPTIONAL in both ordinances means the house may have FEWER beds than women — capacity is not bed count. (v) The exploringsouthwark page is compromised (gambling spam in place of history) — a source-integrity finding; Stow's list of 18 houses "reduced to 12" remains unverified (ledger).

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** The house: `THROUGH_ROOM` — street door → `Stube` (the common room with wine, hearth, bench) → `Kammern` off it or over it by a `STAIR_HALL{WINDER}`; the Lupanar's `CORRIDOR{central}` with cells both sides is the Roman exception (a CORRIDOR licensed by PROGRAM — cells of equal rank — exactly the CIRC addendum's prison/college licence) and its SEPARATE upper entrance is a `STAIR_HALL{EXTERNAL}` with `joint.target: STREET`; the bath cell (Nuremberg) off the Stube. The LANE GATE is the family's signature circulation cell: `EXTERIOR_WALK{LANE}` closed by a `LOBBY{DOOR_LOBBY}` at its mouth — two-leaf gate + self-closing wicket (1543) → locked by timetable (1557) — a `severable` joint (CIRC addendum §4.3 item 2) with a `schedule` (open an hour into the night, shut at one, shut by day): the first TIMED joint in the corpus. The walled quarter (Valence) is an `EXTERIOR_WALK{COURT_RING}` with `approach: SINGLE` (one door — item 3). Sign on the front (Bankside) = an exterior fixture; whitewash = a facade state. Widths: none measured; the Lupanar corridor is PLAUSIBLE < 1.5 m (cells 1.4 m wide either side of it in a narrow insula plot).

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** `CELLAR` for wine (the common room serves wine — PLAUSIBLE, not stated); `CLOSET{LINEN}` (sheets, quilts, covers — the Valence kit; a chest per chamber); the keeper's `CLOSET{PLEDGE}` — the women's seized clothes "halten und versperren" (a locked store of pledges, R-INST-2's pawn store in a new register); the bath cell's `STORE{FUEL}` (family E); no pantry/buttery recorded (the women may board out). Prohibition: none architectural; the use-exclusivity rule (stewholders may not victual — digest) is a FUNCTION prohibition on the host.

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: LICENSED_STUBE_HOUSE` (town L1492) = a dwelling parti (DWR1A Stube/Kammer or the English hall-and-chambers) + `{common_room, chambers{n ≤ headcount}, bath_cell?, keeper_lodging, sign, lane_gate?}`; `parti: STEW_ROW` (city L2044) = N former-bathhouse/dwelling units on one street inside a `liberty` boundary, each with a sign ("whited and painted"), the street itself the district — R-INST-2's N-identical-buildings at street scale (the Borough High Street mechanism of family B again); `parti: WALLED_QUARTER_ONE_DOOR` (city L2076, Mediterranean) = a court ring of houses with a single gate and armed keepers (Castelletto; Valence); `parti: NO_BUILDING{EDGE_ZONE}` (city L2076, the tolerated open ground at the walls/river/gate — Nuremberg's sant). Ceiling: `LUPANAR_CORRIDOR_CELLS` (Roman only; 5 + 5 cells 1.4 × 2.7 m, masonry beds, separate upper entrance). `functions[]`: floor = `common_room`, `chamber{1+}`, `keeper`; rungs = + `sign` → + `bath_cell` (ordinance) → + `lane_gate` → + `second Stube by clientele` (married/young) → + `wine cellar`; ceiling = the walled quarter. `fixtures[]`: `BED{+ sheets, quilt, cover}`, `SIGN_FRONT`, `WHITEWASH`, `GATE_TWO_LEAF + WICKET{self-closing stone weight}`, `LOCK{keyholder: keeper; schedule}`, `MASONRY_BED` (Roman), `OIL_LAMP` (windowless cells), `LATRINE_SCREEN`. Structural: none beyond the host's; `windowless` at the Roman ceiling. Licensing: tier ≥ town (the catalog is right); era 1360 (Castelletto) / 1403 (Onestà) / 15th c. (Bankside text; Nuremberg ordinance) to 1546 (England) / 1562 (Nuremberg) — CLOSED by dated ordinance in the Protestant register, surviving in the Catholic/Mediterranean one (PLAUSIBLE beyond 1562 — not researched); culture: the liberty/enclave (England), the town-owned house (German), the municipal quarter (France/Italy); `hiddenBy: SCREEN_GATE (1543)`, `timedBy: LOCK (1557)`, `closedBy: ORDINANCE (1546/1562)`. VERDICT: town Brothel **HOSTED** (host: a dwelling with a Stube; the licence makes it a brothel — `OWN BUILDING` only where the town OWNS the house, which is still a house parti); city Brothel (red light district) **BUILDING ×N as a STREET parti in a LIBERTY** (the stew row); city Red light district **NO_BUILDING{EDGE_ZONE}** + the walled quarter as its Mediterranean BUILDING form. HOME: `INTERIOR_KINDS.vice` (common + kitchen + cellar + lodging) is a fair fit for the licensed house; NO TYPED HOME for the lane gate, the bath cell, the walled quarter; the FACET_INFERENCE regex matches `brothel` → `vice` correctly (the one entry in the tranche it gets right).

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) A TIMED, KEYED gate on a lane — `joint.severable` with a `schedule` and a `keyholder` (the keeper): a news-visible state (open at night, shut by day) that the CIRC addendum's severable joint lacks. (2) A SCREEN gate whose purpose is SIGHT, not passage ("dadurch also das gesicht zum haus abgeschnitten") — an exterior fixture with a `visibility` effect, i.e. the first fixture that edits the MAP's sightlines (a fabric seam). (3) `liberty`/jurisdiction as a licensing boundary: the stew row exists because the Bishop's liberty is not the City — the engine's settlement needs a jurisdiction layer for this family (and for R-INST-6's fronts). (4) Capacity as a HEADCOUNT, not a bed count (26 → 10–12), with boarding optional — the `capacity` field on `hostedIn` (family C's horses) returns here as persons. (5) Dated `hiddenBy → timedBy → closedBy` is a three-step decline fossil sequence (law 5) on a single house. (6) The bath cell inside the brothel (ordinance) inverts family E's England story — the relation between E and F is a CULTURE-gated containment (bath ⊃ brothel in England; brothel ⊃ bath in Nuremberg).

**(h) Continental vs English.** England: the stew row in a riverside liberty, ex-bathhouses, closed 1546; Germany: the town-owned Frauenhaus with an ordinance, a keeper who was often also executioner or gaoler (CONFIRMED-digest), a lane gate, closed 1562 at Nuremberg; France/Italy: the municipal prostibulum publicum or the walled Castelletto/Valence quarter with a single door and guards, under a magistracy (Onestà 1403); Rome: the purpose-built Lupanar. The catalog's three rows map onto three REGISTERS as much as three tiers — the engine's culture weight decides which parti a city draws, and the Protestant/Catholic split is an ERA × CULTURE gate with a dated closure.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None (treated clinically as a building type; the catalog's city rows are zoning, and the "Zone of Truth enforcement" of the gambling district (family G) has no counterpart here).
