## §8 · FAMILY G — gaming houses: Gambling den (town L1471 "Dice, cards, simple games of chance.") · Gambling halls (city L2036 "Dedicated establishments. Some illegal.") · Gambling district (city L2084 "Concentrated gaming houses. Zone of Truth enforcement.")

*Provenance: sub-lane EFG (the last family it reached before the kill). Evidence: Wikipedia Ridotto; the Unlawful Games Act 1541 (Wikipedia digest + the rkarl.org commentary fetched); Wikipedia Cockpit-in-Court and the SHALT Cockpit-at-Court page (both thin); search digests on the Whitehall cockpit (the 40-ft diameter), jeu de paume courts, and the Ridotto; Brown 2007 (LOCAL) for the tavern's playing tables and shuffleboard rooms (family A); the French prostibulum page for dice in the brothel's common room (family F).*

**(a) Analogue.** The gaming "house" in the English register before 1638 is a ROOM or a SHED, never a building of its own — and the statute says so. The Unlawful Games Act 1541 (33 Hen. VIII c. 9, "An Acte for Mayntenance of Artyllarie and debarringe of unlawful Games") made "the keeping of any common house, alley or place where the games were played for gain, lucre or living" the offence — the games themselves were not unlawful — and provided licences ("placards", s. 15) for "Bowling Alleys, Dysing Houses, or any other unlawful" establishments; it barred servants and the lower orders from "tables, tennis, dice, cards, bowls, clash, coyting, logating, or any other unlawful game, except during Christmas and in their masters' houses", reserving them to the wealthy; parts stood until 1960 (CONFIRMED-digest, Wikipedia "Unlawful Games Act 1541"; SUB-LANE CONFIRMED for the placard licence and ss. 22–23 at [rkarl.org: The Unlawful Games Act 1541](https://1884.rkarl.org/2020/01/the-unlawful-games-act-1541.html), fetched 2026-08-23 — a commentary, not the statute text). The three licensable forms the Act names are the grammar's three partis: the **dicing house** (a room: Southampton's taverns and alehouses kept "playing tables" — two pairs in one alehouse hall, a "shuffleboard room" in a 1646 tavern and two 1690s inns; "table playing in his cellar" was presented in 1600 — LOCAL, Brown 2007 pp. 65–66, 114; the French brothel's common room had dice "though officially prohibited" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, family F), the **bowling alley** (a linear shed or green — no fetched plan; PLAUSIBLE 30–40 m × 3–4 m for the covered alley), and the **tennis play** (the jeu de paume / real-tennis court: "long and narrow … around 90 feet long and 27 feet wide" — 27.4 × 8.2 m — with "over 500 courts" in France "at the dawn of the 17th century", surviving courts at Oxford, Cambridge, Falkland and Hampton Court; the modern Bordeaux court 29.1 m long — CONFIRMED-digest, 2026-08-23, of real.tennis / topendsports / Smithsonian; the New Inn's real-tennis court of 1649 in its rear yard is family B's LOCAL instance). The fourth form is the **cockpit**: Henry VIII's Cockpit at Whitehall, c. 1530–32, "most likely a round building with a peaked roof, about 40 feet (12 m) in diameter", "an octagonal cockpit … surrounded by a ring of seats" in the digest's other phrasing, redesigned by Inigo Jones in 1629 as a court theatre for masques; Christopher Beeston in 1616 "bought and refurbished an old cock-fighting pit in Drury Lane, turning it into a new theatre called the Cockpit" (CONFIRMED-digest; SUB-LANE CONFIRMED that the Whitehall cockpit "was used as an actual cockpit" before the 1629 conversion, [Wikipedia: Cockpit-in-Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cockpit-in-Court), and that it "staged plays until 1642", [SHALT: The Cockpit-at-Court](https://shalt.dmu.ac.uk/locations/the-cockpit-at-court.html), both fetched 2026-08-23 — neither gives a dimension; the 40 ft is digest-only). The cockpit is the one GAMING form with its own geometry (a ring of tiered seats round a small pit), and its two conversions to theatres (1616, 1629) are family H's origin story in miniature: the round gaming house and the round playhouse are one parti at two scales. **The first dedicated public gaming house** is Venetian: the **Ridotto**, opened 1638 "in a wing of Venice's Palazzo Dandolo near the church of San Moisè", "the West's first public, legal mercantile casino"; the wing "was four stories tall and featured a long entrance hall, dining rooms and other fineries", with gaming tables "primarily situated in its upper floors"; nominally public but restricted by stakes and by dress — "three-cornered hats and masks"; games biribi (a 70-outcome lottery paying 64:1), basetta, later faro; open in Carnival (26 December to Ash Wednesday — digest); closed by the Great Council in 1774 on Giorgio Pisani's motion "to preserve the piety, sound discipline and moderate behavior" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Ridotto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ridotto), fetched 2026-08-23). So the catalog's city "Gambling halls — Dedicated establishments" is a ≥ 1638 Venetian form HOSTED in a palazzo wing (a long entrance hall → dining rooms → upper-floor gaming rooms: the piano-nobile enfilade of a palace re-used), and its "district" is Venice's private casini and ridotti of the 18th century (CONFIRMED-digest, thinvenice/wheninvenice — not fetched) — a scatter of rented apartments, not a zone. **Siting**: the dicing room is wherever the tavern is; the cockpit and bowling alley at the court, the inn yard or the edge of town (the Drury Lane cockpit was in a suburb); the Ridotto beside a parish church in the city's heart. **Live/work**: the keeper is the tavern keeper or the palazzo's lessee; no one sleeps in a cockpit. **Prosperity/wear**: floor = a pair of playing tables in an alehouse hall; rung 1 = a "shuffleboard room" (a dedicated games cell in a tavern or inn, ≥ 1646); rung 2 = a cockpit/bowling alley/tennis play as a licensed shed in a yard; ceiling = the Ridotto's palazzo wing. Decline: the licence (1541 placards; the 1774 closure); the cockpit becomes a theatre.

**(b) Measured.** Whitehall cockpit c. 40 ft / 12 m diameter (CONFIRMED-digest only); real-tennis court c. 90 × 27 ft (27.4 × 8.2 m) and 29.1 m (Bordeaux) (CONFIRMED-digest); Ridotto four storeys (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED); Southampton: two pairs of playing tables + "sixty yards of wainscot benches" in one hall, a fore chamber seating 29 (LOCAL, Brown — family A); biribi 70 outcomes / 64:1 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). NOT FOUND: any plan of a dicing house or a bowling alley; the Cockpit-in-Court's seating tiers and pit diameter from a primary (Jones's plan is referenced but not fetched); the Ridotto's room count. The search for "Whitehall Cockpit plan octagonal … pit diameter tiered seating" returned the 40-ft figure from a London-history blog (exploring-london.com) — single-sourced.

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) The negation "the gaming house was a tavern room, not a building" is CONFIRMED by the 1541 Act's wording ("common house, alley or place"), by the Southampton inventories (tables in halls, a room in a tavern, a cellar) and by the Ridotto's being a WING of a palace — every fetched gaming "house" is hosted. (ii) The cockpit is the counterexample: a round, roofed, purpose-built seating ring (c. 1530) — but at COURT, not in a town; the catalog's "Gambling den" at town tier has no purpose-built form. (iii) The shape question (octagonal vs round) is contested between the two digests for the same building — carry both; Jones's 1629 plan would settle it (ledger). (iv) The catalog's "Some illegal" and "Zone of Truth enforcement" describe the 1541 regime well: licensed by placard, otherwise an offence; the fantasy enforcement is a fixture-use of the magistrate's office, not a building. (v) Tennis and bowling are GAMING only under the Act's class rule (unlawful for servants, lawful for gentlemen in their own houses) — the same court is a gentleman's exercise and a commoner's gaming house: licence is by PERSON, not by cell.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** Dicing house/shuffleboard room: the host tavern's `THROUGH_ROOM` (a back room or an upper chamber, or the cellar — family A's tap-room cellar with "table playing in his cellar" = a `STAIR_HALL{DOWN}` joint to a gaming use). Cockpit: a CENTRAL pit with a RING of tiered seats — a `GALLERY{RING, MEZZANINE}` at one or two levels round a `pit` cell, entered by one or two doors; the 1616/1629 conversions add a stage and a `STAIR_HALL` to boxes (PLAUSIBLE). Bowling alley/tennis play: a `RANGE/LINEAR` exterior walk or a long shed (`OPEN_AT_ONE_END` or fully roofed) with a spectators' `GALLERY{dedans/penthouse}` along one side (the real-tennis penthouse is a sloping gallery roof — PLAUSIBLE from the game's rules, not fetched). Ridotto: the palazzo's `LONG_GALLERY`-class "long entrance hall" → dining rooms → `STAIR_HALL{GRAND}` → upper gaming rooms in enfilade (`THROUGH_ROOM`); masks at the door = a `LOBBY` with a dress check (a fixture-use). Widths: cockpit 12 m diameter (digest); tennis court 8.2 m wide; none for the rest.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** `STORE{PROPERTY}` for tables/boards/dice/cards (chests — fixtures in the host); `CELLAR` (wine; the cellar as a gaming room itself — 1600 presentment); the cockpit's `STORE{BIRDS}` (pens — PLAUSIBLE, not fetched); the Ridotto's bank/cash `CLOSET{STRONGROOM}` (PLAUSIBLE — the banker's table is a fixture); no pantry/larder beyond the host's. Prohibition: none architectural; the Act's class bar is a person rule.

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: NO_BUILDING / HOSTED{host: TAVERN | INN | ALEHOUSE | BROTHEL}` (town L1471 — the dicing room: a fixture set `{PLAYING_TABLES, DICE, CARDS, SHUFFLEBOARD}` on a common room, back room, upper chamber or cellar); `parti: COCKPIT_RING` (a round/octagonal roofed ring c. 12 m, pit + tiered seats, 1–2 doors; court/inn-yard/suburb; `reclassedTo: PLAYHOUSE` 1616/1629); `parti: GAMING_SHED_LINEAR` (bowling alley; tennis play 27 × 8 m with a side gallery; in a yard or a garden — the New Inn 1649); `parti: RIDOTTO_WING` (city L2036, ≥ 1638, Venetian register: a palazzo wing's enfilade — entrance hall, dining rooms, upper gaming rooms — HOSTED in a great house); `parti: NO_BUILDING{SCATTER}` (city L2084: the casini — rented apartments across the city, no zone). `functions[]`: floor = `tables`; rungs = + `dedicated games room` (≥ 1646) → + `licensed shed` (1541 placard) → + `cockpit ring` → + `palazzo wing`; ceiling = the Ridotto. `fixtures[]`: `PLAYING_TABLES{pairs}`, `SHUFFLEBOARD`, `BIRIBI_BOARD{70}`, `FARO_BANK`, `MASK_RULE` (door), `TIERED_SEATS`, `PIT`, `PENTHOUSE_GALLERY`, `PLACARD` (the licence as a displayed fixture). Structural: `roundPlan` (cockpit — the only round cell in the tranche besides the theatre), `linear` (alley/court). Licensing: town Gambling den = HOSTED at any era, licence-gated 1541–1960 in England (a PERSON/CLASS rule: the same table is lawful in a gentleman's house); city Gambling halls = `RIDOTTO_WING` era ≥ 1638, culture Venetian/Italian (the English club house is post-register), prosperity high; city Gambling district = scatter, era 18th c. Venetian; the cockpit era c. 1530–1642 (court) / inns and suburbs to the 18th c. (PLAUSIBLE). VERDICT: town **NO_BUILDING/HOSTED** (host: tavern/alehouse/inn/brothel; the cockpit and the alley are licensed SHEDS in a host's yard); city Gambling halls **HOSTED** (a palazzo wing) — `BUILDING` only in the sense that the host is a named great house; city Gambling district **NO_BUILDING{SCATTER}**. HOME: `vice` template via the regex (`gambling`) — acceptable for the hosted room; NO TYPED HOME for the cockpit ring, the tennis play, the palazzo wing.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) A licence by PERSON/CLASS rather than by cell (the 1541 Act): the same fixture set is lawful or an offence by who plays — a rule the engine can only express as a `licence` attribute on the FUNCTION with a demography input, not on the room. (2) The ROUND ring parti (cockpit → playhouse) is the smallest instance of family H's geometry — one `RING{pit, tiers}` primitive serves both at 12 m and at 24 m. (3) `reclassedTo` with a named architect's plan (Jones 1616/1629) — the dated conversion fossil again (law 5). (4) A "district" that is a SCATTER of hosted rooms (casini) — the engine's district primitive (R-INST-2 gap 2, a zone) needs a `scatter` mode with no geometry. (5) The palazzo-wing host means a `hostedIn` target can be a PRIVATE great house (DWR1A §2-bis.7), not an institution — the same extension family A needed for dwellings.

**(h) Continental vs English.** England: a licensing regime over rooms and sheds, no gaming building before the 18th-century club; Venice: the state-run Ridotto of 1638–1774 in a palace wing and the private casini; France: the tripot (the tennis-court-cum-gaming-house — PLAUSIBLE, not fetched) and "over 500" paume courts by 1600; the Islamic and Roman registers were not researched for this family (the Roman tabernae lusoriae are PLAUSIBLE hosted rooms). The catalog's "Gambling district" has no historical zone anywhere fetched — it is a fantasy convenience the grammar should render as a scatter of hosted rooms with a `concentration` weight.

**(i) Fantasy note.** "Zone of Truth enforcement" is a magistrate's fixture-use (a truth-spell at the door = the Ridotto's mask rule inverted); no building consequence beyond a `LOBBY` with a door check.

## §9 · FAMILY H — playhouse, opera and music: Theaters (city L2028 "Permanent performance venues.") · Multiple theaters (city L2059 "Permanent venues. Professional companies.") · Opera house (city L2093 "High culture. Elite patronage.") · Bardic college (city L2101 "Formal musical education. Amphitheater, dormitories…" — the performance hall only; the collegiate plan is R-INST-3's)

*Provenance: sub-lane HI (killed 06:12:13Z after 49 tool calls, 30 of them fetches; no report). Evidence: the *Reconstructing the Rose* open-access monograph chapters saved by curl (LOCAL, `RINST4-HI-rose-*.txt`: 4.3.1 outer walls, 4.4.1 galleries, 4.5 yard, 4.5.2 main entrance, 4.6 ingressus, 4.7 stairways, 4.8.1 seating, 4.9 stage, 4.11.2 tiring house, 4.15 roof, 5.1 alterations, introduction — the sub-lane's bullet extracts; the site's TLS certificate had expired, so curl with -k), the henslowe-alleyn.org.uk Fortune-contract essay (fetched by the sub-lane — the parent's §1.4 had found it 500), Survey of London 22 pp. 66–77 (Bankside playhouses and bear gardens), Wikipedia Globe / The Rose / Hope / Blackfriars / Cockpit Theatre / Teatro San Cassiano / Teatro Olimpico / Corral de Almagro / Hôtel de Bourgogne / Roman Theatre St Albans, the Vitruvius Book V text (Thayer), the Gorhambury estate page, and search digests (Rose excavation figures; Teatro Farnese; Boar's Head; Verulamium). Parent §1.4 (the Fortune contract, PARENT-VERIFIED) stands and is the anchor.*

**(a) Analogue.** The English public playhouse is family B's galleried inn yard made permanent (family D (g)), and its measured corpus is now four buildings. (1) **The Rose, Bankside, 1587 / enlarged 1592** (LOCAL, *Reconstructing the Rose*, chs 4–5; SUB-LANE CONFIRMED [Wikipedia: The Rose (theatre)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Rose_(theatre)), fetched 2026-08-23): a **fourteen-sided** timber polygon, "external diameter … about 72 feet (22 m)", internal "about 47 feet (14 m)", lath-and-plaster walls and a THATCHED roof (the drip line eroded into the mortar yard "approximately 0.50m from … the inner wall foundation" shows unguttered eaves — LOCAL 4.15); "the outer sides of the 14 bays were an average of 5.0m in length, equivalent to the Tudor building measurement of a rod (5.5 yards; 16ft 6in …)", each bay on three pile-cap piers "approximately 2.5m … apart" — 28 piers round the ring — on chalk foundations "0.24m deep and wide", with a low brick wall above (LOCAL 4.3.1); "the inner yard was a fragile grey screed floor, partly raked, … measuring some 15m (49ft 2.5in) from east to west and from the stage, 9m (29ft 6in) from north to south, covering approximately 117.3m2", which "might accommodate 400 people loosely packed, and 530 tightly packed"; "the northern half raked downwards about 0.5m (1ft 8in) — approximately 5 degrees — to the front of the stage" (LOCAL 4.5); the Phase I stage was **trapezoidal**, "a central section of 8.08m, and two tapering sides, c. 3.0m … covering an area approximately 47.7m2", or in Bowsher's earlier figures "a maximum depth of 5m (16ft 6in) into the yard, and … maximum width of 8.20m (26ft 10in) at the front … some 46.4m2", on "a brick footing about 0.30m wide" (LOCAL 4.9; the digest gives the fuller trapezoid: 5 m deep, 11.5 m at the front tapering to 8.2 m at the rear — CONFIRMED-digest); the 1592 stage was rebuilt "2.1m (6ft 10 1/2in.) farther north … a depth of 5.6m (18ft 4in) … estimated … 8.4m (27ft 7in) long" and a ROOF was put over it, "which meant extending the galleries to the north to help accommodate sightlines" — the "bulging tulip" plan; MOLA's experiments put the Phase II yard at "550 people loosely packed, and 740 tightly packed", a 39 % gain, while "little extra space was gained" in the galleries (LOCAL 5.1; CONFIRMED-digest); the galleries: Gurr and Orrell's "internal depth … about 10ft 6 ins, and the average length of the benches in them was about 13 ft … an Elizabethan standard separation of 18 ins fore-and-aft … at least six benches in each gallery bay … a total gallery capacity of 1404 persons" (LOCAL 4.8.1); three storeys "common for playhouses" (Kiechel c. 1585: "about three galleries over one another"), though Greenfield argues the Rose may have had two (LOCAL 4.4.1); the lower-gallery "ingressus": "a pair of parallel brick walls within bay 4 (1.47m apart …) perpendicular to the outer wall" at the building's centre point, with a worn Purbeck-marble slab beside it — "probably an entranceway to the galleries, via a threshold, which may have supported steps", mirrored on the unexcavated east (LOCAL 4.6); the gallery floor stood "c. 1.24m … [to] c. 1.00m high" above the yard (LOCAL 4.5.2); **no evidence for stairs** — "internal stairs were probably limited in number because they would … have reduced seating capacity", likely next to the ingressus or in the bay by the stage, and "there is no evidence for any form of external stair turret or tower at the Rose, which seems likely to have been a later innovation, possibly around 1610" (LOCAL 4.7); the money-gatherers at the Theatre (1576) stood "at The door that goeth uppe to the Galleries … to take … money", coins in clay money-boxes broken open in Henslowe's office (LOCAL 4.7); the tiring house left no internal evidence but "internal walls must have been built to separate it from the spectator galleries" (LOCAL 4.11.2); the Red Lion (1567) stage is recorded as "forty foote by thirty foot" and 5 ft high (LOCAL 4.9 citing Loengard). (2) **The Globe, 1599 / 1614**: "a polygon of 20 sides", "approximately 100 feet (30 m)" across, "up to 3,000 spectators", three storeys; stage "approximately 43 feet (13 m)" wide, "27 feet (8 m)" deep, "raised about 5 feet (1.5 m)"; built for £700 from the Theatre's timbers, burnt 29 June 1613 when a cannon's wad "lighting in the Thatch … burn'd it down to the Ground in less than two Hours", rebuilt by June 1614 with a TILE roof for £1,400, closed 1642, demolished 1644–45 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Globe Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globe_Theatre) and [Survey of London 22, pp. 66–77](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/survey-london/vol22/pp66-77), fetched 2026-08-23). (3) **The Fortune, 1600** — parent §1.4 (square 80/55 ft; galleries 12/11/9 ft high × 12½ ft deep; stage 43 ft × half the yard; gentlemen's rooms; TILED, by contract); the henslowe-alleyn essay adds "Fower convenient divisions" for gentlemen's rooms, the stage "paled in" with boards and "fenced with strong iron pykes", a "Shadowe or cover" over it, "Staircases, frame, and stage cover to be tiled (not thatched)", main posts "carved as pilasters with satyr figures", £440, completion 25 July 1600 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [henslowe-alleyn.org.uk: The Contract for the Fortune Playhouse](https://henslowe-alleyn.org.uk/essays/the-contract-for-the-fortune-playhouse-1600/), fetched 2026-08-23). (4) **The Hope, 1613–14** — the dual-use "game place or plaiehouse fitt … bothe for players to plaie in and for the game of Beares and bulls to be bayted", "of suche large compasse, fforme, widenes and height as the plaie housse called the Swan", with "a stage to be carryed or taken awaie and to stand uppon tressels", "the Heavens all over the saide stage, to be borne or carryd without any postes", "two Boxes in the lowermost storie … for gentlemen", "two staircases on the outside", brick foundation "xiij teene ynches at the leaste above the grounde", a tile roof, inner posts "twelve feet high, ten inches square" (first storey), 8 in and 7 in above, an "entrance gate and outbuildings" range 56 × 16 ft, £360 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, Survey of London 22 pp. 66–77; [Wikipedia: Hope Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hope_Theatre), fetched 2026-08-23); the excavated plan: "a polygonal building with ten sides, with an internal diameter of 16m and external diameter of 24m" (CONFIRMED-digest of Historic England 1433280 — the entry itself is Cloudflare-gated: `RINST4-HI-he-hope-1433280.html` is a 5.8 KB challenge page); the bears were "shot to death" in 1656, the last animal sport 1682, Bear Garden Square on the site 1714 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, Wikipedia Hope). (5) **The hall playhouse**: the first Blackfriars (1576–84) in the friary's former BUTTERY, "perhaps 46 feet (14 m) long and 25 feet (7.6 m) wide"; the second (1596, Burbage, the frater for £600) "perhaps 100 feet (30 m) long and 50 wide", playing space "about 69 feet (21 m) long and 46 feet (14 m) wide … including tiring areas", capacity "below 600 to almost 1000", "at least two or three galleries; stage boxes; approximately ten spectators could occupy the stage", CANDLELIT, demolished 6 August 1655 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Blackfriars Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackfriars_Theatre), fetched 2026-08-23); the Cockpit/Phoenix, Drury Lane — a c. 1530–32 round cockpit "about 40 feet (12 m) in diameter" converted by Beeston in 1616, "52 feet (16 m) by 37 feet (11 m)" after expansion, "noticeably smaller than the Blackfriars", attacked by apprentices in March 1617, no plays after 1665 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Cockpit Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cockpit_Theatre), fetched 2026-08-23); the Boar's Head, Whitechapel, 1598 — an INN-YARD with "the initial stage … 39 feet seven inches by 25 feet … positioned in the yard's centre", galleries added 1598 and more in 1599 with a stage roof, "a few feet beyond the ordinary jurisdiction of the lord mayor" (CONFIRMED-digest of Wikipedia/Berry). (6) **The continental line**: the Hôtel de Bourgogne, Paris, 1548, "long and narrow, 102 feet (31 m) by about 42 feet (13 m), and the depth of the stage, about 43 feet (13 m)", a standing pit, "a steep tier of benches on a base of about 10 feet (3.0 m)" at the rear, "two levels of boxes, seven on each side and five at the back for a total of 38"; used to 1783 then "converted to a leather market" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Hôtel de Bourgogne (theatre)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%B4tel_de_Bourgogne_(theatre)), fetched 2026-08-23); the Corral de comedias, Almagro, 1628 — a courtyard originally "622 square metres", now 300 m² / 300 people, two storeys on red-ochred wooden pillars, the upper gallery floor "9 feet 2 inches" above the yard, the "cazuelas" (women's enclosed rooms) on the first floor opposite the stage, "private apartments on both sides of stage", "side corridors between cazuelas and chambers", the stage opposite the entrance "with dressing rooms behind", awnings (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Corral de comedias de Almagro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corral_de_comedias_de_Almagro), fetched 2026-08-23) — the exact Spanish twin of the inn-yard playhouse, in a tenement court; the Teatro Olimpico, Vicenza, 1580–85 (Palladio; opened 3 March 1585 with *Oedipus Rex*): the Roman semicircle "flatten[ed] … into an ellipse" to fit the site, a wood-and-stucco scaenae frons, Scamozzi's "seven hallways" of forced perspective that "recede only a few meters" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Teatro Olimpico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_Olimpico), fetched 2026-08-23 — no dimensions; the cavea has "13 rows of tiered stone benches" — CONFIRMED-digest); the Teatro Farnese, Parma, 1618: "87 metres deep, 32 metres wide and 22 metres high", the stage "40 metres deep with an opening of 12 metres", "more than 3,000 spectators" (CONFIRMED-digest only); the **Teatro San Cassiano, Venice, 1637** — "the world's first public opera house" (*L'Andromeda*), rebuilt after fires in 1629 and 1633, the Tron family's, with **five tiers totalling 153 boxes** — "four tiers of 31 boxes each, plus a first 'ground-floor' tier … of 29 boxes with two side-entrances" — box widths "approximately 95–120 centimetres" (the central box c. 120 cm), second-tier height "slightly less than 2.10 metres", fourth "just over 1.80 metres", proscenium "a little wider than 8 metres", stage depth 6.5 m (9.5 m after 1763), demolished 1812 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Teatro San Cassiano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_San_Cassiano), fetched 2026-08-23). (7) **The Roman ceiling**: Vitruvius Book V — the orchestra circle with "four equilateral triangles" inscribed, the pulpitum "must not exceed five feet, so that those who sit in the orchestra may be enabled to see", seats "not … less than twenty inches in height, nor more than twenty-two … width … not more than two feet and a half, nor less than two feet", the sightline rule ("a line drawn from the first to the last step should touch the front angle of the tops of all the seats"), the site "as healthy as possible … not exposed to the south", bronze sounding-vessels (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Vitruvius V (Thayer, Gwilt tr.)](https://penelope.uchicago.edu/Thayer/E/Roman/Texts/Vitruvius/5*.html), fetched 2026-08-23); Verulamium's theatre (c. AD 140; stage extended c. 180; c. 2,000 seats by c. 300 — one page; "7,000" in a digest — DISCREPANCY carried; "the only example of its kind in Britain, being a theatre with a stage rather than an Amphitheatre"; a rubbish dump in the 4th century) (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Gorhambury: The Roman Theatre](https://www.gorhamburyestate.co.uk/The-Roman-Theatre) and [Wikipedia: Roman Theatre, St Albans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Theatre,_St_Albans), fetched 2026-08-23 — Kenyon's 1935 *Archaeologia* figures not fetched). **Structural imperatives**: SPAN (the polygon's ring of 5-m bays; the hall playhouse's 14–15 m width; the Farnese's 32 m), CROWD (1,400–3,000 in three tiers; Fidenae's AD 27 collapse killed 20,000–50,000 and made the Senate require "a sound foundation … inspected and certified" — family I), FIRE (thatch → tile by contract after 1613), SIGHTLINES (the raked yard; the galleries extended for the new roof), SOUND (Vitruvius's vessels; the hall playhouse's candlelight and enclosure), WEATHER (the stage "heavens"; the corral's awnings), MONEY (one controlled door, the gatherers at the gallery stair, the office). **Siting**: outside the jurisdiction — Bankside (the Clink liberty), Shoreditch, Whitechapel "a few feet beyond" the City — the playhouse is a LIBERTY building like the stew (family F); the hall playhouse in a dissolved friary; the opera house in a patrician palazzo's parish. **Live/work**: Henslowe's office in the playhouse (the money-boxes); no one sleeps there (the tiring house is a store and dressing room). **Prosperity/wear**: floor = the inn yard with a scaffold (family D); rung 1 = the inn yard with added galleries and a stage roof (Boar's Head 1598–99); rung 2 = the polygon of 14 bays, two or three galleries, a trapezoid stage, thatch (Rose I); rung 3 = the 20-sided Globe / square Fortune with tile, gentlemen's rooms, a tiring house, iron-spiked stage rail, a stair turret (≥ c. 1610); the hall playhouse (candles, boxes, 600–1,000) as the WINTER parallel; ceiling = the box-tier opera house (153 boxes in five tiers) and the ducal Farnese. Decline: the 1642 ordinance (every London playhouse), the bears shot 1656, the Bourgogne hall a leather market 1783, the Globe pulled down 1644 — the playhouse is an ORDINANCE-KILLED type.

**(b) Measured.** Rose: 14 bays × 5.0 m; ext. c. 22 m / int. c. 14 m; piers 2.5 m c/c; foundations 0.24 m; yard I 15 × 9 m = 117 m² (400/530), raked 5° / 0.5 m; stage I 5 m deep × 11.5 → 8.2 m (46–48 m²), footing 0.30 m; stage II 5.6 m deep × c. 8.4 m, 2.1 m north; yard II 550/740; gallery depth 10 ft 6 in (3.2 m), benches 13 ft at 18 in pitch, six per bay, 1,404 seats; ingressus 1.47 m; gallery floor 1.0–1.24 m above yard; drip line 0.5 m (LOCAL). Globe: 20 sides, c. 30 m, 3,000, stage 13 × 8 m, 1.5 m high (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). Fortune: §1.4 (PARENT-VERIFIED). Hope: 10 sides, int. 16 m / ext. 24 m (CONFIRMED-digest); posts 12 ft × 10 in; gate range 56 × 16 ft (17 × 4.9 m) (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). Red Lion 1567: stage 40 × 30 ft, 5 ft high (LOCAL citing Loengard). Boar's Head 1598: stage 39 ft 7 in × 25 ft (12.1 × 7.6 m) (CONFIRMED-digest). Blackfriars I: 46 × 25 ft; II: hall 100 × 50 ft, playing space 69 × 46 ft, 600–1,000 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). Cockpit/Phoenix: 12 m round → 52 × 37 ft (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). Bourgogne: 102 × 42 ft hall, stage 43 ft deep, bench base 10 ft, 38 boxes (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). Almagro: 622 m² court, gallery 9 ft 2 in up (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). San Cassiano: 5 tiers, 153 boxes, 95–120 cm wide, 2.10 / 1.80 m tier heights, proscenium > 8 m, stage 6.5 → 9.5 m (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). Farnese: 87 × 32 × 22 m; stage 40 m deep, opening 12 m; 3,000+ (CONFIRMED-digest). Vitruvius: pulpitum ≤ 5 ft; seats 20–22 in high, 24–30 in wide (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). NOT FOUND: the Rose's gallery WIDTH from its own archaeology (the 10 ft 6 in is Gurr/Orrell's inference from the outer and inner walls — internal depth, not a corridor); any stair width (none survive); Verulamium's diameter (Kenyon not fetched; two capacity figures conflict); the Olimpico's cavea width; the Swan's dimensions (de Witt's sketch names no figure in the fetched text).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) Three galleries or two at the Rose (Greenfield vs the model) — unresolved; the grammar should treat storey count as a DRAW within 2–3 for the polygon type. (ii) Gallery capacity is disputed (Gurr's +500 vs MOLA's "little extra space") — the playhouse's prosperity instrument is the YARD, not the galleries. (iii) The negation "the theatre was an inn yard, not a building" holds for the Boar's Head (a working inn yard with a centre stage) and the Spanish corral (a tenement court), and FAILS for the Rose/Globe/Fortune/Hope (purpose-built polygons on contracts) — the catalog's "Permanent performance venues" is right only from 1567 (Red Lion) / 1576 (the Theatre) in England, 1548 in Paris, 1585 in Vicenza, 1637 (opera) in Venice; before those dates the hosted form (family D) is the only one. (iv) The Hope's removable stage makes ONE building two institutions by the day (bears Tuesdays/Thursdays, plays otherwise — PLAUSIBLE schedule, the fact of dual use SUB-LANE CONFIRMED) — a `schedule` on the fixture set, as with the brothel's gate (family F). (v) Thatch vs tile is a dated fire law (1613) inside one type. (vi) The "amphitheater" in the catalog's Bardic college row is a Roman-cavea word; the measured collegiate music hall is NOT in the evidence (the Olimpico is an academy's theatre — the Accademia Olimpica — which is the closest fetched analogue: a learned society's own hall with a permanent scaenae frons).

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** The polygon playhouse: ONE main door (`LOBBY{GATE}` with the gatherer — the Hope's 56 × 16 ft "entrance gate and outbuildings" range is a gate-house RANGE), the `EXTERIOR_WALK{YARD}` as the pit (raked 5°), the `ingressus` — a 1.47-m threshold with steps from yard to lower gallery (a `STAIR_HALL{STEPS}` joint yard → gallery, 1.0–1.24 m rise) — at the building's mid-point on each side; the galleries as a `GALLERY{RING, OPEN_ONE_SIDE, UPPER ×2–3}` of 3.2–3.8 m depth (Rose inference 10 ft 6 in; Fortune contract 12½ ft) that is both seating AND circulation (the CIRC addendum's INN_GALLERY sub-form at its densest); internal stairs few, in one bay near the ingressus or by the stage (PLAUSIBLE), then the EXTERNAL `STAIR_TURRET` from c. 1610 (the Hope's "two staircases on the outside"; the Fortune's tiled staircases) — the playhouse's stair turret is a dated exterior consequence (law 9); the tiring house as a walled-off block of bays behind the stage with its own back door ("back entrance to the tiring house", Rose 4.11.3) and internal stairs to the balcony levels; the gentlemen's rooms/boxes as `CLOSET`-class cells beside the stage reached THROUGH the tiring house (Rose 4.11.2's conjecture). The hall playhouse: a monastic frater/buttery `THROUGH_ROOM` with a stage at one end, galleries on three sides, boxes, candles — CORRIDOR-less. The corral: the tenement court with side corridors "between cazuelas and chambers" — a `CORRIDOR{SIDE}` licensed by the gender partition (the cazuela), 1628. The opera house: the box tiers are a `GALLERY{RING, ENCLOSED}` of 95–120-cm cells (the narrowest cells in the corpus) reached by side corridors and stairs; the "Pepiano" tier's "two side-entrances". The Roman theatre: Vitruvius's radial stairs and the colonnaded passage behind the cavea (the vomitorium belongs to family I). Widths: ingressus 1.47 m; gallery depth 3.2–3.8 m; box 0.95–1.2 m; stair — NONE measured (ledger). Buckets (vetoable): `GALLERY{PLAYHOUSE}` depth 3.2–3.8 m × storey 2.7–3.7 m; `BOX` 1.0–1.2 m × 1.8–2.1 m high; `INGRESSUS` 1.4–1.5 m; `STAIR_TURRET` external, ≥ 1610.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** `STORE{PROPERTY/TIRING}` = the tiring house (costumes, properties, the "heavens" machinery — the Rose's 1595 "throne in the heavens" needed a winch loft: a `VERTICAL{WINDLASS}` fixture over the stage, CIRC addendum §4.1); `CLOSET{OFFICE}` = Henslowe's office (money-boxes broken open and takings recorded — a counting closet, `GUARDIAN_ADJACENT` to the gate); `STORE{BEARS/KENNELS}` at the Hope (family I); `CELLAR`: the Rose's Phase II trap door implies a SUB-STAGE void (R-INST-2 gap 9: a sub-floor cell without walls; the "sub-stage decorations" of 5.8.2); the opera house's scene store behind a 6.5–9.5-m stage; no pantry/buttery (the playhouse sells no food — the "tap house" is the inn's). Prohibition: thatch forbidden by contract after 1613 (a material law, not a cell law).

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: INN_YARD_PLAYHOUSE` (city L2028 floor; the Boar's Head: a working inn yard + centre stage + added galleries — family B's court with a `stage` fixture), `parti: POLYGON_YARD_GALLERIES{sides: 14|20|24, galleries: 2–3, roof: THATCH|TILE}` (Rose/Globe/Hope; yard 9–15 m, ring 22–30 m), `parti: SQUARE_YARD_GALLERIES` (Fortune — §1.4), `parti: HALL_PLAYHOUSE` (Blackfriars: a 14–15-m-wide hall, galleries on three sides, boxes, candles; in a re-used frater — `reclassedTo` from R-INST-3's refectory), `parti: COCKPIT_PLAYHOUSE` (family G's ring, 12 m → 16 × 11 m), `parti: CORRAL` (Spanish: a tenement court with cazuela and side corridors), `parti: ITALIAN_BOX_TIER` (city L2093: a horseshoe/U of 4–5 box tiers × 29–31 boxes, proscenium 8–12 m, deep stage 6.5–40 m — San Cassiano/Farnese), `parti: ROMAN_CAVEA` (Verulamium/Vitruvius: semicircle/ellipse on four triangles, pulpitum ≤ 5 ft, seats 20–22 × 24–30 in — the Bardic college's "amphitheater" is THIS, and the Olimpico is its academy-scale revival). `functions[]`: floor = `yard/pit`, `stage`, `one door + gatherer`; rungs = + `galleries` → + `stage roof/heavens` → + `tiring house` → + `gentlemen's rooms/boxes` → + `external stair turret` → + `trap/sub-stage` → + `winch loft` → + `tile`; ceiling = the box-tier opera house. `fixtures[]`: `STAGE{trapezoid|rectangle; 5 ft high; paled; iron pikes}`, `HEAVENS{posts|postless}`, `TRESTLE_STAGE` (removable — dual use), `BENCHES{13 ft at 18 in}`, `MONEY_BOX`, `FLAGPOLE` (performance signal — Survey of London), `SIGN` (the Rose's), `RAKED_FLOOR{5°}`, `BOXES{95–120 cm}`, `SCAENAE_FRONS`, `PERSPECTIVE_STREETS`, `AWNING` (corral), `CANDLES` (hall). Structural buckets: `openSpan` (ring bays 5 m), `crowdEgress` (one door + ingressi; the Senate's foundation law after Fidenae), `soundShell` (hall/opera), `fireRisk{THATCH→TILE, 1613}`, `sightlines` (raked yard; gallery extension), `heavyFloor` (galleries of 1,400). Licensing: city tier (the catalog is right — every fetched playhouse is metropolitan or a provincial capital's inn yard), era ≥ 1567 (England) / 1548 (Paris) / 1585 (Vicenza) / 1637 (opera), liberty/suburb siting in England, prosperity high (£440–£1,400 builds), `killedBy: ORDINANCE 1642`; culture: the polygon is ENGLISH (no continental polygon fetched), the corral SPANISH, the box tier ITALIAN, the long hall FRENCH. VERDICT: Theaters / Multiple theaters **BUILDING** (polygon/square/hall/corral/cockpit partis; the inn-yard form is HOSTED on family B); Opera house **BUILDING** (`ITALIAN_BOX_TIER`, ≥ 1637, in a patrician palazzo's fabric — often HOSTED in its shell); Bardic college performance hall **BUILDING as a cell of R-INST-3's college** (`ROMAN_CAVEA` or `HALL_PLAYHOUSE` scale; the Olimpico as the academy precedent). HOME: `ROOM_KINDS.dais` is a fixture here (§1.4); NO TYPED HOME for yard, gallery ring, tiring house, box tier, stair turret (five cells); FACET_INFERENCE → `generic` for every theatre row (§Σ).

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) A RING parti with N sides (14/20/24-sided polygons, the 12-m cockpit, the square Fortune) — the grammar needs a polygonal-ring primitive with a bay module (5 m = a rod) and a storey count drawn in 2–3; the inn yard is its degenerate rectangle. (2) Yard RAKE as a floor attribute (5°, 0.5 m) and a gallery floor 1.0–1.24 m above grade — vertical honesty (law 9) inside a single storey. (3) DUAL USE by schedule (the Hope's trestle stage; bears and plays) — a building whose institution FLIPS by day; `hostedIn` with a timetable. (4) The external stair turret as a DATED exterior consequence (c. 1610) — law 9's named massing part with a `mintedBy` year. (5) Box cells of 0.95–1.2 m — a cell width below every bucket in the CIRC addendum (NARROW < 1.8 m) — the opera house forces a `MICRO` cell bucket. (6) Fire law as a material transition inside a type (thatch → tile, 1613) — `materialBy` on the roof, a renovation fossil. (7) Jurisdiction siting (liberty/suburb) again — the same layer family F needs.

**(h) Continental vs English.** The English polygon playhouse (1576–1642) has no continental sibling in the evidence; Spain's corral (1579 Madrid; Almagro 1628) is the inn-yard made permanent without leaving the court; France's Bourgogne (1548) is a long hall with a standing pit, a bench tier and 38 boxes — the hall playhouse at city scale a generation before Blackfriars; Italy invents the academic Roman revival (Olimpico 1585), the ducal perspective theatre (Farnese 1618, 87 m deep) and the PUBLIC box-tier opera house (San Cassiano 1637) that becomes Europe's theatre for 250 years; Rome is the origin (Vitruvius's geometry, Verulamium's provincial cavea). The catalog's "Opera house — High culture. Elite patronage" is right for the court theatres and WRONG for San Cassiano, which was a commercial venture selling box leases to the public — the grammar should carry `patronage ∈ {COURT, ACADEMY, COMMERCIAL}`.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None for the theatres; for the Bardic college the "amphitheater" is the Roman cavea above and the dormitories are R-INST-3's — no magical cell is implied by the catalog text.

## §10 · FAMILY I — arena and fighting: Gladiatorial school (town L1478 "Trains fighters for public spectacle. Recruits from prisoners, debtors, and volunteers. Feeds the arena circuit.") · Fighting pits (city L2052 "Illegal or semi-legal combat. Underground locations.") · Colosseum/arena (city L2068 "Gladiatorial games or monster fights. Massive scale.")

*Provenance: sub-lane HI. Evidence: Wikipedia Colosseum / Ludus Magnus / Chester Roman Amphitheatre / Arles Amphitheatre / Fidenae amphitheatre collapse / Masters of Defence / Fechtschule / Bull Ring Birmingham / Hope Theatre; Survey of London 22 (the Bear Gardens); search digests on the Pompeii gladiators' barracks, the Masters of Defence prizes, bare-knuckle venues; the Caerleon page 404'd.*

**(a) Analogue.** The catalog's three rows are a Roman building type (the arena and its ludus) and a medieval/early-modern NON-building (the pit, the ring, the prize). (1) **The amphitheatre** — the Colosseum (begun AD 72, dedicated 80, altered under Domitian): 189 × 156 m, 48 m high, perimeter 545 m, base 24,000 m²; the arena "87 m (287 ft)" × "55 m (180 ft)" behind a 5-m podium wall; "eighty entrances at ground level, 76 of which were used by ordinary spectators", the four axial entrances for the elite; the vomitoria — "passageways that opened into a tier of seats from below or behind" — allowing evacuation "within only a few minutes"; a "two-level subterranean network of tunnels and cages" (the hypogeum) with "eighty vertical shafts"; seating by class in four tiers plus Domitian's top wooden maenianum for "the common poor, slaves and women"; 50,000–80,000 spectators; last gladiators c. 435, last venatio 523; a chapel by the late 6th century, a Frangipani fortress c. 1200, housing and workshops in the middle ages, the 1349 earthquake and quarrying (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Colosseum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colosseum), fetched 2026-08-23); Arles (AD 90): 136 × 109 m, 21 m high, "over 20,000", 120 arches in two levels, and after Rome "a fortress with four towers" holding "more than 200 houses" and two chapels until cleared 1826–30 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Arles Amphitheatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arles_Amphitheatre), fetched 2026-08-23); Chester (late AD 70s, rebuilt c. 86 and c. 275): 320 × 286 ft (98 × 87 m), 40 ft (12 m) high, 0.85 ha, "could easily seat 8,000", foundations "2.7m wide and 1.3m deep", "exits … at all four points of the compass", a shrine to Nemesis "at the north entrance to the arena", and the 2004–06 finding of "two separate stone amphitheatres" rather than a timber predecessor (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Chester Roman Amphitheatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chester_Roman_Amphitheatre), fetched 2026-08-23); the Fidenae disaster (AD 27): a freedman's "wooden amphitheater, apparently cheaply built" collapsed under 50,000 — Tacitus "50,000 dead or wounded", Suetonius "20,000 dead" — and the Senate then barred anyone "with a fortune of less than 400,000 sestertii from hosting gladiator shows" and required "all future amphitheatres be erected on a sound foundation and inspected and certified" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Fidenae amphitheatre collapse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidenae_amphitheatre_collapse), fetched 2026-08-23, citing Tacitus *Ann.* IV.62 and Suetonius *Tib.* 40) — the first BUILDING CODE in the tranche, and a law-2 economic clamp on who may build. (2) **The ludus** — the Ludus Magnus, Rome (Domitian; rebuilt under Trajan with the pavement "raised by 1.4 m"): a practice arena "roughly 63 m long x 42 m wide" inside a rectangular court with "Tuscan-style colonnades" and "fountains flanking each corner", "living quarters and storage chambers arranged on multiple levels around the arena perimeter", a cavea of "nine tiers" for c. 3,000, and an "underground passage" to the Colosseum "22.17 ft wide" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Ludus Magnus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludus_Magnus), fetched 2026-08-23); the Pompeii "gladiators' barracks" — the theatre's quadriporticus, "approximately 42m x 35m" with 74 Doric columns, "originally designed as a space for the audience to stroll in during the intervals" and "after the earthquake of 62 CE … turned into barracks", cells along the sides (CONFIRMED-digest; cell count and sizes not found). So the gladiatorial school is a COURT-AND-CELLS parti (the han/college/cloister family) with a practice arena where the garth would be, a cavea on its long side, and a tunnel to the arena — the catalog's town-tier "Gladiatorial school" is Roman-register only; the FACET_INFERENCE regex types it `learning` on the word "school" (§0 recon), which is accidentally the nearest existing template (cells round a court). (3) **The medieval and early-modern fighting place has no building**: the Masters of Defence (a corporation of fencing masters "prior to the year 1540" under Henry VIII; Sloane MS 2530 records c. 1540–1590) "played their prizes" — scholar, provost, master — "before an audience in a public venue", specifically at the Bel Savage and the Bull inns in the 1560s–70s, the same inns that were licensed for plays in 1584 (CONFIRMED-digest; SUB-LANE CONFIRMED that the corporation existed and had rules, [Wikipedia: Masters of Defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masters_of_Defence), fetched 2026-08-23 — the page names no venue); the German Fechtschule "does not refer to the 'German school' but to individual fencing competitions", held by the Marxbrüder (imperial privileges 1487/1514) and the Federfechter (Vienna 1570) "in public spaces without dedicated buildings … town halls, inn yards, and market squares during fairs", the last recorded 5 November 1741 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Fechtschule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fechtschule), fetched 2026-08-23); the bull-ring: Birmingham's "ring" was "a hoop of iron in Corn Cheaping to which bulls were tied for baiting before slaughter", first written "the Bulrynge" c. 1529 — a FIXTURE on the market ground (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Bull Ring, Birmingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bull_Ring,_Birmingham), fetched 2026-08-23); the Bankside Bear Gardens: "kennels for dogs", "scaffolded rings for baiting spectators", a 1590 stock of "three bulls, nine bears, one horse, one ape", and the 1583 collapse — "the old and underpropped scaffolds round about the beare garden … overcharged with people fell suddenlie downe, whereby … eight persons men and women were slaine" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, Survey of London 22 pp. 66–77) — then the Hope of 1613–14, the bear garden rebuilt as a dual-use playhouse (family H); bears shot 1656, last animal sport 1682 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, Wikipedia Hope); the 18th-century prize ring: "boxing booths" touring the fairs, "prize rings" in fields and taverns, Molesey Hurst by the Thames as the favoured ground — and the NEGATION "were fights held in cellars?" returned NOTHING: "the search results do not contain specific information about whether bare-knuckle prize fights were held in cellars" (CONFIRMED-digest, 2026-08-23) — the catalog's "Fighting pits … Underground locations" has NO historical instance in this lane's evidence; it is a genre convention (the undercity train's, law 7), and the historical floor is the open field, the inn yard and the fair booth. **Structural imperatives**: CROWD (the Fidenae and 1583 collapses — timber scaffolds fail; the Senate's certification; the Colosseum's 80 doors and vomitoria), SPAN (the cavea on vaults), ANIMALS (kennels, cages, the hypogeum's 80 shafts — an animal store with lifts: `VERTICAL{HOIST}`), SMELL/BLOOD (the arena's sand; siting at the edge — Chester's amphitheatre outside the fortress; Bankside), RELIGION (Nemesis at the north gate; the Colosseum's chapel). **Siting**: the Roman amphitheatre at the fortress/city edge; the bear garden in the liberty; the bull ring in the market; the prize ring in a field or an inn yard. **Live/work**: the ludus houses its fighters in cells round the court (the one residential building in the family); the bear-ward lives at the garden (PLAUSIBLE); no one lives at an arena. **Prosperity/wear**: floor = an iron ring in the market / a field with a rope; rung 1 = a timber scaffold ring round a pit (the bear garden; Henry VIII's 12-m cockpit — family G); rung 2 = the polygonal dual-use playhouse with trestle stage and kennels (Hope, 10 sides, 24 m); rung 3 = the provincial stone amphitheatre (Chester 98 × 87 m, 8,000; Arles 136 × 109, 20,000); ceiling = the Colosseum with hypogeum and the Ludus beside it. Decline (the richest fossil sequence in the tranche): arena → fortress (Arles's four towers, Frangipani c. 1200) → housing (200 houses inside Arles; "housing and workshops" in the Colosseum) → chapel(s) → quarry → cleared monument (Arles 1826–30).

**(b) Measured.** Colosseum 189 × 156 × 48 m; arena 87 × 55 m; podium 5 m; 80 entrances; 80 shafts; 50–80,000. Arles 136 × 109 × 21 m; 120 arches; 20,000+. Chester 98 × 87 × 12 m; 0.85 ha; 8,000; foundations 2.7 × 1.3 m; four exits. Ludus Magnus arena 63 × 42 m; nine tiers; 3,000; tunnel 22.17 ft (6.8 m) wide; pavement raised 1.4 m. Pompeii quadriporticus 42 × 35 m, 74 columns (CONFIRMED-digest). Hope 10 sides, 16/24 m (CONFIRMED-digest); cockpit 12 m (family G, digest). Fidenae 20,000–50,000 dead/hurt; 400,000 HS threshold. 1583: eight dead. 1590 stock: 3 bulls, 9 bears, 1 horse, 1 ape. NOT FOUND: Caerleon (404); any bear-garden ring dimension ("no specific measured dimensions for bear-baiting rings" — Survey of London); the Pompeii cells; any prize-ring or booth size; any "fighting pit" at all.

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) The catalog's "Fighting pits — Underground locations" is a NEGATIVE finding: no fetched source puts a fight underground; the pit is a cockpit (a floor-level ring) or a bear pit (open); the cellar fight is fiction — the grammar may keep it ONLY as an undercity-train room (law 7), never as a surface institution. (ii) "Gladiatorial school" at TOWN tier is anachronistic in every non-Roman register: the medieval fencing school is a master's room in his house or an inn (Bonetti's Blackfriars school — digest-named, not fetched) and the Fechtschule is an EVENT; the ludus is a Roman capital's building. (iii) The Colosseum's arena figure in the sub-lane's own prompt (83 × 48 m) differs from the page's 87 × 55 m — the page is carried, the prompt's figure discarded (a fast-model recall caught by the fetch). (iv) Chester's two capacities ("8,000") and Arles's "over 20,000" are the pages' figures; the Colosseum's 87,000 (historical) vs 50,000 (modern) is flagged by the page itself. (v) The bear garden and the playhouse are ONE building at the Hope — the arena row and the theatre row share a parti at the English floor; the catalog's separate rows are right for Rome and wrong for Bankside.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** The amphitheatre is the corpus's largest CROWD-EGRESS machine: N radial `LOBBY{GATE}` entrances (80 / "four points of the compass"), `CORRIDOR{ANNULAR}` passages on each level (the arcaded ambulatories — 120 arches in two levels at Arles), radial `STAIR_HALL` flights to the `VOMITORIUM` (a passage "from below or behind" into a tier — a typed joint tier ↔ corridor the CIRC addendum lacks), the cavea tiers as `GALLERY{RING, OPEN}` seating by class, the podium wall as a `joint.refused` between arena and seats (5 m high), the axial gates for the elite and the dead (`approach` by rank), the hypogeum as an undercity of its own (two levels, cages, 80 lifts — `VERTICAL{HOIST ×80}`, law 7's graph at a single building), the Ludus's 6.8-m tunnel as a `CORRIDOR{BRIDGE/TUNNEL}` with `joint.target: OTHER_BUILDING` (CIRC addendum §4.3 item 1 — the first underground one). The ludus: a `GALLERY{COURT_RING}` colonnade round the practice arena with cells opening off it (the han/cloister), a cavea on one side. The bear garden / cockpit: a ring of timber scaffolds (`GALLERY{RING}` on props — the collapse hazard), one gate, kennels behind. The prize ring / Fechtschule / bull ring: `EXTERIOR_WALK{GROUND|YARD}` with a rope or an iron ring as the only fixture. Widths: tunnel 6.8 m; podium 5 m; foundations 2.7 m; none for vomitoria or stairs (ledger). Bucket (vetoable): `VOMITORIUM` 1.5–2.5 m (PLAUSIBLE); `ANNULAR_CORRIDOR` 3–5 m (the arcade bay — PLAUSIBLE from 120 arches on a 545-m class perimeter at Arles: c. 3.2 m per arch — a derived figure).

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** `STORE{ANIMALS}` = kennels (bear garden), cages (hypogeum) — a LIVE store with a hoist: lightReq NONE, `REQUIRED_FAR(seating)`, `REQUIRED_NEAR(arena gate)`; `STORE{ARMS}` = the ludus's armamentarium (PLAUSIBLE — not in the fetched page; "storage chambers … around the arena perimeter" is); `STORE{SAND}` (the arena's harena — a fixture yard); the ludus cells are LODGING not storage (the CIRC addendum's attic-cells rule); the Hope's trestle stage stored off-days (a `STORE{STAGE}` — dual use needs a store for the unused institution's fixtures); the bull ring's slaughter link (`REQUIRED_NEAR(shambles)` — baiting "before slaughter"). Prohibition: animals ↔ spectators (the podium; the kennels behind).

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: AMPHITHEATRE_ELLIPSE{axes 98–189 × 87–156 m; tiers by class; gates 4–80; hypogeum?}` (city L2068 — Roman register, or the fantasy "colosseum"), `parti: LUDUS_COURT{arena 63 × 42 m; cell ring; cavea; tunnel to arena}` (town L1478 — Roman capital only; elsewhere **NO_BUILDING**: the fencing master's room in his house/an inn, the Fechtschule event), `parti: SCAFFOLD_RING{pit; timber tiers; kennels}` (the bear garden / cockpit — rung 1–2, 12–24 m; dual-use with family H as `HOPE_DUAL`), `parti: NO_BUILDING{RING_FIXTURE}` (city L2052: the bull ring in the market, the prize ring in a field or inn yard, the booth at the fair — the "pit" is an iron ring or a rope), and `UNDERCITY{FIGHT_ROOM}` as a law-7 graph room ONLY (the catalog's "underground" reading, unattested). `functions[]`: floor = `ring/rope`, `crowd ground`; rungs = + `timber scaffold ring` → + `kennels` → + `gate with gatherer` → + `roof/trestle stage (dual use)` → + `stone cavea` → + `vomitoria + annular corridors` → + `hypogeum + hoists` → + `ludus with cells and tunnel`; ceiling = the Colosseum + Ludus Magnus pair. `fixtures[]`: `IRON_RING` (bull), `ROPE_RING`, `SCAFFOLD_TIERS{underpropped}`, `KENNEL_ROW`, `CAGE+LIFT`, `PODIUM_WALL{5 m}`, `NEMESIS_SHRINE` (a liturgical-functional cell at the gate — deity-doctrine-safe as "a shrine of the games"), `SAND_FLOOR`, `AWNING/VELARIUM` (PLAUSIBLE). Structural buckets: `crowdEgress` (the strongest case in the corpus: certified foundations after AD 27; the 1583 collapse), `heavyFloor/vault`, `animalStore`, `tunnel` (law-7 joint to another building), `bloodSmell` (edge siting), `timberCollapseRisk` (a wear grade: "underpropped"). Licensing: Colosseum/arena — culture ROMAN (or fantasy-Roman), city tier, prosperity very high, and the Fidenae wealth floor (400,000 HS) as a law-2 clamp; Gladiatorial school — Roman register only (NO_BUILDING otherwise); Fighting pits — NO_BUILDING at every tier, era any, licence illegal/semi-legal by the catalog's own text (the 1541 Act's class rule and the bear-baiting suppression 1642/1656 are its dated `killedBy`). VERDICT: town Gladiatorial school **NO_BUILDING / HOSTED** (host: a master's house or an inn; Roman `LUDUS_COURT` as a culture-gated BUILDING); city Fighting pits **NO_BUILDING{RING_FIXTURE}** on ground/yard (the "underground" form is the undercity's, unattested historically); city Colosseum/arena **BUILDING** (`AMPHITHEATRE_ELLIPSE`, Roman/fantasy register; the English floor is the `SCAFFOLD_RING` bear garden = family H's Hope). HOME: NO TYPED HOME for arena, cavea, vomitorium, hypogeum, kennel, ring; FACET_INFERENCE `learning` for the school is wrong-but-near (§Σ).

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) CROWD EGRESS as a validated property (S9): entrances × tiers × vomitoria must reconcile with capacity — the amphitheatre is the type that forces S9 to prove egress, and the Fidenae rule (certified foundations; a wealth floor for the builder) is a law-2 clamp with a historical text behind it. (2) The hypogeum is a building-local UNDERCITY with 80 vertical lifts — law 7's "basements are the graph's projection" meets a building whose basement IS a machine; the undercity train should own it as a typed node (`ARENA_HYPOGEUM`). (3) A tunnel to ANOTHER building (Ludus → Colosseum, 6.8 m wide) — `joint.target: OTHER_BUILDING` below grade. (4) The arena → fortress → housing → chapel → quarry sequence is the longest dated reclassification chain in the program (law 5 over 1,500 years) — a `reclassedTo[]` list, not one event. (5) The catalog's "Fighting pits … Underground" is an owner DATA note: the historical floor is a ring fixture on open ground; the underground pit exists only as genre (R-INST-6 / undercity). (6) The bear garden = playhouse identity (Hope) means two catalog rows can share ONE building by schedule — the `dualUse` relation proposed in family H.

**(h) Continental vs English.** Rome is the only register with the building (the amphitheatre and the ludus; Chester and Caerleon on the British frontier); medieval Europe has the EVENT (Fechtschule in town halls, inn yards and market squares; the English prize at the Bel Savage and the Bull; the Spanish bullring is a post-register 18th-century building — PLAUSIBLE, not fetched) and the FIXTURE (the bull ring); the Bankside bear garden (scaffold ring, kennels, one gate) is England's only purpose-built fighting place and it merged with the playhouse in 1613. The Islamic and Byzantine registers (the hippodrome; the maydan) were not researched — ledgered.

**(i) Fantasy note.** "Monster fights" in the catalog's arena row map onto the venatio and the hypogeum's cages/lifts without a new cell; the "underground" fighting pit is the one catalog claim this family finds NO historical ground for — it belongs to the undercity train's typed rooms (law 7), and R-INST-6 should own its fixture set.
