## §11 · FAMILY J — hospital and healing: Midwife (village L703 "Assists with births… The most-used medical service in any settlement.") · Small hospital (town L1243 "Care for sick poor. Usually religious-run.") · Major hospital (city L1763 "Large facility for sick poor. 50-100 beds.") · Hospital network (metropolis L2322 "Multiple hospitals and infirmaries across districts…")

*Provenance: sub-lane JK (killed 06:13:41Z after 45 tool calls; no report). Evidence: Fox & Brazier 2020 on midwife regulation (LOCAL, `RINST4-JK-fox-brazier-midwives.txt`, 10 pp., the Birmingham repository PDF fetched by curl), the Hospices de Beaune museum page, the Bourgogne-Franche-Comté inventory dossier for Tonnerre, the Great Hospital (Norwich) buildings page, Wikipedia Great Hospital / Ospedale degli Innocenti / St Cross / Browne's Hospital / Fuggerei / Ca' Granda (it), the britishlistedbuildings and britainexpress Chichester pages (saved as `RINST4-JK-7739ec9b.html`, `-d3b4a51f.html`), the Chichester Society page (`-27e01056.html`), and search digests (Beaune, Chichester, Tonnerre, Milan, Fuggerei, Ewelme, Norwich, Bristol/Rasphuis, midwife licensing, the hospital-types negation). Parent §1.5 (Chichester) stands; this section supplies the spans it owed — with one number audit.*

**(a) Analogue.** (1) **The midwife has no building** — and the evidence is a law text: "From early in the 16th century, episcopal licensing required that midwives be in possession of a licence from their diocesan Bishop"; the aspirant "must have undergone a period as Deputy to a licensed midwife", paid "a significant fee", was examined, appeared "before the Bishop or his Chancellor accompanied by the women who had testified in her favour", swore an oath, and "was obliged to retain the licence for display at parish visitations"; the 1511 Act (3 Hen. VIII c. 11) licensed physicians and surgeons by the Bishop of London "within vii myles" or by the diocesan bishop elsewhere and "nowhere in the Act are midwives expressly mentioned", yet three midwives were licensed by the Bishop of London "in the immediate aftermath"; birth "was a social event as much as a medical matter" attended by "birth attendants or gossips" — married women, neighbours, the mother — who "acted as witnesses", cared for the infant and oversaw the four-week "lying-in" in the birthing woman's OWN house (LOCAL, Fox & Brazier, *Medical Law International* 2020, pp. 3–8). The first lying-in HOSPITALS are 1749–1765 (British Lying-In 1749, City of London 1750, Queen Charlotte's 1752, Royal Maternity 1757, General 1765 — CONFIRMED-digest, Wikipedia "British Lying-In Hospital"): post-register. The catalog's "most-used medical service" is right and its interior is the PATIENT'S chamber — NO_BUILDING, a fixture set (birthing stool/chair, the licence on the midwife's own wall) on any dwelling. (2) **The infirmary hall with the chapel at its end** — the English form (parent §1.5): St Mary's, Chichester, "a fine medieval tithe-barn type structure, that consists of a chapel and a series of small self-contained flatlets" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Chichester Society](https://chichestersociety.org.uk/2018/03/30/st-marys-almshouses-a-deadly-tale/), saved page; the 1950s cells "about 10′ x 10 ft … off the nave. There were rows of them each side … dividing partitions but no ceilings" — the parent's digest, now read in the saved page); the listing text: "Almshouses for women. Gatehouse and C14 hall and chapel under the roof. Chimneys and small dwellinghouses of 2 rooms each were inserted in the hall in 1680. Very fine open timber. C14 roof supported on massive wooden posts" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [britishlistedbuildings 101026704](https://britishlistedbuildings.co.uk/101026704-st-marys-hospital-chichester), saved as `RINST4-JK-7739ec9b.html` — the HE original is Cloudflare-gated); the britainexpress page: founded 1158–1170 (plaque) or under Henry II, refounded 1232–48 (statutes survive), the Greyfriars' site taken after 1269 (c. 1290 per Berriman), the building "completed sometime around 1292 … a timber-framed roof supported on massive beams, soaring 40 feet high, with a chapel at one end", visitations of 1382 and 1402 found "failure to provide a weekly payment of a groat to inmates", the warden a priest from 1528, refounded by Elizabeth 1582, the two west bays "pulled down in the late Victorian period" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [britainexpress: St Mary's Hospital Almshouses](https://www.britainexpress.com/counties/westsussex/properties/st-marys-hospital-almshouses.htm), saved as `RINST4-JK-d3b4a51f.html`); and the DIMENSION the parent owed: "The Great Hall is timber framed and consists of six massive oak bays. It is 25 metres wide and was 58 metres long when first built … The chapel at the east end is 14 metres long and is separated from the hall by a carved oak screen" (CONFIRMED-digest, 2026-08-23, of the Sussex Express article — the page itself 403'd; NUMBER AUDIT in (c)). The Great Hospital, Norwich (St Giles, founded 1249 by Bishop Suffield "for aged priests, poor scholars, and impoverished individuals"; "thirty beds were earmarked for the sick poor, and thirteen paupers were to be fed at the hospital gates each day"; four chaplains, a deacon, sub-deacon and master — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Great Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Hospital), fetched 2026-08-23): the infirmary hall of the 1380s "with four bays, a great west window and north and south aisles 20m in length", which "could have easily accommodated about sixteen beds", its "central aisle opening to nave via great archway"; the chancel "finished around 1383", "over 25 metres and five bays in length", with a chestnut ceiling of "at least 252 panels" of eagles for Anne of Bohemia's 1383 visit (the Eagle Ward); a bell tower 1397 (c. £100); a porch with a chantry chapel over; after 1547 "an upper-storey ward for poor men … after 1548; wall inserted between aisle and nave; south aisle lost during Kett's rebellion", the chancel "walled off c. 1570 as separate ward for poor women" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [thegreathospital.co.uk: Medieval Buildings](https://www.thegreathospital.co.uk/history/medieval/buildings.html), fetched 2026-08-23, citing Rawcliffe, *Medicine for the Soul* 1999, p. 62; CONFIRMED-digest for the 1547 civic acquisition and the men/women split). So the English hospital is a CHURCH-SHAPED building — nave = ward, chancel = chapel — with the sick in the nave's aisles seeing the altar, and its later life is PARTITION (Chichester's 1680 two-room houses with chimneys; Norwich's walls of 1548/1570 and upper-storey ward) — the DW-law-5 fossil sequence the parent named. (3) **The French hôtel-Dieu ward**: Beaune (founded 1443 by Nicolas Rolin and Guigone de Salins): the Grande Salle des Pôvres, inaugurated 1452, "50 m de long, 14 m de large, 16 m de haut", "two rows of curtained beds lining the South and North walls, with the central space reserved for tables and benches for meals", "up to 30 beds … often occupied by two people", the chapel "integrated into the Pôvres ward" at its end (the van der Weyden Last Judgment hung there; the foundress buried there), the Salle Saint-Nicolas of 12 beds for both sexes, the kitchen's "Gothic chimney with two hearths", the pharmacy of 130 faience pots (1782), and "the Bouzaize river flowed beneath, providing waste disposal" — a stream under the building as the latrine drain (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Hospices de Beaune: Le musée](https://musee.hospices-de-beaune.com/le-musee), fetched 2026-08-23; the digest gives the same 50 × 14 × 16 m); Tonnerre (founded 1293 by Marguerite de Bourgogne): the salle des malades "88,50 m de long (96 m avec l'abside) … 21.50 metres" wide, "Europe's longest medieval hospital hall", "18 roof trusses", a coffered barrel vault, "four altars (main altar to Virgin Mary; three side altars)", service rooms, a sacristy/"revestiaire", the foundress's "château" lodging where she lived to 1308, a kitchen; in the 17th century "former hall abandoned as humid" for an L-shaped block round a side court, in 1763–67 the Salle Courtanvaux with "new surgical cabinet and council chamber" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Patrimoine Bourgogne-Franche-Comté: ancien hôtel-Dieu Notre-Dame-des-Fontenilles](https://patrimoine.bourgognefranchecomte.fr/dossiers-inventaire/ancien-hotel-dieu-notre-dame-des-fontenilles-actuellement-centre-hospitalier-et-musee), fetched 2026-08-23; the digest's "90 × 18 × 27 m" is the rounder tourist figure — the inventory's 88.5 × 21.5 m is carried; the roof "4,500 m²" and "inverted ship's hull" are digest). (4) **The Italian ospedale**: the Ca' Granda, Milan (first stone 12 April 1456; Filarete left 1465; functioning 1472; work halted 1499; resumed on a 1624 bequest; completed 1805; patients moved out 1939): "a rectangular central courtyard with a church in the center, and two lateral squares subdivided into four smaller courtyards by two crosses with beds for the sick, with men in the right wing and women in the left" — the CROCIERA, "each arm of the cross reserved for a different type of disease", "an altar in the center so they could attend religious functions from their bed", and "hygienic services were located in corridors that ran parallel to the arms of the cross, with a series of doors from the wards allowing access, and drainage occurred directly into the underlying sewage system" (CONFIRMED-digest, 2026-08-23, of lombardiabeniculturali / skuola / divinamilano; SUB-LANE CONFIRMED for the dates and the "almost three hundred meters" facade, [it.wikipedia: Ca' Granda](https://it.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ca'_Granda), fetched 2026-08-23 — "no specific numerical measurements" for the arms). (5) The Roman military valetudinarium and the Byzantine xenon were NOT researched (the sub-lane's budget went to the measured medieval halls) — ledgered; the Islamic bimaristan likewise. **Structural imperatives**: SPAN (14–21.5 m clear for a stone-walled hall; the aisled timber hall divides the span), LIGHT (the great west window; the altar sightline), WATER/WASTE (Beaune's river under the hall; Milan's corridor latrines draining to the sewer — the first hospital PLUMBING in the corpus), HEAT (Chichester's 1680 chimneys came WITH the partition — an open hall cannot be heated per bed), SMELL (Tonnerre's hall "abandoned as humid" in the 17th century). **Siting**: the edge of the town or the gate (the almonry "outside the gate"; Chichester's friary site; Norwich by the river; Beaune in the town; Milan a whole block of 300 m). **Live/work**: the master/warden in a lodging at the gate (St Cross's Beaufort Tower "with the Master's former lodging above" — family K); the chaplains in a range; the sisters/nurses in the hall or a chamber (PLAUSIBLE); the foundress IN the hospital (Tonnerre's "château"; Guigone at Beaune). **Prosperity/wear**: floor (village) = the midwife in the patient's house; rung 1 (town) = a chapel with a few beds in its nave — the "spital" of "about a dozen elderly or sick persons" outside a monastery gate (CONFIRMED-digest, Wikipedia Almonry) or a 16-bed aisled hall (Norwich); rung 2 = the long hall with chapel under one roof, 30 beds (Beaune; Chichester's eight bays), kitchen, pharmacy, store; rung 3 = the 88-m hall with four altars and a founder's lodging (Tonnerre); ceiling = the cruciform multi-ward block with specialised arms, corridor latrines and sewers (Milan). Decline: partition into two-room houses with chimneys (Chichester 1680), walls between ward and chapel (Norwich 1548/1570), an upper floor inserted (Norwich), the hall left for a new block (Tonnerre 17th c.), the hospital made an almshouse (Norwich 1547; Chichester "almshouses for women") — the hospital ALWAYS decays INTO family K.

**(b) Measured.** Beaune salle des pôvres 50 × 14 × 16 m, 30 beds (two to a bed); Saint-Nicolas 12 beds (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). Tonnerre 88.5 × 21.5 m (96 m with apse), 18 trusses; digest 90 × 18 × 27 m high (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED / digest). Norwich infirmary hall four bays, aisles 20 m long, c. 16 beds; chancel > 25 m, five bays; 30 sick-poor beds in the 1249 foundation (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). Chichester: "25 metres wide … 58 metres long when first built", chapel 14 m, six bays now (eight originally), roof "40 feet" (britainexpress) vs "30ft" (parent's digest) vs "over 40'" (the almshouse site, parent §1.5); 1950s cells c. 10 × 10 ft; 1680 houses of 2 rooms each (CONFIRMED-digest / SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). Innocenti loggia 71 m, nine bays, each bay a cube (column height = intercolumniation = arcade width) (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Ospedale degli Innocenti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ospedale_degli_Innocenti); 71 m CONFIRMED-digest). Ca' Granda facade "almost three hundred meters" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). Midwife: licence fee "significant"; lying-in four weeks (LOCAL). PARENT-VERIFIED (this session): Chichester 190 ft original length, 81 ft surviving, roof over 40 ft, side walls 6 ft, 16 aisle windows, 24 chapel stalls, 30 residents original (silvertraveladvisor). NOT FOUND: Chichester's figures from a primary (the HE entry 1026704 403'd three ways; VCH Sussex ii blocked by BHO's CAPTCHA; the Cambridge Eagle Ward recording project PDF 404'd twice — `RINST4-JK-karenlim-eagleward.pdf` is an error page); the Ca' Granda's arm lengths; the Beaune hall's bay count; any bed-spacing figure.

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) NUMBER AUDIT — Chichester "25 metres wide": an aisled timber hall 25 m wide would be wider than Tonnerre's stone hall (21.5 m) and nearly twice Beaune's (14 m); 25 m as a clear TIMBER span is impossible, but as an OVERALL width under one roof it is geometrically consistent with what the parent fetched this session (PARENT-VERIFIED, [Silver Travel Advisor: St Mary's Hospital Almshouses](https://silvertraveladvisor.com/reviews/st-marys-hospital-almshouses/), fetched 2026-08-23 by the parent): "Once 190 feet long, the building is now reduced to 81 feet" (190 ft = 58 m — the digest's length INDEPENDENTLY matched), "timber framed roof over 40 foot high", "roof span slopes down to side walls only 6feet high", "16 small aisle windows", "60,000 handmade clay tiles", a chapel with "24 stalls", and a residence ladder of "up to 30 residents" (original) → 5 (1528 statutes) → 10 (1656) → 20 (1898) → flats 1986/2003/2012. A ridge at c. 12 m over 1.8-m side walls gives a roof rise of c. 10 m, which at a 40° pitch spans c. 24 m — so "25 m wide" reads as the aisled hall's FULL width (nave + two aisles), not a clear span; the 1950s 10 × 10 ft cells "each side" of the nave are the aisles. The CLEAR SPAN of the nave remains NOT FOUND (ledger); the "81 feet" surviving length conflicts with six bays of a 190-ft eight-bay hall (c. 142 ft) and is carried as a discrepancy; the roof-height figures now read 40 ft (two sources) vs 30 ft (one digest). P1c must measure from the RCHME/VCH plan. (ii) The roof-height discrepancy (30 vs 40 ft) stands. (iii) The negation "the hospital had no ward" returns a STRUCTURAL yes: "Contemporaries did not conceive of hospitals as a uniform group … overlapping use of the terms 'church,' 'college,' 'hospital' and 'almshouse'"; leper hospitals housed inmates "in partitioned cells, halls and galleries", and at Harbledown (1084) "lepers lived in timber buildings clustered around the chapel" — NO hall at all; "almshouses were generally smaller and less expensive than hospitals" (CONFIRMED-digest, 2026-08-23, of Roffey 2012 / Lim / the H-Net review of Orme & Webster — the Roffey PDF came back as an HTML error page twice). So the town "Small hospital" has TWO partis: the hall-with-chapel AND the cell-cluster round a chapel (the leper house), and the catalog has no leper-house row (§0.1b) — a §Σ absent-entry note. (iv) The catalog's "50–100 beds" for the city hospital is HIGH for the measured halls (Beaune 30 + 12; Norwich 30 / 16; Chichester's eight bays at two rows PLAUSIBLE 24–32) and RIGHT only at the Milan scale; a "Major hospital" in the English register is 30 beds. (v) "Usually religious-run" is right to 1547 and then INVERTS: Norwich was bought by the city (1547), Bristol's 1696 Corporation ran its workhouse-hospital (family K) — the run-by field is ERA-gated at the Reformation. (vi) The Beaune river-under-the-hall and the Milan sewer corridors are the COUNTER to the English hall's total lack of plumbing — a culture/prosperity gate on the latrine, not a universal law.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** The hall hospital is a single `THROUGH_ROOM` of church shape: porch (`LOBBY{PORCH}` with a chantry chamber over at Norwich — the porch-chamber module of the CIRC addendum) → nave/ward (aisled: the CENTRAL aisle is the circulation, the side aisles the bed rows — an aisled hall is a THROUGH_ROOM with an internal `CORRIDOR{CENTRAL}` made of posts, not walls) → screen → chapel; the sightline bed → altar is a `visibility` requirement (every bed sees the altar — the grammar's first SIGHTLINE constraint inside a cell). Beaune: the hall with two bed rows against the long walls and "the central space reserved for tables and benches" — the centre is dining AND circulation. Milan: the crociera — four ward-arms radiating from a central altar (a `CROSS` of four THROUGH_ROOM halls meeting at a `LOBBY{ALTAR}`), with `CORRIDOR{SERVICE}` running PARALLEL to each arm carrying the latrines ("a series of doors from the wards") — a double-loaded service corridor licensed by PROGRAM in 1456, the earliest dated hospital corridor in the corpus (the CIRC addendum's "hospitals license it earlier by their own program" now has a date and a mechanism); the courtyards as `EXTERIOR_WALK{COURT_RING}` with loggias (`GALLERY{ARCADE}`). Innocenti: the nine-bay street `GALLERY{ARCADE}` (71 m) as the public face; separate men's and women's cloisters; "a basement with cryptoporticus beneath cloister walks" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED) — a law-7 undercroft under the circulation. The fossil: Chichester's two-room houses "inserted in the hall" turn the nave into a `CORRIDOR{SPINE}` between rows of cells (the 1950s "rows of them each side") — a through-room that BECOMES a corridor by partition, the mirror of the stack-into-passage mechanism (CIRC addendum §4.3 item 8). Midwife: the patient's house. Widths: Beaune 14 m hall; Norwich aisles 20 m long; the central aisle width NOT FOUND (ledger); the Milan corridor width NOT FOUND. Buckets (vetoable): `WARD_HALL` width 12–22 m × length 20–90 m; `BED_BAY` 7 m (Chichester 58 m / 8 bays) — PLAUSIBLE; `CENTRAL_AISLE` 3–5 m (PLAUSIBLE); bed pitch c. 1.7–2.0 m along the wall (Beaune: 30 beds on two 50-m walls = c. 3.3 m per bed WITH curtains — a derived figure).

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** `STILL_ROOM` = the **apothicairerie** (130 pots, alembics — Beaune; the hospital's pharmacy is the still-room class at institutional scale; R-INST-2 §4's apothecary lab); `PANTRY/BUTTERY/KITCHEN` = the two-hearth Gothic kitchen (Beaune) feeding 30 beds + "100 men at the gates" (St Cross's 15th-century kitchen — family K); `CLOSET{SACRISTY}` = the "revestiaire" (Tonnerre) — the chapel's vestment store, liturgical-functional only; `STORE{LINEN}` (curtains, sheets — Beaune's curtained beds; a linen room PLAUSIBLE); `CELLAR/UNDERCROFT` = the Innocenti's cryptoporticus; `STORE{GRAIN/ALMS}` = the almonry's alms store at the gate (the 13 paupers fed daily at Norwich; 100 at St Cross); the "audit room" (Browne's, family K) as `CLOSET{MUNIMENT}`; the 1763 "surgical cabinet and council chamber" (Tonnerre) — late cells. Adjacency: kitchen `REQUIRED_NEAR(hall)` but `REQUIRED_FAR(chapel)`; pharmacy `REQUIRED_NEAR(hall)`; the latrine over water (`REQUIRED_OVER(stream)` — Beaune; R-INST-1's reredorter rule); sacristy `REQUIRED_NEAR(altar)`. Prohibition: lepers, madmen, the infectious and "the incontinent were excluded" from Ewelme (family K) — a person rule that becomes a separate BUILDING (the leper house).

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: NO_BUILDING{MIDWIFE}` (village L703: a licence + a fixture set — birthing chair, linen, the oath — on the patient's chamber; `hostedIn: DWELLING(patient)`, the first hosted function whose host CHANGES per event); `parti: CHAPEL_WITH_BEDS` (town L1243 floor: a chapel/spital of c. 12 beds at a gate — NO separate hall; or the leper `CELL_CLUSTER_ROUND_CHAPEL`); `parti: INFIRMARY_HALL_CHAPEL_END` (town/city: aisled or clear-span hall 14–22 m × 20–90 m, screen, chapel, porch, kitchen, pharmacy; 16–30 beds; the English/Burgundian form); `parti: CROCIERA` (city L1763 ceiling: four ward arms on a central altar, service corridors with latrines, courtyards, men/women halves — Milan); `parti: HOSPITAL_NETWORK` (metropolis L2322: N hospitals by district — Milan's own project absorbed the city's small hospitals into one "Ca' Granda", so the network CONSOLIDATES at the ceiling; PLAUSIBLE from the digest's "per ospitare" context, not a fetched claim). `functions[]`: floor = `beds + altar`; rungs = + `screen` → + `porch` → + `kitchen` → + `warden's lodging` → + `pharmacy` → + `sacristy` → + `bell tower` → + `gender split (two halls / two crosses)` → + `latrine over water` → + `service corridors`; ceiling = the crociera. `fixtures[]`: `BED{curtained; 2 persons}`, `ALTAR_END`, `SCREEN{oak, carved}`, `GREAT_WEST_WINDOW`, `EAGLE_CEILING` (a dated patronage fixture), `POTS{130}`, `ALEMBIC`, `TWO_HEARTH_CHIMNEY`, `ROTISSERIE` (1698 — late), `LICENCE_ON_WALL` (midwife). Structural buckets: `openSpan{14–22 m}`, `aisled`, `sightline(bed → altar)`, `waterUnder` (Beaune), `sewer` (Milan — a law-7 joint), `partitionable` (the hall that becomes cells — a wear/era grade), `heat: NONE → chimneys on partition`. Licensing: town Small hospital tier ≥ town, era 1084 (Harbledown) onward, religious-run to 1547 then civic; city Major hospital tier city, 30 beds English / 30 + 12 Burgundian / hundreds Milanese (culture-gated), era ≥ 1249 (Norwich) / 1293 (Tonnerre) / 1443 (Beaune) / 1456 (Milan); metropolis network = consolidation; midwife any tier, licence ≥ c. 1512 (England), NO_BUILDING always. VERDICT: Midwife **NO_BUILDING** (every tier; host = the patient's dwelling); Small hospital **BUILDING** (`CHAPEL_WITH_BEDS` or `INFIRMARY_HALL_CHAPEL_END` at the small end; the leper cell-cluster as the second parti); Major hospital **BUILDING** (`INFIRMARY_HALL_CHAPEL_END` → `CROCIERA` by culture/prosperity); Hospital network **BUILDING ×N** (a district roster, or ONE consolidated crociera at the Milanese ceiling). HOME: `heals` variant adds `quarters` (bed/shelf/cauldron) — the right FIXTURE set but the wrong parti (the hospital is a HALL, not a room appended to a `generic` building); NO TYPED HOME for ward, chapel-end, pharmacy, porch-chamber, service corridor; FACET_INFERENCE `generic`+`heals` (§Σ).

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) A SIGHTLINE constraint inside a cell (every bed sees the altar) — the first `visibility` rule on fixtures within a THROUGH_ROOM; it also explains the crociera (four arms on one altar maximise beds-with-sightline). (2) The hall → cells transition (Chichester 1680; Norwich 1548/1570) is the cleanest "partition mints a corridor" fossil in the program — the CIRC addendum's `mintedBy` gains a PARTITION event that converts a THROUGH_ROOM's central aisle into a CORRIDOR{SPINE} and adds chimneys (heat arrives WITH walls: law 1's "prosperity buys walls first" running in reverse as decline). (3) The hospital's service corridor (Milan 1456) is a dated, program-licensed CORRIDOR with latrines — the addendum's "hospitals license it earlier" now has its primary. (4) A hosted function whose HOST is the patient's house (the midwife) — `hostedIn` must accept a per-event target, not a fixed building. (5) The catalog's bed counts should be culture-banded (English 16–30 / Burgundian 30–42 / Milanese hundreds), and "religious-run" should be an era-gated `runBy` field. (6) Absent catalog rows the evidence demands: the leper house (cell cluster round a chapel) and the lying-in hospital (post-1749, out of register).

**(h) Continental vs English.** England: the church-shaped aisled hall with a chapel under one roof, 16–30 beds, unplumbed, partitioned into cells after the Reformation; Burgundy/France: the clear-span stone hall of 14–21.5 m × 50–90 m with curtained beds, a river or drain beneath, a pharmacy and a kitchen as named cells (Beaune, Tonnerre); Italy: the civic ospedale as a city block — the crociera with specialised arms, corridor latrines and a sewer (Milan), the loggia'd foundling hospital (Florence, family K); the Islamic bimaristan and the Byzantine xenon (PLAUSIBLE: courtyard wards by ailment, a pharmacy, a lecture hall) and the Roman valetudinarium (a double-corridor ring of small wards round a court — PLAUSIBLE) were NOT fetched and are ledgered as the setting-agnostic ceiling. The catalog's "Hospital network" is Milanese in spirit: Francesco Sforza's 1456 project consolidated the city's hospitals into one — the network becomes a building.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None (a divine healer is R-INST-3/5's spell service — §0.1b; the hospital's chaplain-physician is the historical floor of that entry).

## §12 · FAMILY K — almshouse, foundling home, workhouse: Almshouse (town L1250 "Charitable house for the destitute poor, aged, and disabled who cannot work… in return they pray for their benefactors.") · Foundling home (city L1770 "Receives abandoned infants… The wheel in the wall allows anonymous deposit at night.") · Workhouse (city L2218 — the poverty half; R-INST-1 §7 holds the carceral half)

*Provenance: sub-lane JK. Evidence: Wikipedia Hospital of St Cross / Browne's Hospital Stamford / Fuggerei / Ospedale degli Innocenti; the St Cross history page; the Ewelme village-history page; workhouses.org.uk (Bristol); and search digests (Ewelme 1437; Fuggerei; Bristol 1696 / Amsterdam Rasphuis 1596; almshouse names and the almonry). The Historic England disability-history page 403'd.*

**(a) Analogue.** (1) **The almshouse is the hospital's successor and its decay product** (family J): medieval "hospitals, Maison Dieu, bede-houses, lazar houses, and Godshouses" are one vocabulary; "the word almshouse was not used until the 14th century"; the oldest are St Oswald's, Worcester (c. 990) and St Cross, Winchester (1130s) "for thirteen poor impotent men"; the ALMONRY is "the place or chamber where alms … were distributed to the poor in churches or other ecclesiastical buildings", and "in some cases the duty to take in the poor and the sick was met by setting up a hospitium (inn) or spital (hospital) outside the gate in which about a dozen elderly or sick persons were maintained" (CONFIRMED-digest, 2026-08-23, of Wikipedia Almshouse / Almonry and almshouse-charity pages). The measured English partis are three. (a) **St Cross, Winchester** (1132–36, Henry of Blois: "thirteen poor men, so frail that they were unable to work, and to feed one hundred men at the gates each day"; Cardinal Beaufort's "Order of Noble Poverty" in the 15th century "added the Almshouse to the existing Hospital buildings" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Hospital of St Cross: History](https://www.hospitalofstcross.co.uk/history), fetched 2026-08-23): "main set of two-storeyed lodgings" for the brethren (1445 onward) on the north-west and west sides, "tall, regularly spaced chimneys and doorways, each leading to four sets of apartments" — a STAIRCASE MODULE of four lodgings per door (the barracks/college module of the CIRC addendum, here for the poor); the Brethren's Hall (14th c., north range) "large enough for the Brethren and 100 poor men", with "a timber screen with gallery above", an arch-braced roof, "a central hearth and a dais"; the Beaufort Tower (c. 1450), "a three-storey structure … serving as gatehouse with the Master's former lodging above"; a 15th-century kitchen sized "to produce food for thirteen poor men and 100 men at the gates"; an "ambulatory" in "the eastern or infirmary range"; the 12th–13th-century church; 25 brothers today (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Hospital of St Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospital_of_St_Cross), fetched 2026-08-23). (b) **Ewelme** (licensed July 1437, William and Alice de la Pole, "a Charity of Two Chaplains and Thirteen Poor Men"; the two priests at £10 each, one the Almshouse Master and one the Grammar Master; "lepers, madmen, those with infectious diseases or the incontinent were excluded"; residents chosen for "piety and sobriety" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Ewelme Village History: Almshouses](https://www.ewelmevillagehistory.uk/almshouses), fetched 2026-08-23): "accommodation for 13 almsmen, ranged around a courtyard and accessed from a covered walkway with a pentice roof"; "a quadrangle surrounded by a cloister walk with a well in the centre of the square. Each man has two rooms allotted to him"; "Eleven of the Units comprised a Single Upstairs & Downstairs Room linked probably by a recessed Stair or Ladder, with a Stone window (possibly glazed) in the External Wall, a Timber-mullioned Window to the Cloister & a Fireplace" (CONFIRMED-digest, 2026-08-23, of the VCH Oxon xviii / Ewelme charity / fordsfarm pages — the VCH page itself not opened). (c) **Browne's Hospital, Stamford** (1475/1485, William Browne, wool merchant; "ten poor men and two poor women" plus a Warden and Confrater, later twelve men and two women): a chapel at the east end with original glass, "a common room where residents lived", an "audit room", the "confrater's sitting room", a courtyard — the COMMON-HALL type where the poor lived in cubicles off one hall with the chapel beyond (the sub-lane's prompt; the page confirms hall + chapel + audit room + court; the cubicles are PLAUSIBLE from the type), the cottages made flats in 1963 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Browne's Hospital, Stamford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Browne%27s_Hospital,_Stamford), fetched 2026-08-23). The continental ceiling is the **Fuggerei, Augsburg** (founded 1516 by Jakob Fugger; built 1514–23; "52 houses" by 1523; today 67 houses with 142–147 flats and a church of 1582; rent "1 Rhenish gulden" a year — €0.88 — "plus three daily prayers"; "approximately 150 needy Augsburg citizens of the Catholic faith"; flats of "45–65 square metres", each "a kitchen, a parlour, a bedroom and a tiny spare room, altogether totaling about 60 square metres"; "the identical red-roofed buildings are all two floors with an apartment on each floor", ground-floor flats with "a small garden and garden shed", upper with "an attic"; "five gates locked daily at 10 PM"; one well; an administrative building; Mozart's great-grandfather 1681–94 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Fuggerei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuggerei) and [fugger.de](https://www.fugger.de/en/fuggerei), fetched 2026-08-23; the 45–65 m² and room list also CONFIRMED-digest). So the almshouse has FOUR partis — the chapel-with-common-hall-and-cubicles (Browne's; Chichester's afterlife), the two-room units round a cloistered court (Ewelme), the staircase-module lodgings with a gatehouse and a great hall (St Cross), and the walled STREET-TOWN of identical two-flat houses with timed gates (Fuggerei) — and the catalog's "in return they pray for their benefactors" is literally the Fuggerei's rent. (2) **The foundling home**: the Ospedale degli Innocenti, Florence — commissioned 1419 by the Arte della Seta from Brunelleschi, built 1419–27, widened by a bay 1430, an attic storey 1439, opened 5 February 1445 ("first infant abandoned"), closed 1875; the nine-bay loggia (71 m — CONFIRMED-digest) on the piazza, "each bay a cube" (column height = intercolumniation = arcade width; entablature half the column); "men's and women's separate cloisters", "a basement with cryptoporticus beneath cloister walks", and the deposit mechanism: "a 'pila' basin and later the rotating wheel ('ruota') for anonymous infant placement" — the basin "replaced with rotating wheel mechanism" in **1660** (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia: Ospedale degli Innocenti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ospedale_degli_Innocenti), fetched 2026-08-23; "dormitories, wet nurses' quarters, and church are not specifically detailed"). The catalog's "wheel in the wall" is therefore RIGHT for Florence after 1660 and for the Italian ruota generally (PLAUSIBLE earlier elsewhere — not fetched); before 1660 the Innocenti used an open BASIN under the loggia — a fixture, not a wall-cell. (3) **The workhouse (poverty half)**: the Amsterdam Rasphuis, "the first prison-workhouse for men, opened in Amsterdam in 1596 … in the former Convent of the Poor Clares on the Heiligeweg", with an inner courtyard watched by a statue of Castigatio and "on the east side of the inner courtyard … a hall known synonymously as the 'church' and the 'school,' where inmates attended religious services" (CONFIRMED-digest, 2026-08-23, of Wikipedia Rasphuis / canvasjournal) — a convent re-used; the Bristol Corporation of the Poor (1696, eighteen parishes incorporated under a Local Act; John Cary's 1700 *Account*): the "New Workhouse" rented in 1698 for "a hundred pauper girls", and the "Mint Workhouse" — "a house at the south of St Peter's church once owned and largely reconstructed by sugar merchant Robert Aldworth. The building had recently been occupied by the Treasury as a mint" — taking "a hundred boys" in August 1698 plus the elderly and young children, c. 170 inmates combined; "segregated dormitories, work rooms, dining areas, and medical spaces"; girls spinning under "Tutresses" and schooled, boys spinning cotton and weaving fustians (£6 a week); staff "a Master, Mistress, Tutresses, School-Mistress, and porter" under sixty Guardians (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [workhouses.org.uk: Bristol](https://www.workhouses.org.uk/Bristol/), fetched 2026-08-23); a later description of the Bristol house: "a passage running directly through it, branching off right and left, and dividing the men's from the women's portion of the house, and effecting a complete separation of the sexes … The day-rooms and dormitories of the aged and infirm of both sexes are placed facing the south; the floors of their rooms are boarded, they have separate staircases" (CONFIRMED-digest — 19th-century in tone; the date of the description was not given). So the early workhouse is a RE-USED great house (a sugar merchant's house that had been a mint; a convent) with a SEX-SPLITTING SPINE passage, dormitories, work rooms, a dining hall and a school/chapel — the carceral discipline (R-INST-1 §7) sits on a merchant-house or convent parti. **Siting**: the almshouse by the church (Ewelme's almshouse, church and school are one group) or at the gate; the Fuggerei a walled quarter inside the town; the foundling hospital on a piazza; the workhouse in a re-used town house. **Live/work**: the almsmen in their units; the master/warden at the gate (St Cross's tower; Browne's warden; Ewelme's two priests); the Fuggerei's administrator in its own building; the workhouse's Master and Mistress resident. **Prosperity/wear**: floor (town) = "about a dozen" poor in a spital at a gate, or the hospital's nave partitioned into cells (Chichester 1680 — the almshouse as the hospital's decay); rung 1 = thirteen two-room units round a cloistered court with a well, a chapel, a master's house (Ewelme); rung 2 = lodgings in four-unit stair modules + gatehouse tower + great hall + kitchen for 100 + church (St Cross); ceiling = the walled town of 52 houses with five gates and its own church (Fuggerei). Foundling: basin under a loggia → wheel in the wall (1660) → cloisters by sex + wet-nurses' quarters. Workhouse: a re-used house with a spine passage. Decline: the almshouse SURVIVES (Ewelme, St Cross, Browne's, the Fuggerei are all still running — the most durable type in the tranche); the workhouse is the 1834 Union's.

**(b) Measured.** Fuggerei flats 45–65 m² (c. 60 m² typical: kitchen, parlour, bedroom, spare room); 52 houses (1523) / 67 houses, 142–147 flats; two storeys, one flat each; five gates, 22:00 curfew; rent 1 gulden (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). St Cross: 13 → 25 brethren; 100 fed at the gate; four apartments per stair door; three-storey gate tower (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). Ewelme: 13 men, two rooms each (up + down), eleven such units; a well in the court (CONFIRMED-digest). Browne's: 10 + 2 → 12 + 2 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). Innocenti: loggia 71 m / nine cube bays; wheel 1660 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED / digest). Bristol 1698: 100 girls + 100 boys; c. 170 inmates; £6/week fustians; 60 Guardians (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). Norwich 1249: 13 paupers fed daily (family J). NOT FOUND: Ewelme's quadrangle or cloister-walk width and unit sizes (the village page has none; the VCH not opened); St Cross's lodging room sizes; the Rasphuis court; Chichester's 1680 house sizes (the 1950s "10 × 10 ft" cells stand in).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) The negation "no almshouse before 1550 — the church house/guild/almonry housed the poor" is PARTLY confirmed: the almonry and the gate-spital are the floor, the guild's maison-dieu is the town form (PLAUSIBLE — the Berkshire/guild almshouses were not fetched), and the named almshouses (St Cross 1130s; Ewelme 1437; Browne's 1475) are the rungs — the catalog's town-tier almshouse at baseChance 0.3 is RIGHT for 1437+ and HIGH before. (ii) The workhouse has NO pre-1596 building and NO purpose-built English one before the 18th century (Bristol re-used a sugar house; "houses of correction" from 1576 are R-INST-1's) — the catalog's city-tier placement is right, its "Infrastructure" shelf wrong-but-harmless. (iii) The Fuggerei is NOT an almshouse in the English sense (no common hall, no chapel until 1582, families not single almsmen, a RENT however small) — it is social HOUSING, DWR1A's row house at institutional scale; the catalog's single "Almshouse" row spans both. (iv) The "wheel in the wall" is a 1660 Florentine fixture; the 1445 Innocenti had a BASIN (pila) — the catalog's text is right only from 1660 in the one fetched case. (v) "Disabled who cannot work" vs Ewelme's EXCLUSION of "lepers, madmen, those with infectious diseases or the incontinent" — the almshouse selected the RESPECTABLE poor; the excluded went to the leper house (family J) or nowhere. (vi) The almshouse's durability (all four fetched examples still operating) is the counter to the whole tranche's decline stories — an ENDOWMENT keeps a building in its original use for 500–900 years, which law 5 must be able to express as a zero-renovation anchor.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** Ewelme: `GALLERY{CLOISTER_WALK}` with a pentice roof round a square court ("accessed from a covered walkway") — the inn gallery's ground-floor sibling — giving onto 13 two-room units each with an internal `VERTICAL{LADDER}` or "recessed stair" up to the chamber (the poverty-floor stair of the CIRC addendum, inside an endowed building); the well at the centre (`VERTICAL{SHAFT}`); the units open ONLY to the cloister (`approach: SINGLE`). St Cross: `STAIR_HALL{MODULE}` — one door → four apartments (the CIRC addendum's MODULE sub-form from Berwick barracks, here c. 1445 — its earliest dated civilian instance); a `LOBBY{GATE}` three-storey tower with the master above (`REQUIRED_ABOVE(gate)` = guardian-adjacent); the Brethren's Hall with `SCREENS_PASSAGE` and a `GALLERY{GALLERY_OVER}` "above" the screen; an `ambulatory` (a `GALLERY{CLOISTER_WALK}` along the infirmary range). Browne's: a `THROUGH_ROOM` common hall with cubicles (the hospital nave at small scale) → chapel; the audit room off the hall. Fuggerei: `EXTERIOR_WALK{STREET ×N}` inside a walled enclave with five `LOBBY{GATE}` cells that are `severable` on a SCHEDULE (22:00 — the brothel's timed gate (family F) in a charitable register); each house a two-flat stack with its own street door (no shared stair recorded — PLAUSIBLE separate entries). Innocenti: the nine-bay `GALLERY{ARCADE}` as the street front and the deposit point (the basin/wheel is a joint THROUGH the wall with NO door — `joint.target: EXTERIOR`, `severable: rotating`); two cloisters by sex; a cryptoporticus under the walks. Bristol workhouse: a `CROSS_PASSAGE{SPINE}` "running directly through it, branching off right and left" as the sex barrier, "separate staircases" for each side — the corridor-as-segregation licence (the CIRC addendum's prison/barracks program licence) on a merchant-house parti. Widths: NONE measured for any cloister walk, stair or spine (ledger); buckets by analogy to the CIRC addendum: cloister walk 2.5–4 m; the Fuggerei's street PLAUSIBLE 4–6 m.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** The almsman's unit: two rooms and a fireplace — storage is a chest (fixture); the Fuggerei flat's "tiny spare room" (Kammer) is a `CLOSET` at 60 m² — the smallest endowed closet in the corpus — plus a garden shed (`STORE{GARDEN}`) and an attic (`ATTIC/GARRET_STORE`); the institution's `PANTRY/BUTTERY/KITCHEN` scaled to the dole (St Cross: 13 + 100 at the gate — the almonry's dole is a STORE{ALMS} function at the gate: bread, ale, the "Wayfarer's Dole"); `CLOSET{AUDIT/MUNIMENT}` (Browne's audit room — the charity's deeds; R-INST-1's muniment bundle at small scale); the Innocenti's wet-nurses' quarters and linen (NOT detailed — ledger); the workhouse's `STORE{RAW MATERIAL}` (cotton wool, yarn) and `STORE{PRODUCT}` (fustians) beside the work rooms — a process store pair (R-INST-2's textile cells inside a poor-law building). Adjacency: the chapel `REQUIRED_NEAR(common hall)` (Browne's; Chichester) and sightline from cubicles (family J); the master's lodging `GUARDIAN_ADJACENT(gate)`; the kitchen `REQUIRED_NEAR(hall, gate-dole)`. Prohibition: the Ewelme exclusions (persons); the Fuggerei's confession and residence rules (persons); none architectural.

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: GATE_SPITAL` (town floor: a dozen poor in a hall at a gate or in a partitioned hospital nave — `reclassedTo` from family J), `parti: CLOISTERED_COURT_UNITS{13 × (2 rooms, stair, hearth); well; chapel; master's house}` (Ewelme — the canonical English almshouse), `parti: STAIR_MODULE_LODGINGS{4 per door} + GATE_TOWER + GREAT_HALL + KITCHEN{dole}` (St Cross — the college form for the poor), `parti: HALL_WITH_CUBICLES + CHAPEL` (Browne's; Chichester after 1680), `parti: WALLED_ROW_TOWN{N houses × 2 flats; gates with curfew; church; well; admin}` (Fuggerei — continental ceiling; city tier), `parti: FOUNDLING_LOGGIA{street arcade; basin|wheel; two cloisters by sex; wet-nurse range; cryptoporticus}` (city L1770), `parti: REUSED_HOUSE_WORKHOUSE{spine passage by sex; dormitories; work rooms; dining; school/chapel; separate stairs}` (city L2218 poverty half; the carceral half is R-INST-1 §7's). `functions[]`: almshouse floor = `cells + prayer`; rungs = + `common hall` → + `chapel` → + `cloister walk` → + `well` → + `master's lodging at gate` → + `kitchen with dole` → + `audit room` → + `church`; ceiling = the walled town. Foundling floor = `basin under a loggia`; rungs = + `wheel` (1660) → + `cloisters by sex` → + `wet-nurse range`. Workhouse floor = `a re-used house + spine passage`; rungs = + `work rooms by sex` → + `school` → + `medical room`. `fixtures[]`: `HEARTH_PER_UNIT`, `CLOISTER_WINDOW{timber-mullioned}`, `EXTERNAL_STONE_WINDOW`, `WELL_CENTRAL`, `DOLE_HATCH/GATE`, `CHIMNEY_ROW{regular}`, `DOORBELL{unique}` (Fuggerei), `GATE_LOCK{22:00}`, `PILA_BASIN` → `RUOTA_WHEEL`, `SPINNING_WHEELS`, `FUSTIAN_LOOMS`, `CASTIGATIO_STATUE` (the Rasphuis court — a moral fixture). Structural buckets: `endowed` (a zero-renovation anchor — law 5's strongest stability), `enclave` (walls + gates), `rowRepeat` (52 identical houses — R-INST-2 gap 3 at its largest), `spineSplit` (sex segregation), `reuse` (convent/merchant house). Licensing: Almshouse tier ≥ town, era ≥ 1130s (St Cross) with the quadrangle form ≥ 1437, prosperity by ENDOWMENT not by the settlement's economy (a founder's gift — the three-clamp ceiling's economic clamp is BYPASSED by endowment: law 2 needs an `endowed` exception); Foundling home tier city, era ≥ 1445 (Florence; the wheel ≥ 1660), culture Italian/Catholic (PLAUSIBLE elsewhere); Workhouse tier city, era ≥ 1596 (Amsterdam) / 1696 (Bristol), re-used house until the 18th century. VERDICT: Almshouse **BUILDING** (four partis by culture/prosperity; the GATE_SPITAL floor is HOSTED on a hospital or a gate); Foundling home **BUILDING** (`FOUNDLING_LOGGIA`, Italian register; elsewhere HOSTED on a hospital — PLAUSIBLE); Workhouse **BUILDING by RE-USE** (`REUSED_HOUSE_WORKHOUSE`: the parti is a merchant house's or a convent's with a spine passage added — `upgradeOf: DWELLING|CONVENT`). HOME: NO TYPED HOME for cloister walk, almsman's unit, dole gate, wheel, spine passage, work room; the `heals` variant's `quarters` is the nearest fixture set for the almsman's cell; FACET_INFERENCE `generic`+`heals` for the almshouse (the "healing" tag), `generic` for the others (§Σ).

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) ENDOWMENT as a prosperity source outside the settlement's economy — law 2's economic clamp needs an `endowed` exception, and law 5 needs a zero-renovation anchor that holds 500–900 years (every fetched almshouse still runs). (2) The stair MODULE (four apartments per door, c. 1445) and the cloister-walk-to-units plan (1437) are the two CIVILIAN ancestors of the barracks/college modules — the CIRC addendum's MODULE sub-form should cite St Cross, not Berwick, as its first instance. (3) A joint THROUGH a wall with no door (the foundling wheel) — `joint.target: EXTERIOR, severable: rotating, passage: OBJECT_ONLY` — a new joint kind the contracts cannot express. (4) A walled ENCLAVE of identical houses with timed gates (Fuggerei) — the N-identical-buildings generator (R-INST-2 gap 3) plus the timed severable gate (family F) plus an enclave boundary (the liberty layer of family F/H) in one parti. (5) The workhouse as RE-USE with a spine passage is `upgradeOf: DWELLING` (family C's mechanism) applied to a great house — the grammar's renovation narrative covers it without a new parti. (6) The almshouse as the hospital's DECAY PRODUCT (family J (a)) means two catalog rows are one building at different dates — `reclassedTo: ALMSHOUSE` on the hospital.

**(h) Continental vs English.** England: the endowed almshouse in three partis (cloistered units, stair-module lodgings with a great hall, hall-with-cubicles), all church-anchored, all still running; Germany: the Fuggerei — a walled social-housing town with rent-in-prayer, families not almsmen, gates at 22:00; Italy: the civic foundling hospital with a loggia and a wheel, run by a guild (the Arte della Seta); the Low Countries: the Rasphuis — the convent re-used as a prison-workhouse (1596), the model Bristol followed a century later; the Islamic waqf-endowed imaret (soup kitchen) and the Byzantine gerokomeion (old-age home) are the setting-agnostic cousins (PLAUSIBLE, not fetched) — the catalog's absent "soup kitchen" row (§0.1b) is the imaret and the almonry dole.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None.

## §13 · FAMILY L — water supply: Water source (thorp L82 REQ "Well or spring. 2-4 gallons/person/day minimum."; hamlet L365 REQ "Well or spring access.") · Multiple water sources (village L437 REQ "Wells and springs throughout village."; town L1529 REQ "Wells, fountains, or piped water.") · Aqueduct or water system (city L2239 REQ "Engineered water supply. Conduits, cisterns, fountains.") · Advanced water infrastructure (metropolis L2461 "Multiple aqueducts, cisterns, and distributed fountains…")

*Provenance: sub-lane LM (killed 06:12:47Z after 33 tool calls — the thinnest lane; no report). Evidence: the Current Archaeology feature "Underground Exeter: water for the city" (LOCAL, `RINST4-LM-exeter-thepast.txt`, 26 KB, curl — WebFetch 403'd), the Subterranea Britannica Exeter visit (LOCAL, `RINST4-LM-subbrit-exeter.txt`), the Kent Archaeology Convent Well report, the Canterbury Historical and Archaeological Society water-tower page, MoEML Great Conduit, archaeology-travel Nîmes, insidethelagoon Venice cisterns, and six search digests (Great Conduit bore; wells; Canterbury plan; Venice pozzi; Nîmes/Pompeii castellum; the wells-contaminated / piped-water-rare negation; Frontinus). The Springer Venice-cistern article redirected to a login; romanaqueducts.info did not resolve; Historic England 1003851 (Exeter passages) is Cloudflare-gated (`RINST4-LM-exeter-HE-1003851.html` = 60 bytes).*

**(a) Analogue.** Water is the tranche's one family whose catalog rows are ALL correct in kind and wrong only in the size of the step between them. (1) **The well** (thorp/hamlet/village): the Convent Well at Woodnesborough (the Sandwich Whitefriars' conduit head, land given 1306): a shaft "0.84m square (2ft 9in.)" and "over 1.60m (5ft 3in.)" deep, lined with "five courses of dressed Caen stone blocks bonded with grey clay", on flint cobbles and four ragstone slabs; an outlet "roughly-shaped circular hole about 0.10m in diameter" halfway up the shaft with its invert at 9.29 m OD and a lime-scale ridge marking the medieval water level at 9.33 m OD; a WELL-HOUSE over it "1.11m across (3ft 8in.)" internally, walls "between 0.38 and 0.50m", standing 1.35 m with vault springers at 1.13 m and a "0.50m wide" entrance; the conduit to the friary "c.3km long" with "around 7m" of fall, "approximately 1 in 300", as "open ditch" in the country and lead pipes in the town; maintained 1483–1534 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Kent Archaeology, Arch. Cant. 137: Scouring the Conduit Head at Woodnesborough](https://www.kentarchaeology.org.uk/journal/137/scouring-conduit-head-woodnesborough-investigations-convent-well-near-sandwich), fetched 2026-08-23) — a COMPLETE measured spring-house, the smallest building in the corpus (1.1 m across, a vault, a door, a pipe). Sheffield Castle's well: "around 12.5 metres deep … lined with stones at the top and cuts through solid bedrock", still holding water (CONFIRMED-digest, Wessex Archaeology 2026). The 1189 London Assize treats "any kinds of pits made for receiving water, whether clean or foul" under the same neighbour-distance procedure as cesspits (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, family M) — the well is a PIT in law. (2) **The conduit** (town/city): Canterbury's waterworks of the 1160s under Prior Wibert, drawn on the c. 1165 plan in the Eadwine Psalter (Trinity College Cambridge MS R.17.1): water "from a source over 1 km away" at a conduit house, "through settling tanks", stored "in a large tank in the water tower" north of the cathedral "close to the dormitory passage", where the laver served the monks' hand-washing; the water ran in order of use "for drinking and cooking, then for washing, and last for the removal of sewage"; five springs fed it (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Canterbury Historical and Archaeological Society: Water Tower (Cathedral)](https://www.canterbury-archaeology.org.uk/water-tower-cathedral), fetched 2026-08-23; CONFIRMED-digest for the psalter/Trinity/five springs). London's Great Conduit: permission to tap the Tyburn springs 1236/7, construction from 1245 "fifty years in the making", the route by Charing Cross, the Strand and Fleet Street to Cheapside; the pipe "a wood and lead water pipe with an internal diameter of 90 mm, which lay, encased in clay, at the bottom of a deep trench"; MoLAS exposed the 13th-century CONDUIT HOUSE's stone foundations at No. 1 Poultry in 1994 (CONFIRMED-digest; SUB-LANE CONFIRMED for 1245/"fifty years"/the route and Stow as the source, [MoEML: Great Conduit (Cheapside)](https://mapoflondon.uvic.ca/GREA1.htm), fetched 2026-08-23 — "no specific details about the conduit house dimensions"). **Exeter** is the corpus's best-documented medieval town supply (LOCAL, *Current Archaeology*, 18 Nov 2014, reporting Mark Stoyle's book): "Exeter had no Pont du Gard. Instead, in common with many other Medieval cities, Exeter's first external water-supply was brought into the city using lead pipes buried in pipe trenches" with "puddled yellow clay in the base", from the St Sidwell spring line north-east of the walls, probably "the last third of the 12th century", routed round the walls through the South Gate over the canons' own land "to avoid having to dig up public roads"; stored "in a stone-lined tank" in the close, with branch pipes to St Nicholas Priory and "the town well … the chief public source of domestic water" for which the city paid the cathedral; the Black Friars' supply of 1248–59 crossed the town ditch "on a timber pipe bridge" and went "beneath the city wall" through "a chamber, just big enough for a person to pass through" (found 1951) — "the forerunner of all the other underground passages"; the cathedral's new supply of 1346–49 breached the wall by the East Gate (£12 and "750 loads of stone" to mend it), crossed St John's hospital garden in "a subterranean stone passage" ("1,850 loads of stone … at a cost of £4, and the cathedral masons were paid £6") entered by "a manhole … with footholes sunk into the stone-lined shaft"; the spring head: "a large and deep pit over the water source. A hefty circular stone was placed over the spring, with a central hole, and a vertical lead pipe was inserted into the hole", the pit lined with puddled clay so the water rose "up through the vertical standpipe, where it gushed into a cistern, set within the stone conduit-house"; the main: "another lead pipe descended slowly into Exeter at a gradient that kept the water running at sufficient pressure to be able to cope with the occasional uphill pipe run", ending in "a large storage cistern from which it could be drawn off via a brass tap", with "removable plugs" along the line for air and sediment; "by the late 14th century, Exeter thus had four major gravity-flow systems, but all primarily designed to supply ecclesiastical establishments" — and Gloucester, Southampton, Bristol, Coventry and Ipswich likewise "entered into agreements with local monastic houses in order to secure a share in their supplies"; the civic **New Conduit** of 1420–24 (two ex-mayors' bequests), "lead pipes sometimes supported on timber bridges, sometimes raised on earthen banks, revetted with stakes or stone walls, sometimes buried in a pipe-trench, and sometimes carried along the base of a stone-lined tunnel", ending at "a 'recever', or reservoir, probably quite a substantial stone structure with a lead-covered roof, opposite St Stephen's church" with public "brass taps"; a city "plommer" from 1425; remodelled after 1441 (£150, of which £84 donated); at the Dissolution the monastic systems passed to private hands that "robbed out and sold substantial portions of lead piping", and in 1549 rebels "robbed the conduits of lead pipes to serve for shot"; Hooker's 1550s Exeter "full of springs … furnished with welles and tyepittes"; the piped water "purer and lighter" and "more fit for the dressing of meates" (cooking more than drinking); leet complaints that a conduit "hathe ben dry this quarter" and that "timber trapdoors over the access shafts had rotted"; in 1642 the tunnels were "stopped upp" against siege; by 1698 river water ran "directly to individual houses by means of timber pipes"; the railway cut the springs off in 1857. The passages: "between 4 and 6 metres underground stretching 425 metres" (CONFIRMED-digest); Castle passage "about 5' 2" in height throughout most of its length and … about the width of one person", of Silverton lava blocks; a Civil War guard's ledge; a conduit too small to crawl (LOCAL, Subterranea Britannica); the 1490s Pipe Chamber at the East Gate doubled as an artillery position "to provide flanking fire along the city ditch" (LOCAL, *CA*). (3) **The cistern** (Venice; the dry site): the vere da pozzo "were not true wells … underground cisterns designed to collect and filter precious rainwater", dug "5–6 meters below ground and sea level", lined with "a layer of impermeable clay half a metre thick", filled with "layers of clean, wet, river sand and gravel that formed a graduated filter", with a central "canna" of "porous pozzali bricks set upon a slab of Istrian stone"; "the Maggior Consiglio ordered 50 new public cisterns" in 1322 and "another 30" in 1424; "thousands of cisterns eventually existed"; in use "from the 5th century to about 1884" (CONFIRMED-digest; SUB-LANE CONFIRMED for the 0.5 m clay, the 1322/1424 orders and the canna, [Inside the Lagoon: Venice's wells](https://insidethelagoon.com/living-style/venice-wells-rainwater-cisterns/), fetched 2026-08-23). (4) **The aqueduct and the castellum** (the Roman ceiling): Nîmes — "around 50 km" of aqueduct with "only about 17 meters" of fall ("35 cm per kilometre"), "an estimated 40,000 cubic metres" a day, c. AD 50, the Pont du Gard "over 50 m high, and 275 m long"; the castellum aquae: "5.90 metres" in diameter, "about 1.40 metres" deep, "ten openings, each about 40 centimetres in diameter" for the lead mains, "three holes on the floor of the tank" to drain (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Archaeology Travel: Nîmes Aqueduct](https://archaeology-travel.com/france/nimes-aqueduct/), fetched 2026-08-23; the digest's 5.5 m × 1.3 m with a "1.5 m wide" stone walkway is the nimesnotes figure — the two differ by 0.4 m, both carried); Pompeii's castellum "one inlet from the aqueduct and three outlets" and the town's "system of towers … to permit the gradual reduction of pressure" (CONFIRMED-digest); Rome — Frontinus's 591 lacus delivering "60 cubic meters of water per day" each, which with Bruun's 900 persons per lacus "computes to a per capita water use of 67 liters per day", against the naive 992,000 m³/day ÷ one million = 1,000 L (CONFIRMED-digest, waterhistory.org) — the catalog's "2–4 gallons/person/day minimum" (9–18 L) is a survival floor an order of magnitude under Rome's 67 L and within the range of a well-and-bucket settlement (PLAUSIBLE; no fetched source gives a medieval per-capita figure). **Structural imperatives**: GRAVITY (1:300 at Sandwich; 35 cm/km at Nîmes; Exeter's gradient with "the occasional uphill pipe run" — a siphon), FROST/THEFT (buried pipes; lead robbed for shot), ACCESS (the passage "big enough for a person", the manhole with footholes, the rotten trapdoor), SEDIMENT (settling tanks; plugs), LAND (the route kept to the canons' land; compensation to the hospital), WALL (the breach at the East Gate; the pipe under the wall; the pipe chamber that became a gun position). **Siting**: the spring line outside the walls (St Sidwell; Tyburn; Canterbury's five springs "over 1 km"); the conduit house at the source; the receiver/cistern at the market or the close; the well in the court or the street. **Live/work**: the "plommer" (1425), the conduit-keeper (PLAUSIBLE — the London conduit "keeper" was sought by LM and not found), the Venetian acquaroli (digest — not fetched). **Prosperity/wear**: floor (thorp) = a spring, or a dug well with a timber curb (NO_BUILDING: a fixture on ground); rung 1 (hamlet/village) = a stone-lined shaft with a windlass and a well-house of 1–2 m (the Convent Well house); rung 2 (town) = a monastic conduit with a town share: conduit house at the spring, lead main in a clay trench, a stone tank with a brass tap, a town well; rung 3 (city) = the civic New Conduit with a lead-roofed receiver, multiple sources, tunnels with manholes, a plumber on the payroll, fountains; ceiling = the aqueduct with a castellum of ten mains and pressure towers (Rome/Nîmes/Pompeii), or the cistern city (Venice's thousands). Decline: lead robbed (1539, 1549), conduits "dry this quarter", trapdoors rotten, tunnels blocked (1642), the spring cut (1857) — and the fallback is ALWAYS the well ("full of springs … welles and tyepittes"): the floor never leaves.

**(b) Measured.** Convent Well: shaft 0.84 m square × > 1.6 m; well-house 1.11 m internal, walls 0.38–0.50 m, 1.35 m standing, springers 1.13 m, door 0.5 m; outlet 0.10 m; conduit 3 km, 7 m fall, 1:300 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). Sheffield well 12.5 m (digest). Great Conduit pipe 90 mm bore, 1245, c. 50 years (digest). Canterbury source > 1 km (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). Exeter: passages 4–6 m deep, 425 m (digest); Castle passage c. 1.57 m high × one person wide (LOCAL, subbrit); 1,850 loads of stone £4; 750 loads for the breach; £12; £150 (1441) with £84 donated (LOCAL, *CA*). Venice: 5–6 m deep, 0.5 m clay, 50 (1322) + 30 (1424) public cisterns (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED / digest). Nîmes: 50 km, 17 m, 35 cm/km, 40,000 m³/day; castellum 5.9 (or 5.5) m × 1.4 (1.3) m, ten outlets 0.40 m, three drains, walkway 1.5 m; Pont du Gard 50 × 275 m (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED / digest). Rome: 591 lacus × 60 m³/day; 67 L/person (Bruun) (digest). NOT FOUND: the Great Conduit house's size (MoEML has none; the MoLAS report not fetched); the Exeter receiver's size; any conduit-house plan beyond the Convent Well; a Venetian cistern's capacity; the Pompeii water-tower height; the castellum's lead-pipe bores beyond "40 cm" openings.

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) The negation "piped conduit water was rare before 1600" is CONTESTED by Exeter's OWN evidence: FOUR gravity systems by the late 14th century and a civic one by 1424, and "similar arrangements … in several other provincial cities" — the right statement is that piped water was MONASTIC first and civic by share, and the civic-owned system is a 15th-century town's pride, not a rarity; the counter-counter is that the citizens "depended on their part-share" and the town well remained "the chief public source of domestic water" — the catalog's town row "Wells, fountains, OR piped water" is right to put wells first. (ii) "Wells contaminated by cesspits" is CONFIRMED in general (manor court rolls; the 1189 distance rule — family M) but the lane found no measured English case — PLAUSIBLE as a mechanism, not a figure. (iii) The Roman aqueduct was ABANDONED in the post-Roman west (Paris; Patras — digest) and the Dorchester aqueduct was "twelve miles long" (digest): the city row "Aqueduct or water system" must be register-gated — Roman/Islamic/Italian-cistern registers have the aqueduct; the English city has lead mains and tunnels instead ("Exeter had no Pont du Gard"). (iv) The Nîmes castellum's diameter is given as 5.5 m and 5.90 m by two pages — carry both; the 1.5-m walkway is single-sourced. (v) Frontinus's per-capita figure spans 67–1,000 L by method — the catalog's 2–4 gallons is a floor, not an average; a number audit finds 9–18 L/day consistent with a carried-bucket economy (PLAUSIBLE). (vi) The water supply is the one family where the REQ rows are right at every tier: the floor (a spring or well) IS existence (law 3) — but it is NO_BUILDING at the floor and a 1-m vault at the first rung.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** Water's circulation is NON-HUMAN and the CIRC addendum's `VERTICAL` class carries it: `VERTICAL{SHAFT}` = the well (0.84 m square; 12.5 m deep; a head at grade — the well-house), `VERTICAL{STANDPIPE}` = the spring head's vertical lead pipe in a clay-sealed pit, `VERTICAL{MANHOLE}` = the access shaft "with footholes sunk into the stone-lined shaft" and a timber trapdoor (a `HATCH` joint between the street and the undercity). The human circulation is the **conduit PASSAGE**: a stone-vaulted `CORRIDOR{UNDERGROUND}` "big enough for a person to pass through" — Exeter's c. 1.57 m high × one person (c. 0.6–0.8 m — PLAUSIBLE) — licensed by PROGRAM (pipe maintenance) from 1248–59, running 4–6 m down for 425 m, with a `LOBBY{PIPE_CHAMBER}` at the wall that doubled as a gun position (a `dualUse` cell again), a guard's ledge, and dead-end conduits "which would never have been navigable": the tranche's one below-grade LINEAR circulation cell, and the undercity train's (law 7) — the DW grammar owns only its surface joints (the manhole, the conduit house door, the receiver's tap face). The conduit HOUSE is a `LOBBY`-sized vault (1.1 m) with a 0.5-m door; the receiver is a `STORE{CISTERN}` with a public tap face on the street (an exterior fixture); the castellum is a `LOBBY{BASIN}` with a 1.5-m `GALLERY{WALKWAY}` ring round a 5.9-m basin; the water tower (Canterbury) is a `VERTICAL{TANK}` building beside the dormitory passage. Widths: passage c. 0.7 m × 1.57 m (LOCAL/PLAUSIBLE); walkway 1.5 m; well-house door 0.5 m. Buckets: `CONDUIT_PASSAGE` 0.6–1.0 m × 1.5–1.8 m (NARROW-below-NARROW — a new MICRO bucket shared with the opera box); `WELL_HOUSE` 1–2 m internal.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** Water IS the stored good: `STORE{CISTERN}` in three forms — the stone-lined tank in the close (Exeter), the lead-roofed receiver with brass taps (1424), the water tower's "large tank" (Canterbury) — lightReq NONE, `REQUIRED_NEAR(public street | kitchen | laver)`; `STORE{SETTLING_TANK}` upstream of the cistern (`REQUIRED_BEFORE` in the flow order — a SEQUENCE attribute like the bath's thermal order); the Venetian cistern as a `CELLAR`-class sand-filled vault under a campo with the canna as its shaft; the castellum's basin with three floor drains (`joint: SEWER`); the `STORE{LEAD}` (the plumber's stock — lead was the theft target). Adjacency: water tower beside the dormitory passage and the laver (Canterbury — the monks' hand-washing en route to services), kitchen first in the use order, sewage last (a FLOW-ORDER law: drinking → washing → sewage, the first time the corpus states a cascade of uses through cells). Prohibition: the 1189 distance rule between a water pit and a neighbour (family M) — `REQUIRED_FAR(cesspit)` PLAUSIBLE as the inverse of the Assize.

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: NO_BUILDING{SPRING | DUG_WELL}` (thorp L82 REQ: a fixture on ground — curb, windlass, bucket; the spring as a map feature), `parti: WELL_HOUSE` (hamlet/village L365/L437: a 1–2 m stone vault over a stone-lined shaft; or N such across the village — the catalog's "throughout village"), `parti: CONDUIT_SYSTEM{conduit_house(spring) → main(lead, 90 mm, clay trench, 1:300) → cistern/tank(brass tap) + town_well}` (town L1529: monastic-owned with a civic share, era ≥ 1160s (Canterbury) / 1170s (Exeter) / 1245 (London)), `parti: CIVIC_CONDUIT{receiver(lead roof, taps) + tunnels(passage + manholes) + plumber}` (city L2239: era ≥ 1420 English; the aqueduct-and-castellum form in the Roman/Islamic register; the cistern city in the lagoon register), `parti: AQUEDUCT_CASTELLUM{50 km; castellum 5.9 m; ten mains; pressure towers; fountains (lacus) every 900 persons}` (metropolis L2461 ceiling — Roman register). `functions[]`: floor = `draw_point`; rungs = + `well_house` → + `spring_house + pipe` → + `tank with tap` → + `settling tank` → + `passage with manholes` → + `public receiver` → + `multiple sources` → + `fountains`; ceiling = the castellum network. `fixtures[]`: `CURB+WINDLASS`, `STANDPIPE{lead, vertical}`, `CIRCULAR_STONE{central hole}`, `PUDDLED_CLAY`, `LEAD_MAIN{90 mm}`, `PIPE_BRIDGE{timber}`, `BRASS_TAP`, `PLUG{air|sediment}`, `TRAPDOOR{timber — rots}`, `FOOTHOLES`, `LAVER`, `CANNA{porous brick}`, `VERA_DA_POZZO` (the carved well-head), `LACUS` (street fountain). Structural buckets: `gravityFall{1:300 … 35 cm/km}`, `buried`, `tunnelled` (law-7 joint), `wallBreach` (a dated event on the town wall — a fabric seam), `leadTheftRisk` (a wear/news state), `frost`. Licensing: every tier (REQ rows are right); the conduit era-gated ≥ 1160s and OWNER-gated (monastic → civic share → civic-owned 1420s → private pipes 1698); culture: the lead-main-and-tunnel town (England), the aqueduct city (Rome, the Islamic qanat/aqueduct — PLAUSIBLE), the cistern city (Venice; the Mediterranean dry site). VERDICT: thorp/hamlet Water source **NO_BUILDING** (a fixture on ground) rising to **BUILDING{WELL_HOUSE}** at 1–2 m; village/town Multiple water sources **NO_BUILDING ×N + CONDUIT_SYSTEM** (the conduit house and tank are buildings; the main is a linear fixture; the passage is the undercity's); city Aqueduct or water system **BUILDING{CIVIC_CONDUIT}** (English) / **AQUEDUCT_CASTELLUM** (Roman) / **CISTERN_FIELD** (Venice) by register; metropolis **BUILDING ×N** (multiple systems — Exeter had four by 1400 at TOWN scale, so the catalog's "multiple aqueducts" threshold is low). HOME: NO TYPED HOME for any water cell (well house, conduit house, cistern, receiver, passage, castellum); the engine's `waterSupply` exclusive group and `INDUSTRY_WATER_NEEDS` (R-INST-2 §Σ gap 15) are the consumers; FACET_INFERENCE `generic` (§Σ).

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) Water is a LINEAR, GRAVITY-ORDERED system crossing lots, walls and jurisdictions — it is the fabric/undercity program's, and the DW grammar owns only its BUILDINGS (well house, conduit house, receiver, water tower, castellum) and their JOINTS (manhole, tap face, the pipe under the wall) — the seam to state at DW-0 is that `waterSupply` is a settlement GRAPH with building-nodes, exactly as law 7 says of basements. (2) A FLOW-ORDER of uses through cells (drinking → washing → sewage) is a new adjacency law: `REQUIRED_BEFORE/AFTER` on a resource, not a door. (3) Ownership transitions (monastic → shared → civic → private) are dated `runBy` events on infrastructure — law 5 at settlement scale, with the Dissolution as the one event that RE-OWNS every conduit at once. (4) Lead theft (1539, 1549) and "dry this quarter" are NEWS-visible supply states (R-INST-2's supply-state wear pilot, gap 16, applied to a public utility). (5) The well-house at 1.1 m internal is below every cell bucket — the MICRO bucket (opera box, conduit passage, well house) is now three-sourced. (6) The catalog's tier ladder should read: well (every tier) → monastic conduit with a town share (town) → civic receiver (city ≥ 1420) → aqueduct/cistern by register; "multiple aqueducts" at metropolis is Roman-only.

**(h) Continental vs English.** England: lead mains in clay trenches from a spring line, monastic first, civic by the 1420s, tunnels where walls and gardens must be crossed, the well as the permanent floor; Rome and Gaul: the masonry aqueduct at 35 cm/km, the castellum of ten mains, the lacus every 900 people, pressure towers (Pompeii) — abandoned after the fifth century in the west; Venice: the sand-filtered rainwater cistern under every campo (5–6 m deep, half a metre of clay), thousands of them, ordered by the state (1322, 1424), with the carved well-head as the civic fixture; the Islamic qanat and the Ottoman su terazisi (water-balance tower) are the setting-agnostic ceiling (PLAUSIBLE, not fetched). The catalog's "fountains" belong to the Roman/Italian registers; the English town had a conduit with taps, not a fountain, until the Renaissance.

**(i) Fantasy note.** A "create water" service removes the gravity and the spring but not the cistern and the tap — flag for R-INST-5 that the storage and draw-point cells survive any magical source.
